Open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theories: construction
Amanda Hirschi, Kai Hugtenburg

Abstract
. Open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theories are chain-level field theories based on moduli spaces of stable curves with boundary. We associate to a relatively spin embedded Lagrangian L ⊂ ( X , ω ) L\subset(X,\omega) such an open-closed DMFT. It extends the Fukaya A ∞ A_{\infty} algebra to curves of arbitrarily high genus and with arbitrarily many boundary components and is unique up to homotopy. This is the first step in proving Kontsevich’s conjecture that the Fukaya category determines the Gromov–Witten invariants of X X , following a strategy delineated by Costello.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Context

Cohomological field theories were defined by Kontsevich–Manin,
[
43
]
, in order to describe the algebraic structure of Gromov–Witten invariants and are inspired by the seminal work of Witten,
[
58
]
. In this paper we define a generalisation of this notion, assigning chain-level operations to chains on moduli spaces of stable curves with boundary. We call this algebraic structure an open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory to indicate that the underlying properad differs from the properad previously used to define open-closed field theories.
Open-closed field theories were defined by Moore and Segal
[
47
,
49
]
and have been widely applied in as diverse fields as string topology, mathematical physics, algebraic geometry and symplectic topology.

As with previous constructions of algebraic structures in symplectic topology, we use moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic curves. They were introduced to symplectic topology by Gromov,
[
33
]
, and have become the main tool to prove rigidity results. The first invariants based on counts of pseudo-holomorphic curves are the aforementioned Gromov–Witten invariants as well as Lagrangian Floer theory, the latter based on genus zero curves satisfying Lagrangian boundary conditions,
[
24
]
.
In this case, the geometry of moduli spaces of holomorphic discs gives rise to an
A_{\infty}
category, the Fukaya category defined in
[
25
,
50
]
, respectively an
A_{\infty}
algebra when considering a single Lagrangian. Higher-genus Lagrangian Floer theory has been only studied in special cases such as in
[
46
,
23
]
, partially because a good algebraic framework was lacking.

In
[
19
]
, Costello defines the notion of an open-closed topological conformal field theory (TCFT), and shows that any smooth proper Calabi–Yau
A_{\infty}
category
\mathscr{A}
gives rise to such a structure, yielding an action of the modular properad on the Hochschild homology of
\mathscr{A}
. This is also discussed in
[
44
]
and
[
56
,
57
]
. Several papers, such as
[
20
]
or
[
15
,
14
,
3
]
show how to extract invariants from a TCFT, called
categorical enumerative invariants
, given additionally a splitting of the associated Hodge filtration. There is also recent work on defining
open(-closed) categorical enumerative invariants
from an open-closed TCFT, see
[
54
,
55
]
.
Crucially, Costello connects open-closed TCFTs to one of the most important conjectures in symplectic geometry, the Homological Mirror Symmetry conjecture of Kontsevich,
[
45
]
, by positing them as a key tool for obtaining the Gromov–Witten theory of a symplectic manifold from its Fukaya category.
In order to obtain a cohomological field theory from a closed TCFT, one needs to trivialise the
S^{1}
action coming from rotating the interior boundary punctures, as discussed by
[
21
]
for properads and
[
22
,
48
]
in the operadic case.

1.2.
Main results

This paper modifies Costello’s definition by replacing the modular properad of smooth framed surfaces by modular properad of moduli spaces of stable curves with boundary, carrying marked points. As in
[
19
]
, we do not phrase our algebraic structures in terms of an algebra over this modular properad but as a functor from a symmetric monoidal dg category
C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})
whose objects are pairs of natural numbers and morphisms are chains on said moduli spaces of curves with appropriate numbers of incoming/outgoing interior and boundary marked points. We allow for disconnected curves, whence disjoint union gives rise to a symmetric monoidal structure on
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
. See §
2.1
for the precise definition and §
1.4
for a non-technical discussion. We can now give the main definition of this paper.

Definition 1.1
.

An
open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory
(DMFT) is a symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}\colon C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})\to\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
to the category of chain complexes.

A symmetric monoidal structure for an
A_{\infty}
functor consists of certain natural transformations, defined in §
2.2
, which are inspired by
[
4
]
. It is more involved than the definition of symmetric monoidal dg functors, and our construction relies on the fact that
C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})
is a dg category.

A first important property of open-closed DMFTs is that they define an
A_{\infty}
structure on the chain complex
\mathscr{F}(0,1)
, cf. Lemma
2.40
. This mirrors a similar statement in
[
19
]
for open-closed TCFTS. In contrast to open-closed TCFTs, an open-closed DMFT also directly yields a chain-level lift of a cohomological field theory restricting to moduli spaces of closed Riemann surfaces, cf. §
1.4
.

To be more precise, just as the Fukaya algebra of a Lagrangian is usually a curved
A_{\infty}
algebra, the algebraic structure we naturally obtain from moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic curves forces us to consider the more general notion of a
curved
open-closed DMFT, which is defined on the category
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
but does not satisfy the
A_{\infty}
functor relations. We show that that curved open-closed DMFTs can still be understood as
A_{\infty}
functors, albeit from an extension
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
of the dg category
C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})
; see Definition
2.15
in §
2.1
.

The main result of this paper is the construction of a curved open-closed DMFT based on moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic stable maps with boundary on the Lagrangian. Since we only require the Lagrangian to be relatively spin, embedded and weakly unobstructed, we use virtual perturbations throughout. These geometric foundations, in particular coherent systems of global Kuranishi charts, are built in
[
35
]
.

Theorem A

(Theorem
3.1
)
.

Suppose
(X,\omega)
is a closed symplectic manifold and
L\subset X
an embedded Lagrangian equipped with a relative spin structure.

a)

(X,L)
admits a semi-strict operadic curved open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory
\mathscr{F}_{L}
whose associated
A_{\infty}
algebra
\mathscr{A}_{L}
is the Fukaya
A_{\infty}
algebra of
L
.

b)

If
L
is equipped with a weak bounding cochain, then it admits a semi-strict operadic
uncurved
open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory.

In either case, the associated closed DMFT yields the Gromov–Witten theory of
X
.

Open-closed DMFTs allow for operations coming from curves with any number of outputs and inputs in return for relaxing the assumption that
\mathscr{F}
is a dg functor. By relying on a specific chain model, we can achieve strictness of
\mathscr{F}_{L}
for certain subcategories, as indicated by the properties semi-strict and operadic, which are defined in §
2.3
.
A key feature of the property of being operadic is that the open-closed DMFT is compatible with forgetting incoming marked points. In particular,
\mathscr{A}_{L}
is a cyclic
A_{\infty}
algebra.
Restricting the open-closed DMFT to stable spheres, we have the following other immediate consequence.

Corollary 1.2
.

Any compact symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
admits a strict action of the operad of stable genus-
0
curves on
\Omega^{*}(X;\Lambda)
lifting the quantum cup product.

One should compare this with
[
1
]
, where a strict chain-level action of the operad of framed (stable) genus-
0
curves on Hamiltonian Floer theory is constructed.

While we highlight the restrictions to discs and closed curves, the (curved) open-closed DMFT of Theorem
A
contains far more information. It can be considered a universal curve-counting theory in the sense that it is agnostic about the complexity of the curve. In particular, important structures such as (chain-level lifts of) the open-closed map

(1.1)

HH_{*}(\mathscr{A}_{L})\to\text{QH}^{*}(X,\omega)

and the closed-open map

(1.2)

\text{QH}^{*}(X,\omega)\to HH^{*}(\mathscr{A}_{L})

are part of the data of an open-closed DMFT.

The theory of deformations of
A_{\infty}
structures carries over to open-closed DMFTs. Recall from
[
25
]
that a
weak bounding cochain
of a unital curved
A_{\infty}
algebra is a Maurer–Cartan element
\mathfrak{b}
so that the curvature of the deformed
A_{\infty}
algebra is a multiple of the unit. The second assertion of Theorem
A
follows from the first one by establishing the following result.

Proposition 1.3

(Proposition
2.51
)
.

If
\mathscr{F}
is a suitable curved open-closed DMFT and
\mathfrak{b}
a weak bounding cochain of the associated curved
A_{\infty}
algebra, then the deformed curved DMFT
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}
induces an (uncurved) open-closed DMFT.

While it may seem surprising that weak bounding cochains are sufficient to eliminate curvature in this more general context, the underlying reason is that discs are the only curves with boundary that are unstable if they carry only one or two boundary marked points.

The construction of the curved open-closed DMFT of Theorem
A
(
a
) requires numerous choices, in particular a choice of
\omega
-tame almost complex structure
J
. We establish independence from these choices up to homotopy by geometrically constructing the required homotopies.

Theorem B

(Theorem
3.43
)
.

The (curved) open-closed DMFTs of Theorem
A
are independendent of all auxiliary choices (and gauge equivalence of bounding cochains) up to homotopy of DMFTs.

The basis of the operations of the open-closed DMFT of Theorem
A
as well as the symmetric monoidal structure and the homotopies of Theorem
B
are the correspondences of §
3.2
.
We construct them using moduli spaces of stable maps with Lagrangian boundary. Since we have to incorporate chains coming from moduli space of stable curves, these correspondences are singular in general – even after using regularisation techniques. Inspired by
[
51
]
, we use analytic chains. This means we can apply the real analytic resolution of singularities algorithm of
[
12
]
, extended to real analytic orbifolds with corners in §
A
.

Remark 1.4
.

We expect the open-closed DMFT of Theorem
A
to be defined in any category in which pull-push operations based on correspondences make sense. In the same vein, while we define an
A_{\infty}
functor, our construction should also yield an
\infty
-functor to suitable
\infty
-categories without further geometric work.

1.3.
Applications

Mirror symmetry states that for every Calabi-Yau variety
X
there should be a mirror Calabi-Yau variety
Y
such that, roughly speaking, the symplectic invariants of
X
are ‘isomorphic’ to complex or algebraic invariants of
Y
. The first example of this was given for the quintic threefold
X
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{4}
by
[
16
]
, where a correspondence between the Gromov–Witten invariants of
X
and the period integrals of the mirror quintic
Y
was proven. This is known as
enumerative mirror symmetry
. Kontsevich conjectured this should be the consequence of a more fundamental equivalence, called
homological mirror symmetry
.

Conjecture 1

(
[
45
]
)
.

For mirror varieties
X
and
Y
, there exists an equivalence

\mathcal{F}uk(X)\cong D^{b}Coh(Y)

between the Fukaya category of
X
and the derived category of coherent sheaves on
Y
.
Moreover, this homological mirror symmetry implies enumerative mirror symmetry.

In particular, on the symplectic side, this would imply

Conjecture 2

(
[
45
]
)
.

The Fukaya category determines the Gromov-Witten invariants of the symplectic manifold.

In
[
29
,
30
]
this was proven in genus
0
for Calabi–Yau varieties, under suitable assumptions, using
Hodge-theoretic mirror symmetry
. One of the major technical ingredients is their proof that an
S^{1}
-equivariant enhancement of the open-closed map intertwines the Getzler–Gauss–Manin connection with the quantum connection. This result was also obtained in
[
39
]
under very restrictive regularity assumptions. With similar methods it was shown in
[
38
]
that one can also extract enumerative invariants of Lagrangians, so called
genus
0
open Gromov–Witten invariants
, from the Fukaya category under suitable regularity assumptions. The present paper removes these regularity requirements from these papers.

In order to prove Conjecture
2
in arbitrary genus, Costello
[
19
]
associates categorical enumerative invariants to a (weakly Calabi–Yau)
A_{\infty}
category, which should recover the usual Gromov–Witten invariants in the case of the Fukaya category. These have been shown to be quite computable,
[
14
]
, but it remains to be shown that they recover ‘geometric’ enumerative invariants. As a first step in defining categorical enumerative invariants, Costello proved that any Calabi–Yau
A_{\infty}
category determines an open-closed TCFT.

When the Fukaya category is generated by a single Lagrangian, we conjecture that the open-closed TCFT associated to the open-closed DMFT of Theorem
A
is equivalent to the universal TCFT associated to the Fukaya category by Costello. This conjecture, including comparing open-closed DMFTs and TCFTs, and the construction of a geometric open-closed DMFT for the whole Fukaya category will be addressed in future work.

Remark 1.5
.

It is unknown whether the closed-open map of an arbitrary closed symplectic manifold
X
is necessarily an isomorphism; in fact, it is unknown whether the Fukaya category is non-trivial. In situations where homological mirror symmetry has been established, one can use the generation criterion of
[
2
,
31
]
to show that the closed-open map is an isomorphism.

Remark 1.6
.

In order to obtain Gromov–Witten invariants from the Fukaya category, one needs to equip the category with the correct trivialisation of the
S^{1}
-action/splitting of the non-commutative Hodge-de-Rham spectral sequence. When it is an isomorphism, the closed-open map provides this trivialisation. In the Calabi–Yau case,
[
29
]
use intrinsic properties of the Fukaya category to determine the correct splitting. For a general symplectic manifold, one might need additional data.

1.4.
Field theories and a construction in symplectic topology

In this section we describe open-closed DMFTs in more detail and how they relate to more classical algebraic structures, and give an overview of the construction.

1.4.1.
Cohomological and Deligne–Mumford field theories

Before the first construction of Gromov–Witten invariants was given, Kontsevich and Manin described the algebraic structure they should yield,
[
43
]
, inspired by the seminal work of Witten,
[
58
]
. Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
denote the Deligne–Mumford compactification of the moduli space of (closed) Riemann surfaces of genus
g
with
n
marked points. A
cohomological field theory
consists of a graded vector space
V
equipped with a graded symmetric pairing
\eta
on
V
, a distinguished element
e\in V^{0}
, called the
unit
, and a family of linear maps

\Omega_{g,n}\colon V^{\otimes n}\rightarrow H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n},\mathbb{Q}),

which satisfies a list of recursion relations, the CohFT axioms, cf.
[
43
]
. Crucially, this can be equivalently phrased as the data of a strong symmetric monoidal functor. To see this, let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
is a topological category whose set of objects are the natural numbers
\mathbb{N}_{0}
including
0
and whose morphism spaces are moduli spaces of Riemann surfaces with
k^{-}
incoming and
k^{+}
outgoing marked points.
We allow for moduli spaces in the unstable range, in which case we take the moduli space to be a point. These additional ‘unstable’ morphisms give rise to the unit
e
(the sphere with one outgoing marked point) and the pairing
\eta
(the sphere with two incoming marked points), and to identity morphisms. Composition is given by clutching Riemann surfaces at incoming and outgoing marked points, with the composition with unstable morphisms described in Definition
2.1
. For example, composition with the the unit forgets an incoming marked point.
H_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c})
denotes the category obtained from
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
by taking homology of each morphism space.

Lemma 1.7
.

CohFTs are in bijection to symmetric monoidal functors
\mathscr{F}\colon H_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c})\rightarrow\text{Vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Proof.

Given such a functor
\mathscr{F}
, set
V\coloneqq\mathscr{F}(\{1\})
and
\Omega_{g,n}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})(\sigma):=\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
for
[\sigma]\in H_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n})
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in V
. The unit
e
and the pairing are the image of the unique non-zero unstable element in
H_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(0,1))
, respectively in
H_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(2,0))
. The CohFT axioms follow from the functoriality of
\mathscr{F}
. The fact a CohFT determines such a functor by setting
\mathscr{F}(k)=V^{\otimes k}
, and using the pairing
\eta
to turn all outputs into inputs.
∎

In symplectic topology, CohFTs arise from moduli spaces of stable maps and haven been constructed using several different frameworks. Instead of working over
\mathbb{Q}
, one usually has to work over a
Novikov ring

\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
over
\mathbb{Q}
to deal with non-convergence issues in Gromov–Witten theory.
Let us briefly outline the construction of the functor
\mathscr{F}\colon H_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c})\to\text{Vect}_{\Lambda}
in this setting. Let
(X,\omega)
be a closed symplectic manifold and
J
a tame almost complex structure.
By
[
36
]
, the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\beta}_{g,n}(X)
of
J
-holomorphic stable maps representing
\beta\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
admits a global Kuranishi chart. That is, they construct an orbifold
\mathcal{T}_{\beta}
, an orbibundle
\mathcal{E}_{\beta}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{\beta}
, and an orbisection section
s_{\beta}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\beta}
so that
s^{-1}_{\beta}(0)\cong\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\beta}_{g,n}(X)
. The construction directly yields corresponding charts for moduli spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\beta}_{g;k^{-},k^{+}}(X)
of stable maps, where the marked points are partitioned into incoming and outgoing ones. Evaluating at these marked points, we obtain a correspondence

(1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{k^{-}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,J,\beta}_{g;k^{-},k^{+}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{k^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev^{-}}

which is, moreover, equipped with a map
st
to the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k^{-},k^{+}}
of stable curves. Both the evaluation maps and the stabilisation map extend to the thickening
\mathcal{T}
. Then, we define for
\sigma\in H_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(k^{-},k^{+}))
the map

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}\colon H^{*}(X^{k^{-}};\Lambda)

\displaystyle\rightarrow H^{*}(X^{k^{+}};\Lambda),

(1.4)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\beta}ev^{+}_{*}(\text{st}^{*}\text{PD}(\sigma)^{*}\cup(ev^{-})^{*}\alpha\cup s_{\beta}^{*}\tau_{\mathcal{E}_{\beta}})Q^{\beta},

where
\tau_{\beta}
is the Thom class of
\mathcal{E}_{\beta}
. By
[
36
]
, this map is independent of auxiliary choices, in particular of
J
. In
[
37
]
, the CohFT axioms are shown.

Following Costello, we define a chain-level version of cohomological field theories as follows.

Definition 1.8
.

A
strict
Deligne–Mumford Field Theory (DMFT) is an h-split symmetric monoidal dg functor
\mathscr{F}\colon C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c})\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
to the category of chain complexes of
\Lambda
-modules.

The property h-split requires that
\mathscr{F}(k)
is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathscr{F}(1)^{\otimes k}
instead of being isomorphic. This relaxation is required in the setting of Gromov-Witten theory because the Künneth isomorphism is usually not an isomorphism on the chain level.
Using the correspondences (
1.3
), one could try to emulate (
1.4.1
) in any chain model that allows for pull-push operations. We have chosen to work with the chain complex of differential forms
\Omega^{*}(X;\Lambda)
with (completed) Novikov coefficients, so we will describe the issues in this setting.

(I)

The first issue concerns the definition of the chain-level operations since (
1.4.1
) requires Poincaré duality on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
. We solve this problem geometrically by modifying the correspondence (
1.3
) using simplices in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
: given a simplex
\sigma\colon\Delta^{m}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(k^{-},k^{+})
we define the fibre product

\mathcal{K}_{\sigma}:=\mathcal{K}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}}\Delta^{m}.

Since the stabilisation map is not a submersion, this is usually not a well-defined derived orbifold chart. However, assuming
\sigma
to be
analytic
, we can apply the resolution of singularities algorithm of
[
12
]
to
\mathcal{T}_{\sigma}
to obtain a smooth orbifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma}
with a smooth map
p_{\sigma}\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma}\to\mathcal{T}
. Thus, we can define
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}\colon\Omega^{*}(X^{k^{-}};\Lambda)\rightarrow\Omega^{*}(X^{k^{+}};\Lambda)
by

(1.5)

\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}(\alpha)\;=\;(ev^{+}\circ p_{\sigma})_{*}p_{\sigma}^{*}((ev_{\sigma}^{-})^{*}\alpha\wedge s^{*}\tau).

We show in Lemma
3.22
that the choice of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma}
does not affect
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}
.

(II)

The second issue relates to functoriality.
The global Kuranishi charts of
[
36
]
do not depend on the auxiliary choices
up to equivalence
, which is sufficient to achieve strict compatibility/functoriality of the operation (
1.4.1
). This is no longer true on the chain level: both the choice of global Kuranishi charts as well as the choice of Thom form, i.e., the representative of the Thom class, are relevant for the chain-level pull-push operation. Our strategy is to choose the required auxiliary data for moduli spaces of stable maps arbitrarily and then modify the operations (
1.5
) in order to achieve functoriality. This modification is also defined in terms of a correspondence and uses the fact that the global Kuranishi chart restricted to the boundary is cobordant to the fibre product global Kuranishi chart. Doing this rigorously leads to the notion of a system of cubical cobordisms, where we construct for each stable map graph
\Gamma
a global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
for
I^{E(\Gamma)}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X)
, where

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X)\subset\prod\limits_{v\in V(\Gamma)}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g_{v},k_{v}}^{\,J,\beta_{v}}(X)}

is the fibre product determined by the edges of
\Gamma
. In its simplest form, when
\Gamma
has a unique edge,
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
is a cobordism from
\partial_{\Gamma}\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
to the fibre product of global Kuranishi charts. Given these charts, we can replace (
1.5
) by

\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}\coloneqq\sum\limits_{\Gamma}{\mathscr{F}_{\Gamma,\sigma}},

where
\mathscr{F}_{\Gamma,\sigma}
defined similarly to (
1.5
) with
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
instead of
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
.

While the solution described in (
II
) achieves strict functoriality in the stable range, strict functoriality of
\mathscr{F}
with respect to the unstable morphisms would imply that the pairing on
\Omega^{*}(X;\Lambda)
has to be non-degenerate. Since the chain complex is infinite-dimensional, this is unachievable. The upshot is that one has to relax the condition that
\mathscr{F}
is a dg functor.

Definition 1.9
.

A
Deligne–Mumford Field Theory
(DMFT) is an h-split symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}\colon C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c})\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
.

A DMFT is
semi-strict
if the restriction of
\mathscr{F}
to the subcategory of stable chains
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}^{st}
is a dg functor. Refer to Definition
2.27
for the definition of symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
.

Theorem 1.10
.

Associated to any closed symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
is a semi-strict DMFT, which yields the Gromov–Witten CohFT of
X
when passing to homology.

While the construction of the higher homotopies is natural in our set-up, it requires considerable work, including the construction of a special chain model, to achieve semi-strictness.

1.4.2.
Open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theories

As their name suggests, open-closed DMFTs also incorporate operations from stable curves with boundary. To make this precise, let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
be the category with objects pairs
y=(y_{c},y_{o})
of nonnegative integers (or finite sets more generally) and morphism from
y
to
y^{\prime}
given by possibly nodal disconnected curves with incoming/outgoing interior marked points labelled by
y_{c}\sqcup y^{\prime}_{c}
and incoming/outgoing boundary marked points labelled by
y_{o}\sqcup y_{o}^{\prime}
. As before, we include moduli spaces of unstable curves as points. An
open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory
is an h-split symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
functor

\mathscr{F}\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}\coloneqq C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}.

Remark 1.11
.

The key difference between an open-closed DMFT and the open-closed TCFT considered by Costello is that our moduli spaces are compactified and that we consider interior marked points as opposed to interior punctures. Moreover, Costello defines an open-closed TCFT to be a dg functor, whereas we allow for higher homotopies and consider
A_{\infty}
functors.

One can restrict an open-closed DMFT to
C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c})
to obtain a DMFT, and to the subcategory generated by discs with one output to obtain an
A_{\infty}
algebra. They thus serve as a bridge between the closed and the open theory.

In practice (at least in symplectic topology), pseudo-holomorphic stable maps with Lagrangian boundary condition do not yield an open-closed DMFT.
Indeed, while the same issues (
I
) and (
II
) arise, the same solutions work in the case with boundary.
However, in the open setting, moduli spaces have codimension-
1
boundary strata, which deform the de Rham differential on
\Omega^{*}(L;\Lambda)
.
This deformation, called the Floer differential, need not square to zero because the Lagrangian might be
curved
. Concretely, the bubbling off of unstable discs creates additional boundary strata that are not seen by moduli spaces of stable curves and which spoil the
A_{\infty}
functor equations.
To remedy this, we change the category
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
by allowing for
unstable
curves in the definition of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
that ‘have higher energy’ and thus contribute differently than the other unstable curves.

Definition 1.12
.

Let
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
be the category obtained by formally adjoining a morphism
D_{0}
from
(0,0)
to
(0,1)
and a morphism
D_{1}
from
(0,1)
to
(0,1)
to
C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})
and defining the differential on a morphism
\sigma

\partial_{oc}\sigma\coloneqq\partial\sigma+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\pi^{*}\sigma\circ_{*}D_{0}-\sum_{i\in y^{\prime}_{o}}D_{1}\circ_{i}\sigma+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\sum_{i\in y_{o}}\sigma\circ_{i}D_{1},

where the chain
\pi^{*}\sigma
is obtained by adding an additional incoming boundary marked point to the curves in the image of
\sigma
; see
2.13
for a precise construction.

We have thus arrived at the definition that is closest to the geometry we see in symplectic topology and which is what we construct in §
3
.

Definition 1.13
.

A
curved open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory
is an h-split symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}\colon\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
.

The uncurved open-closed DMFT in Theorem
A
is obtained from a curved one by two separate purely algebraic deformations, discussed in §
2.4
and §
2.5
respectively.

2.
Open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theories

In §
2.1
we define the notion of a curved open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory and discuss its properties, including how it defines a curved
A_{\infty}
algebra.
In §
2.4
, we define deformations of curved open-closed DMFTs and when a deformation yields an uncurved open-closed DMFT.

2.1.
Chains on moduli spaces of curves

Given a collection
\mathcal{D}
of
D
-branes, Costello defines in
[
19
]
a category
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{D}}
, whose objects are tuples
y=(y_{c},s,t\colon y_{o}\to\mathcal{D})
of finite ordered sets
y_{c}
and
y_{o}
, the latter equipped with the source and target maps
s,t
to
\mathcal{D}
. The morphism spaces are, roughly, smooth Riemannian surfaces with boundary, whose boundaries are divided into three types. We will only consider the case where
\mathcal{D}
consists of a single Lagrangian
L
and define a variation of his category, which we denote by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
.

Definition 2.1
.

The category
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
enriched over oriented orbifolds with corners has objects given by pairs
y=(y_{c},y_{o})
of finite ordered sets, a ‘closed’ set and an ‘open’ one

•

To define the space of morphisms from
y
to
y^{\prime}
, let

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}^{\bullet}(y,y^{\prime})\coloneqq\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\geq 0,\\
h\geq\min\{|y_{o}|+|y_{o}^{\prime}|,1\}\end{subarray}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;y,y^{\prime}}

be the disjoint union of moduli spaces of connected stable curves. The marked points are partitioned into incoming and outgoing marked points, labelled by
y_{c}
and
y_{o}
, respectively
y^{\prime}_{c}
and
y^{\prime}_{o}
. If
(g,h,|y_{c}|+|y_{c}^{\prime}|,|y_{o}|+|y^{\prime}_{o}|)
is in the unstable range, we formally define the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;y,y^{\prime}}
to be a point.
Then, set

\qquad\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime})\coloneqq\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y=y_{1}\sqcup\dots y_{m},\\
y^{\prime}=y^{\prime}_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup y^{\prime}_{m}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\,\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}^{\bullet}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}),

that is, we allow for curves that are not necessarily connected.

•

The composition maps

\psi\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime})\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime\prime})

in the stable range are defined by clutching at the marked points associated to
y^{\prime}
.

We define the composition for the allowed unstable cases for each case separately; observe that we write the unstable moduli space, although in each case it is simply a point.

(C1)

The morphism associated to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0;(1,0),(1,0)}
, respectively
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0;(1,0),(1,0)}
is the identity and so the composition with it is simply the identity.

(C2)

The composition ‘with’
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0;(1,0),0}

is the map forgetting the respective outgoing interior marked point, while the composition with
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0;(0,1),0}
forgets the respective incoming interior marked point.

(C3)

The composition with
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1;(0,1),0}
(
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1;(0,1),0}
) forgets an incoming (outgoing) boundary marked point.

(C4)

For
\{i,j\}=\{0,2\}
, the composition with
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0;i,j}
is given by self-clutching at incoming, respectively outgoing interior marked points. Meanwhile, composition with
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1;(0,i),(0,j)}
is given by self-clutching at incoming, respectively outgoing boundary marked points.

(C5)

For
\{j^{-},j\}=\{0,1\}
, the composition
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1;(j^{-},j),0}
is the inclusion of the boundary stratum where a boundary circle collapses.

By construction, the category
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
admits a symmetric monoidal structure given by the disjoint union on objects and morphism spaces. Moreover,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
has several important subcategories.

Definition 2.2
.

We define
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}^{\text{st}}
to be the
stable subcategory
, that is, the subcategory with the same set of objects but where each morphism space only comprises moduli spaces in the stable range, together with the ‘identity’ morphisms, that is, the spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,0;(1,1),0}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1;0,(1,1)}
.

In particular,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}^{\text{st}}
does not have the compositions (
C2
)-(
C5
).

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}^{\text{opr}}
be the
operadic subcategory
, which is the subcategory with the same set of objects, but where the morphism spaces are generated (using the symmetric monoidal structure and compositions) by connected surfaces with at most one output marked point, which is a boundary marked point if the surface is open.

Morphisms in the operadic subcategory are surfaces for which every connected component has at most one output marked point.

Definition 2.4
.

The
closed subcategory

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
has objects
y=(y_{c},\emptyset)
and morphisms given by closed surfaces. The
open subcategory

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{o}
is the full subcategory on the objects
y=(\emptyset,y_{o})
.

While one could directly work with the topological category
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
, we use the category of analytic singular chains on it. In genus zero, there is an obvious choice of chains; in higher genus, the morphism spaces are orbifolds. The standard definition of singular chains on orbifolds (taking the geometric realisation of the diagonal of the nerve) is too unwieldy for our purposes. Since we are only interested in chains with rational or real coefficients, we can use the following model. Note that we are not working with orbifold (co)homology as defined in
[
17
]
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
X=[X_{1}\rightrightarrows X_{0}]
be a topological groupoid so that the source and target maps
s,t\colon X_{1}\to X_{0}
are topological submersions. We define its
singular chain complex
to be

C_{*}(X)=C_{*}(X;\mathbb{R})\coloneqq\text{coker}(t_{*}-s_{*}\colon C_{*}(X_{1};\mathbb{R})\to C_{*}(X_{0};\mathbb{R})).

The definitions of the subcomplex of
analytic
or
smooth
singular chains on
X
, in the context where it is well-defined, are immediate.

Lemma 2.6
.

This definition is homotopy equivalent to the definition of singular chains in
[
9
]
for any étale proper topological groupoid. In particular, there exists a canonical isomorphism

H_{*}(X)\;\cong\;H_{*}(|X|;\mathbb{R}).

Moreover, any refinement
X\to Y
of compact Lie groupoids induces an isomorphism
C_{*}(X)\to C_{*}(Y)
of smooth singular chains.

For our purposes it is important that the chain complex itself is invariant up to equivalence.

Proof.

[
9
]
defines the singular chains, denoted here
C^{B}_{*}(X)
, on a topological groupoid
X
by taking
X_{p}=N_{p}(X)
to be the
p
th level of the nerve of
X
and letting
C^{B}_{*}(X)
be the totalisation of the bicomplex
C_{*}(X_{*})
. Then,
C_{*}(X)=E^{1}_{*,0}
is the first non-zero column of the first page of the spectral sequence associated to
C_{*}(X_{*})
. Since
X
is étale proper, all isotropy groups are finite. Thus,
E^{2}_{p,q}=0
for
p\neq 0
, which shows that the spectral sequence collapses at the second page and we have
H^{B}_{*}(X)\cong H_{*}(X)
. The second claim then follows from
[
9
, Proposition 36]
. The last claim is a straightforward verification, using Ehresmann’s Lemma.
∎

We will also need the following property in §
2.3
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose
f\colon X\to Y
is a submersion of étale proper Lie groupoids. Then, for any simplex
[\sigma]\in C_{k}(X)
, the (orbifold) fibre product
\Delta^{k}{}_{\sigma}\times_{f}X
does not depend on the choice of representation
\sigma\colon\Delta^{k}\to Y
.

Proof.

If
\sigma^{\prime}
is another representative of
\sigma
, then there exists
\rho\colon\Delta^{k}\to Y_{1}
so that
t_{*}\rho=\sigma
and
s_{*}\rho=\sigma^{\prime}
. Thus,
\rho
induces an isomorphism
\Delta^{k}{}_{\sigma}\times_{f}X\to\Delta^{k}{}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\times_{f}X
given by

(v,\varphi,x)\mapsto(v,\varphi\circ\rho(v),x),

on objects and the identity on morphisms.
∎

Example 2.8
.

The orbifold
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;y,y^{\prime}}
can be represented as the almost free
\text{PU}(N+1)
(respectively
\text{PO}(N+1)
)-manifold
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}_{g,y\sqcup y^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C}P^{N};d)
if
h=0
, respectively
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{*}_{g,y\sqcup y^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N};d)
if
h>0
, where
N
and
d
only depend on
g,h,y,y^{\prime}
. In the closed case this is explained in
[
5
]
; in the open case it follows easily by applying
[
35
, §2]
to stable curves. We will use the associated étale proper Lie groupoids for the remainder of the paper.

Clearly, the restriction of an analytic simplex to one of its faces is analytic as well. Thus, we can write
C_{*}^{an}(Y;\mathbb{R})
for the subcomplex of the singular chains on
Y
generated by analytic smooth simplices.

Lemma 2.9
.

H_{*}(C_{*}^{an}(Y;\mathbb{R}))=H_{*}(Y;\mathbb{R})
for any compact analytic orbifold
Y
with corners.

Proof.

By
[
34
, Theorem VII.2.25]
, a function
f\colon\Delta^{m}\to Y
can be arbitrarily well approximated by an analytic function
g_{\epsilon}
so that, if
f
is analytic on an analytic subvariety
K
of
\Delta^{m}
, we can choose
g_{\epsilon}|_{K}=f|_{K}
.
To be precise, the result of
[
34
]
concerns analytic manifolds without corners; to carry it over to the case with corners, we embed
Y
into some Euclidean space and extend
f
to a continuous function in a neighbourhood of
\Delta^{m}
in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}\mid\sum_{i=0}^{m}x_{i}=1\}
. To extend to the case where
Y
is an orbifold, we represent
Y
as a Lie groupoid
[Y_{1}\rightrightarrows Y_{0}]
and argue with
Y_{0}
.
∎

Definition 2.10
.

The symmetric monoidal dg category
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
has the same objects as
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
with morphisms given by the cochain complexes

\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{*}(y,y^{\prime})=(C_{-*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime})),\partial),

of analytic singular chains on the Lie groupoids of Example
2.8
. The symmetric monoidal structure is given by disjoint union on objects, and on morphisms by applying the Eilenberg-Zilber map to the cross product of chains. The braiding
S
of the symmetric monoidal structure is given by taking a disjoint union of spheres and discs with one incoming and one outgoing marked point each and the appropriate labelling of the marked points.

We have to consider a twisted version of
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
. To this end, define the real line bundle

(2.1)

\text{det}=\text{det}_{y,y^{\prime}}\coloneqq\det(\mathbb{C}^{\times y_{c}^{\prime}})^{\vee}\otimes\det(\mathbb{R}^{\times y^{\prime}_{o}})^{\vee}\otimes\det(\bar{\partial}_{(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R})})[-\chi+2|y^{\prime}_{c}|+|y^{\prime}_{o}|].

of degree zero and let
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\det)
be the associated
\mathbb{Z}/2
-torsor.

Lemma 2.11
.

Any composition map
\psi\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime})\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime\prime})

admits a canonical isomorphism

\text{det}^{\otimes r}_{y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime}}\otimes\text{det}^{\otimes r}_{y,y^{\prime}}\to\psi^{*}\text{det}^{\otimes r}_{y,y^{\prime\prime}}

of degree
0
.

Proof.

This can be seen using the isomorphism in
[
18
]
between the determinant line on the clutched surface and the one on its normalisation.
∎

The category
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
has the same objects as
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
with morphisms given by

\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{*}(y,y^{\prime})\coloneqq(C_{-*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime});\text{det}^{\otimes r}),\partial).

Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{\text{st},*}\coloneqq C_{-*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\text{st}}_{oc};\text{det}^{\otimes r})
be the category of chains on the stable subcategory. Define
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{\text{opr}}
similarly using chains on the operadic subcategory
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\text{opr}}_{oc}
.

Remark 2.12
.

The point of twisting the coefficients by this sheaf is that it cancels the difference between the boundary orientation and the fibre product orientation of a boundary stratum of the moduli space of stable maps. Equivalently, we can use the canonical spin structure and the ordering of the boundary components to trivialise
\text{det}^{\otimes r}
over each component of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime})
. This has the effect that the composition maps are equipped with certain signs, depending on
d
and the distribution of marked points. They can be explicitly determined using
[
18
, CROrient 7C and 7H3]
and
[
35
, Theorem 3.5]
.

Since we do not restrict ourselves to unobstructed Lagrangians, we need to consider curvature. To this end, we modify the category
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
, using a few preliminary definitions. We thank Nick Sheridan for fruitful discussions about this.

Construction 2.13

(Lift along marked point)
.

Given
y,y^{\prime}\in\text{obj}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})
and an analytic simplex
\sigma\colon\Delta^{m}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}(y,y^{\prime})
in the stable range, we define the chain
\pi^{*}\sigma\in C_{k+1}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y^{+},y^{\prime}))
as follows. We have
y^{+}:=(y_{c},y_{o}\sqcup\{*\})
where
*
is ordered as the last element of
y_{o}
in the orientation ordering. Then, we can form the fibre product
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y^{+},y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

where
\pi
forgets the boundary marked point labelled by
*
. Let
\widetilde{Z}
be a real analytic resolution of singularities as discussed in §
A
. By
[
32
]
, we can find a triangulation of
\widetilde{Z}
by real analytic simplices. We define
\pi^{*}\sigma=\widetilde{\sigma}
to be the chain associated to this triangulation with trivialisation of
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\pi^{*}\sigma)
induced by the isomorphism

(2.2)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\pi^{*}\sigma)\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R}).

If
\sigma
maps to an unstable moduli space, we set
\pi^{*}\sigma=0
.

Recall that a chain is
degenerate
if it is in the image of the degeneracy maps of the simplicial set
\text{Sing}(X)
. Clearly, a simplex is degenerate if it factors through a manifold of lower dimension; or equivalently, if it is smooth, its differential is nowhere injective.

Lemma 2.14
.

Up to degenerate simplices, the chain
\pi^{*}\sigma
is a well-defined analytic simplex that does not depend on the choices made in Construction
2.13
.

Proof.

That
\pi^{*}\sigma
is a well-defined analytic chain is immediate from the construction. To see the other assertion, we show first that, up to a degenerate chain, it does not depend on the choice of real analytic triangulation. For this it suffices to show that, for any manifold
M
with boundary, two fundamental chains differ by a degenerate chain. Indeed, if
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
are fundamental chains for
M
, then their difference is exact in
H_{*}(M,\partial M)
, so there exists
\tau\in C_{\dim(M)+1}(M)
and
\rho\in C_{\dim(M)}(\partial M)
such that
\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}=d\tau+\rho
. Then
\tau
and
\rho
are degenerate as claimed. If
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}
is another resolution of singularities, we can find
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime\prime}
with blow-down maps
f\colon\widetilde{Z}^{\prime\prime}\to\widetilde{Z}
and
f^{\prime}\colon\widetilde{Z}^{\prime\prime}\to\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}
and using the given triangulations of
\widetilde{Z}
and
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}
we can find triangulations of
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime\prime}
so that the associated chains agree with
\widetilde{\sigma}
respectively
\widetilde{\sigma}^{\prime}
. By the previous step, we thus have that
\widetilde{\sigma}-\widetilde{\sigma}^{\prime}
is degenerate, whence the claim follows.
∎

Definition 2.15
.

The category
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
is the symmetric monoidal dg category generated under composition and the symmetric monoidal structure by the same objects as
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
and the chain complexes

\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}(y,y^{\prime})
for
y\notin\{(0,0),(0,1)\}
and
y^{\prime}\neq(0,1)
as well as the graded vector spaces

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}((0,0),(0,1))

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{*}((0,0),(0,1))\oplus\langle D_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}((0,1),(0,1))

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{*}((0,1),(0,1))\oplus\langle D_{1}\rangle,

where
D_{0}
and
D_{1}
are treated as formal generators with
|D_{i}|=i-2
. We define the differential
\partial_{oc}
by

(2.3)

\partial_{oc}\sigma\coloneqq\partial\sigma+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\pi^{*}\sigma\circ_{*}D_{0}-\sum_{i\in y^{\prime}_{o}}D_{1}\circ_{i}\sigma+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\sum_{i\in y_{o}}\sigma\circ_{i}D_{1},

for
\sigma\in\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}(y,y^{\prime})
, while
\partial_{oc}D_{0}:=-D_{1}\circ D_{0}
and

(2.4)

\partial_{oc}D_{1}\coloneqq-D_{1}\circ D_{1}+D_{2}\circ_{1}D_{0}-D_{2}\circ_{2}D_{0},

and extending across symmetric monoidal structure and compositions.

Notation 2.16
.

It will be useful to define

\pi^{*}D_{0}\coloneqq D_{1}\qquad\quad\pi^{*}D_{1}=D_{2}^{+}+D_{2}^{-}

where
D_{2}^{+}
is the fundamental chain of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1;(0,1),(0,2)}
with the ordering of the incoming marked points on the boundary agreeing with the order of their labels and
D_{2}^{-}
the fundamental chain of the (isomorphic) moduli space where the order of the incoming marked points opposite to their ordering on the boundary.

Remark 2.17
.

Pictorially, we can view
D_{0}
as a disc (labelled by
D_{0}
) with one output, and
D_{1}
as a disc with one input and one output. A composition
D_{1}\circ\sigma
can be viewed as (formally) attaching a
D_{1}
disc bubble to an outgoing boundary marked point of
\sigma
.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{\text{st}}
and
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{\text{opr}}
be the smallest symmetric monoidal subcategories containing
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{\text{st}}
, respectively
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{\text{opr}}
as well as the (formal) morphisms
D_{0}
and
D_{1}
. Equip them with the differential restricted from
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Suppose
\sigma\in C^{an}_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime}))
as well as
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are stable. Then the following holds up to degenerate simplices.

(1)

\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\sigma
is degenerate.

(2)

\pi^{*}(\sigma\times\rho)=(-1)^{|\rho|}\pi^{*}\sigma\times\rho+\sigma\times\pi^{*}\rho
.

(3)

\pi^{*}(\sigma_{2}\circ\sigma_{1})=(-1)^{|\sigma_{1}|}\pi^{*}\sigma_{2}\circ\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}\circ\pi^{*}\sigma_{1}
.

(4)

\pi^{*}
commutes with
\partial_{oc}
.

Proof.

Assertion (
1
) is immediate from the construction. The second claim follows from the fact that the forgetful map on the product of moduli spaces is the product of the forgetful map on one factor and the identity on the other; the sign is determined by the isomorphism (
2.2
). Assertion (
3
) is a consequence of (
2
) and the fact that the clutching maps commute with the forgetful maps. For the last claim, abbreviate
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{+}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y^{+},y^{\prime})
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}\coloneqq\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime})
. We have by
[
41
]
that

\partial\Delta^{k}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{+}=\partial\Delta^{k}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{+}\sqcup\Delta^{k}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\partial^{v}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{+},

where
\partial^{v}
indicates the boundary of the fibres of the forgetful map
\pi_{\kappa}\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{+}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}
, given by the restriction of
\pi
to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{+}
, where the marked point labelled by
*
is between the
\kappa
and
(\kappa+1)
-labelled marked point on the boundary.
This vertical boundary is given by

\partial^{v}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{+}=\text{im}(s_{\kappa,*})^{-1}\sqcup\text{im}(s_{*,\kappa+1}),

where
s_{\kappa,*}\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{+}
is the canonical section of
\pi_{\kappa}
inserting the additional marked point at the marked point labelled by
\kappa
, creating a ghost disc in the process on which
*
is the marked point situated
after
the marked point labelled by
\kappa
in the counter-clockwise ordering, while in
s_{*,\kappa}
the marked point is situated before the
\kappa
-marked point on the boundary. Note that
\kappa+1
could be an outgoing marked point. Summing over all
\kappa\in y_{o}
, we obtain

(2.5)

\partial\pi^{*}\sigma=\pi^{*}(\partial\sigma)+(-1)^{|\sigma|}D_{2}^{-}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\sigma\times\text{id}-(-1)^{|\sigma|}D_{2}^{+}\,\widehat{\circ}\,(\text{id}\times\sigma)+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\sigma\,\widehat{\circ}\,(D_{2}^{+}-D_{2}^{-}).

The sign is due to the fact that we orient
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\pi^{*}\sigma)
via the isomorphism
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\pi^{*}\sigma)\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\ker(d\pi))
over the locus where
d\pi
is surjective.
Thus,

\displaystyle\partial_{oc}\pi^{*}\sigma-\pi^{*}\partial_{oc}\sigma

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\sigma|}D_{2}^{-}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\sigma\times\sigma_{\text{id}}-(-1)^{|\sigma|}D_{2}^{+}\,\widehat{\circ}\,(\sigma_{\text{id}}\times\sigma)+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\sigma\,\widehat{\circ}\,(D_{2}^{+}-D_{2}^{-})

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{|\sigma|+1}(\pi^{*}\sigma)\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{1}-D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\pi^{*}\sigma+(-1)^{|\sigma|+1}\pi^{*}(\pi^{*}\sigma)\circ_{*^{\prime}}D_{0}

\displaystyle\quad-\pi^{*}\Bigl((-1)^{|\sigma|}\pi^{*}\sigma\circ_{*}D_{0}-D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\sigma+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\sigma\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{1}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}(D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\sigma)-D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\pi^{*}\sigma+(-1)^{|\sigma|}D_{2}^{-}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\sigma\times\sigma_{\text{id}}-(-1)^{|\sigma|}D_{2}^{+}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\sigma_{\text{id}}\times\sigma

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{|\sigma|+1}(\pi^{*}\sigma)\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{1}+(-1)^{|\sigma|+1}\pi^{*}(\sigma\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{1})+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\sigma\,\widehat{\circ}\,(D_{2}^{+}-D_{2}^{-})

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{|\sigma|+1}\pi^{*}(\pi^{*}\sigma)\circ_{*^{\prime}}D_{0}-(-1)^{|\sigma|}\pi^{*}(\pi^{*}\sigma\circ_{*}D_{0}).

The first line of the right-hand side vanishes by Claim (
3
). Adding the terms of the second line of the right hand side, we obtain
(-1)^{|\sigma|}\pi^{*}\sigma\circ_{*}D_{1}
because
\pi^{*}\sigma
has the additional incoming boundary marked point labelled by
*
, which is not accounted for in
\pi^{*}(\sigma\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{1})
. Applying Claim (
3
) to the last line, we obtain that the right hand side vanishes.
∎

The following is immediate from a short sign verification.

Corollary 2.20
.

\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
is a symmetric monoidal dg category.

Recall from
[
50
, (1a)]
that any dg category
\mathscr{C}
defines an
A_{\infty}
category on the same objects. We use cohomological grading throughout the paper. We also introduce the following notation.

Notation 2.21
.

Given a composable sequence
\underline{\sigma}=(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{1})
of morphisms in an
A_{\infty}
category
\mathscr{C}
and
i\leq d-1
, we define

\underline{\sigma}^{>i}\coloneqq(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{i+1})\qquad\quad\qquad\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}\coloneqq(\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1})

and

|\underline{\sigma}|^{\prime}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{d}(|\sigma_{i}|-1)

Definition 2.22
.

Given a dg category
\mathscr{C}
, we define the associated
A_{\infty}
category, denoted
\mathscr{C}_{\infty}
to have the same objects and with the same underlying vector spaces as morphism spaces, equipped with the operations

•

\mu^{1}_{\mathscr{C}}\colon\mathscr{C}(x,y)\to\mathscr{C}(x,y):a\mapsto(-1)^{|\alpha|}d(a)
,

•

\mu^{2}_{\mathscr{C}}\colon\mathscr{C}(y,z)\otimes\mathscr{C}(x,y)\to\mathscr{C}(x,z):(a_{2},a_{1})\mapsto(-1)^{|a_{1}|}a_{2}\circ a_{1}
,

•

\mu^{d}_{\mathscr{C}}=0
for
d\geq 3
.

An
A_{\infty}
functor
between dg categories
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
is an
A_{\infty}
functor between their associated
A_{\infty}
categories, that is, a map
\mathscr{F}\colon\text{obj}(\mathscr{C})\to\text{obj}(\mathscr{D})
and for each
d\geq 1
and any objects
x_{0},\dots,x_{d}
of
\mathscr{C}
a map

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{d}\colon\mathscr{C}_{\infty}^{*}(x_{0},\dots,x_{d})\coloneqq\bigotimes\limits_{i=0}^{d-1}\mathscr{C}_{\infty}^{*}(x_{d-i-1},x_{d-i})\to\text{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\infty}}^{*+1-d}(\mathscr{F}(x_{0}),\mathscr{F}(x_{d})).

which we will denote by
\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}
or
\mathscr{F}^{d}(\underline{\sigma})
depending on notational convenience,
satisfying

(2.6)

\mu^{1}_{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}})\;+\;\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mu^{2}_{\mathscr{D}}\Bigl(\mathscr{F}^{d-i}_{\underline{\sigma}^{>i}},\mathscr{F}^{i}_{\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}}\Bigr)\;=\;\sum_{i=1}^{d}(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{<i}|^{\prime}}\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\mu^{1}_{\mathscr{C}}(\sigma_{i}),\dots,\sigma_{1}}\;+\;\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{<i}|^{\prime}}{\mathscr{F}^{d-1}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\mu^{2}_{\mathscr{C}}(\sigma_{i+1},\sigma_{i}),\dots,\sigma_{1}}}.

The
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}
is said to be
unital
if
\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\text{id}_{y}}=\text{id}_{\mathscr{F}(y)}
for any
y\in\text{obj}\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\dots,\text{id},\dots}=0
whenever
d>1
.

Example 2.23
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and
\text{Ch}_{R}
be the category of chain complexes of
R
-modules. This is a dg category with

\text{Ch}_{R}(C^{*},D^{*})_{n}=\{\text{linear maps }f\colon C^{*}\to D^{*+n}\}
and
\delta(f)=d_{D}\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ d_{C}
. It admits the symmetric monoidal structure given by the tensor product over
R
of chain complexes and on morphisms by

\displaystyle f_{1}\otimes f_{2}\colon C^{*}_{1}\otimes_{R}C^{*}_{2}

\displaystyle\to D^{*}_{1}\otimes_{R}D^{*}_{2}

(2.7)

\displaystyle c_{1}\otimes c_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto(-1)^{|f_{2}||c_{1}|}f_{1}(c_{1})\otimes f_{2}(c_{2}).

Let
\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be two
A_{\infty}
functors between
A_{\infty}
categories
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
. Following
[
50
, Section 1h]
, a
pre-natural transformation
between
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
is a sequence
(T^{d})_{d\geq 0}
of linear maps

(2.8)

T^{d}\colon{\mathscr{C}}(X_{d-1},X_{d})\otimes\dots\otimes{\mathscr{C}}(X_{0},X_{1})\rightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{F}(X_{0}),\mathscr{G}(X_{d}))[g-d].

where
|T|=g
is the degree of
T
and we will usually omit the superscript.
The vector space of pre-natural transformations
\mathscr{Q}:=\text{Fun}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})
can be equipped with an
A_{\infty}
structure. For the rest of this section, we take
\mathscr{C}
to be any dg category and
\mathscr{D}=\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
. In this case, using the conventions of Definition
2.22
, the differential on
\mathscr{Q}
is given by

(2.9)

\hskip-8.0pt\mu_{\mathscr{Q}}^{1}(T)(\underline{\sigma})\,\coloneqq\,(-1)^{|T(\underline{\sigma})|}[\partial,T(\underline{\sigma})]\,+\,\sum_{i=0}^{d}\Bigl((-1)^{|T|+|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}}\mathscr{G}(\underline{\sigma}^{>i})\circ T(\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i})-(-1)^{|T||\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}}T(\underline{\sigma}^{>i})\circ\mathscr{F}(\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i})\Bigr)\\
-\sum_{i=1}^{d}(-1)^{|T|+|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}}T(\partial_{i}\underline{\sigma})-\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(-1)^{|T|+|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}}T(\ast_{i}\underline{\sigma}).

Definition 2.24
.

A pre-natural transformation
T
is a
natural transformation
if
\mu_{\mathscr{Q}}^{1}(T)=0
.

The composition of two natural transformations
T
from
\mathscr{F}
to
\mathscr{G}
and
S
from
\mathscr{G}
to
\mathscr{H}
is given by

(2.10)

\hskip-12.0pt\mu_{\mathscr{Q}}^{2}(S,T)(\underline{\sigma})\,=\,\sum_{j=0}^{d}(-1)^{|T|+|S||\underline{\sigma}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}}S(\underline{\sigma}^{>j})\circ T(\underline{\sigma}^{\leq j}).

2.2.
Symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
functors

In general, defining what it means for an
A_{\infty}
category
\mathscr{C}
to be (symmetric) monoidal is difficult. The
A_{\infty}
categories
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
and
\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
that are important in this paper are dg categories, in which case the being symmetric monoidal is a well-known property. However, an open-closed DMFT is an
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}\colon\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
, and so it is not immediate what it means for
\mathscr{F}
to be symmetric monoidal. Based on
[
4
]
, we will give a definition here in the case of
A_{\infty}
functors defined on dg categories. As before, we assume
\Lambda
is filtered and that all
A_{\infty}
functors discussed below are filtered as well.

Notation 2.25
.

For a sequence
\underline{p}=(p_{0}=0<p_{1}<\dots<p_{r}<d=p_{r+1})
, let

(2.11)

\ast_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma}:=(\sigma_{d}\circ\dots\circ\sigma_{p_{r}+1},\dots,\sigma_{p_{1}}\circ\dots\circ\sigma_{1})

and

(2.12)

\mathscr{F}^{\circ\underline{p}}(\underline{\sigma}):=\mathscr{F}^{d-p_{r}}(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{p_{r}+1})\circ\dots\circ\mathscr{F}^{p_{1}}(\sigma_{p_{1}},\dots,\sigma_{1}),

setting
\mathscr{F}^{-1}=0
to have uniform notation.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be symmetric monoidal dg category and
\mathscr{F}\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
be an
A_{\infty}
functor. The following definitions are inspired by
[
4
, Definition 3.9]
.
We define the dg category
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}
to have

•

objects: pairs
(x,y)
with
x,y\in\text{obj}(\mathscr{C})
,

•

morphism spaces

\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\,:=\,\mathscr{C}(x,x^{\prime})\otimes\mathscr{C}(y,y^{\prime})

with differential
d(\sigma\otimes\tau)=(d\sigma)\otimes\tau+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\sigma\otimes(d\tau)
and

•

composition given by
(\sigma^{\prime}\otimes\tau^{\prime})\circ(\sigma\circ\tau)=(-1)^{|\tau^{\prime}||\sigma|}(\sigma^{\prime}\circ\sigma)\otimes(\tau^{\prime}\circ\tau)
.

Given a composable sequence
\underline{\sigma}
of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
, set

|\underline{\sigma}+\underline{p}|\coloneqq(|\sigma_{d}|,\dots,|\sigma_{p_{r}+1}|,|\sigma_{p_{r}}|^{\prime},|\sigma_{p_{r}-1}|,\dots,|\sigma_{p_{1}}|^{\prime},\dots,|\sigma_{1}|),

that is, adding
-1
to the entries labelled by
p_{r},\dots,p_{1}
.
Similarly let
|\underline{\sigma}+\underline{p}^{c}|
be the sequence obtained by adding
-1
to all entries not labelled by
p_{r},\dots,p_{1}
.
Then, define

\displaystyle\star_{1}

\displaystyle:=\dagger(|\underline{\sigma}+\underline{p}|,|\underline{\tau}+\underline{p}^{c}|)

\displaystyle\star_{2}

\displaystyle:=\dagger(|\underline{\sigma}+\underline{p}^{c}|,|\underline{\tau}+\underline{p}|),

Also define

\dagger(\underline{a},\underline{b})\;\coloneqq\;\sum_{i<j}a_{i}b_{j}

for sequences
\underline{a}
and
\underline{b}
of integers of the same length.

Lemma 2.26
.

The following formulas define
A_{\infty}
functors
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}

•

\mathscr{F}\circ\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}
by
\mathscr{F}\circ\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}(X,Y)=\mathscr{F}(X\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}Y)
on objects and

(2.13)

\quad(\mathscr{F}\circ\otimes_{\mathscr{C}})^{d}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau}):=\mathscr{F}^{d}(\sigma_{d}\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}\tau_{d},\dots,\sigma_{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}\tau_{1})

•

\text{}\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{F})
by
\text{}\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{F})(X,Y)=\mathscr{F}(X)\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\mathscr{F}(Y)
on objects and

(2.14)

\quad(\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{F}))^{d}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau})\coloneqq\sum_{1\leq p_{1}<\dots<p_{r}<d}(-1)^{\dagger(|\underline{\sigma}+\underline{p}|,|\underline{\tau}+\underline{p}^{c}|)}\mathscr{F}(\ast_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma})\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\mathscr{F}^{\circ\underline{p}}(\underline{\tau}),

•

\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathscr{F})
by
\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathscr{F})(X,Y)=\mathscr{F}(X)\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\mathscr{F}(Y)
on objects and

(2.15)

\quad(\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\,\overline{\otimes}\,\mathscr{F}))^{d}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau})\coloneqq\sum_{1\leq p_{1}<\dots<p_{r}<d}(-1)^{\dagger(|\underline{\sigma}+\underline{p}^{c}|,|\underline{\tau}+\underline{p}|)}\mathscr{F}^{\circ\underline{p}}(\underline{\sigma})\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\mathscr{F}(\ast_{\underline{p}}\underline{\tau}),

where we have used
\circ
to denote the composition on both
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
.

Proof.

The first definition is a composition of
A_{\infty}
functors, thus trivially an
A_{\infty}
functor. For the other two, it remains to show that
\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau}\mapsto(-1)^{\star_{1}}\mathscr{F}(\ast_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma})\otimes\mathscr{F}^{\circ\underline{p}}(\underline{\tau})
is an
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\mathscr{D}
.
This is exactly
[
4
, Definition 3.9]
, whence the claim follows from Proposition 3.8 op. cit..
∎

Definition 2.27
.

An
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
between monoidal dg categories is
monoidal
if it is equipped with the data of natural transformations
R
from
\text{}\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{F})
to
\mathscr{F}\circ\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}
, and
L
from
\text{}\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\overline{\otimes}\mathscr{F})
to
\mathscr{F}\circ\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}
with
R^{0}=L^{0}
. If
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
are symmetric monoidal with braiding
S
, then
\mathscr{F}
is
symmetric monoidal
if it is monoidal,

\mathscr{F}(S_{x_{d},y_{d}})\circ R(\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau})=L(\underline{\tau}\otimes\underline{\sigma})\circ S_{\mathscr{F}(x_{0}),\mathscr{F}(y_{0})}

and the diagram

(2.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}(x)\otimes\mathscr{F}(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}(x\otimes y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}(y)\otimes\mathscr{F}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}(y\otimes x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{0}_{x,y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathscr{F}(x),\mathscr{F}(y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}(S_{x,y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{0}_{y,x}}

commutes.
If
\mathscr{F}
is a unital
A_{\infty}
functor,
\mathscr{F}
is
unital symmetric monoidal
if moreover for all
d\geq 1

(2.17)

R^{d}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau})=L^{d}(\underline{\tau}\otimes\underline{\sigma})=0

whenever for each
i
either
\sigma_{i}
or
\tau_{i}
is the identity morphism.

The following definition is due to Costello,
[
19
]
, in the case of dg functors.

Definition 2.28
.

A monoidal
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}
is
h-split
if
R^{0}
and
L^{0}
are quasi-isomorphisms.

Remark 2.29
.

As noted below
[
4
, Definition 3.9]
, the definition of
\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{F})
is not functorial, but is expected to be so up to homotopy.

We have the following direct consequence of unitality.

Lemma 2.30
.

If
\mathscr{F}
is a unital symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
functor, then

\mathscr{F}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}(\sigma_{\text{id}}^{y},\dots,\sigma_{\text{id}}^{y}))\circ R^{0}_{x_{0},y}\;=\;R^{0}_{x_{d},y}\circ\left(\mathscr{F}(\underline{\sigma})\otimes\text{id}_{\mathscr{F}(y)}\right),

and

\mathscr{F}((\sigma_{\text{id}}^{x},\dots,\sigma_{\text{id}}^{x})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}\underline{\tau})\circ L^{0}_{x,y_{0}}\;=\;L^{0}_{x,y_{d}}\circ\left(\text{id}_{\mathscr{F}(x)}\otimes\mathscr{F}(\underline{\tau})\right).

Proof.

By the unitality of
\mathscr{F}
and the natural transformations
R
and
L
, all other terms in Equation (
2.9
) vanish. Thus, the claim follows from a sign computation.
∎

2.3.
Curved open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theories

Fix a valuation ring
(\Lambda,\nu)
equipped with an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra structure. Later, we will take
\Lambda
to be a Novikov ring defined in (
3.1
). Let
\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
be the dg category of cochain complexes over
\Lambda
equipped with the symmetric monoidal product
\otimes_{\Lambda}
.

Definition 2.31
.

A
curved open-closed
r
-dimensional Deligne–Mumford field theory
over a filtered ring
\Lambda
is an h-split unital symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}\colon\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
such that

•

(nondegeneracy)
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{1}}=0
whenever some
\sigma_{i}
is a degenerate chain,

•

(positivity)
\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}}
and
\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}
have positive filtration.

Remark 2.32
.

One could use any other model of chains on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
for this definition. In fact, we will construct a specific model in §
3.1
that will allow us to achieve better properties for the (curved) open-closed DMFT we define.

Remark 2.33
.

Explicitly, a curved
r
-dimensional open-closed Deligne-Mumford field theory consists of a filtered graded chain complex
\mathscr{F}^{*}(y)=(V_{y},d_{y})
for each
y\in\text{obj}(\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r})
, and a sequence
(\mathscr{F}^{d})_{d\geq 1}
of linear maps

\mathscr{F}^{d}\colon\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{*}_{r}(y^{d-1},y^{d})\otimes\dots\otimes\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{*}_{r}(y^{0},y^{1})\to\text{Hom}^{*+1-d}_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}(\mathscr{F}(y^{0}),\mathscr{F}(y^{d}))

such that
\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}}
and
\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}
have positive filtration, and

(2.18)

(-1)^{|\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}|}(d_{y_{d}}\circ\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}-(-1)^{|\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}|}\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}\circ d_{y_{0}})+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}{(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+1}\mathscr{F}^{d-i}_{\underline{\sigma}^{>i}}\circ\mathscr{F}^{i}_{\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}}}\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+1}\mathscr{F}^{d}_{(\sigma_{1},\dots,\partial_{oc}\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{d})}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}{(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+1}\mathscr{F}^{d-1}_{(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{i+1}\circ\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1})}},

such that
\mathscr{F}
is unital symmetric monoidal.

Definition 2.34
.

An
(uncurved) open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory
is an h-split unital symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
functor
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
satisfying analogous conditions.

Definition 2.35
.

The
open DMFT
obtained from
\mathscr{F}
is the restriction to the full subcategory on objects
y=(\emptyset,y_{o})
, while the
(closed) DMFT
underlying
\mathscr{F}
is the restriction to the subcategory with objects
y=(y_{c},\emptyset)
and morphism given by chains on moduli space of closed curves.

Lemma 2.36
.

If
\mathscr{F}
is a curved open-closed DMFT with closed sector
V^{*}
, then
\mathscr{F}
defines the structure of a cohomological field theory on
H^{*}(V^{*})
, i.e. a symmetric monoidal functor
H_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c})\rightarrow Vect_{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

The
A_{\infty}
equation for
\mathscr{F}^{1}
and
\sigma\in C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{c})
yields that
d\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma}(\alpha)=(-1)^{|\sigma|}\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma}(d\alpha)+\mathscr{F}_{d\sigma}^{1}(\alpha)
, whence
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}^{1}
is a chain map for such
\sigma
if
\partial\sigma=0
. Meanwhile, the
A_{\infty}
relation for
\mathscr{F}^{2}
, applied to two cycles
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
yields that the resulting map on cohomology is a functor, using that
|\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{i}}^{1}|=|\sigma_{i}|
.
∎

Definition 2.37
.

An open-closed DMFT
\mathscr{F}\colon\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}\to\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
is

•

strict
if
\mathscr{F}^{d}=0
for
d\geq 2
.

•

semi-strict
if
\mathscr{F}^{d}
vanishes on
({\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}}^{\text{st},*})^{\otimes d}
whenever
d\geq 2
.

•

operadic
if
\mathscr{F}^{d}
vanishes on
({\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}}^{\text{opr},*})^{\otimes d}
whenever
d\geq 2
.

•

weakly curved
if it is operadic and
\mathscr{F}^{1}_{D_{0}}=c\,e_{\mathscr{F}}
for some
c\in\Lambda
, where
e_{\mathscr{F}}
denotes the (open) unit.

Definition 2.38
.

Let
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}_{r}
be the symmetric monoidal dg category obtained from
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
by setting
D_{0}
equal to
0
.

Definition 2.39
.

If
\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}=0
whenever
D_{0}
appears in
\underline{\sigma}
(either as an element of the tuple or composed with another chain), we say that
\mathscr{F}
is a curved open-closed DMFT
with vanishing curvature
. Equivalently, one can view this as a functor
\mathscr{F}\colon\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}_{r}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
.

A semi-strict curved open-closed DMFT gives rise to a curved
A_{\infty}
algebra.

Lemma 2.40
.

Suppose
\mathscr{F}
is a semi-strict open-closed DMFT with
(\mathscr{A},d)\coloneqq\mathscr{F}(0,1)
. Then
\mathscr{F}
defines the structure of a curved
A_{\infty}
algebra on
\mathscr{A}
. If
\mathscr{F}
is operadic, then
\mathscr{A}
is a curved cyclic unital
A_{\infty}
algebra.

Proof.

To make the sign computations more manageable, we introduce the following notation. For
\alpha\in\mathscr{F}(x)
,
\beta\in\mathscr{F}(y)
, let

\alpha\times\beta:=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}R^{0}(\alpha\otimes\beta).

where
R=(R^{d})_{d\geq 0}
is one natural transformation of the right symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathscr{F}
.
We then find that

(2.19)

\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma\times\sigma_{\text{id}}}(\alpha\times\beta)=(-1)^{{|\sigma||\beta|}}\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma}(\alpha)\times\beta.

Let
\mu_{\mathscr{A}}^{1}(\alpha)\coloneqq(-1)^{|\alpha|}(d_{0,1}(\alpha)+\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}(\alpha))
be the
A_{\infty}
differential of
\mathscr{A}
. Additionally define

\mu_{\mathscr{A}}^{0}:=-\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}}.

To define the higher operations, let
D_{k}\in\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{k-2}((\emptyset,\{1,\dots,k\}),(\emptyset,1))
be an inductive choice of analytic fundamental chain so that its boundary is given by

(2.20)

\partial D_{k}\;=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{0}+k_{1}=k+1\\
k_{0},k_{1}\geq 2\end{subarray}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq k_{0}}(-1)^{k_{1}(k_{0}-i)+i}D_{k_{0}}\circ(\sigma_{\text{id}}^{i-1}\times D_{k_{1}}\times\sigma_{\text{id}}^{k_{0}-i}),

where the map
\psi
clutches the output of
D_{k_{1}}
to the
i
’th marked point of
D_{k_{0}}
.
Then, for
k\geq 2
, let

\mu^{k}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k})=(-1)^{\epsilon(\alpha)}\mathscr{F}^{1}_{D_{k}}(\alpha_{1}\times\dots\times\alpha_{k}).

where

(2.21)

\epsilon(\alpha)=1+\sum_{j=1}^{k}j(|\alpha_{j}|+1)

Note that this agrees with the signs in
[
53
]
.
As before, we use the notation
D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{k}
instead of
\sum_{i\in y^{\prime}_{o}}D_{1}\circ_{i}D_{k}
, and similarly for the reverse composition.
Note that
|\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}}|=|D_{k}|\equiv k\mod{2}
. Thus, Equation (
2.18
) specialises to

(2.22)

d\circ\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}}-(-1)^{k}\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}}\circ d\;=\;\mathscr{F}_{\partial D_{k}}-\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{k}}+(-1)^{k}\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{1}}+(-1)^{k}\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{*}D_{k}\circ_{*}D_{0}}

To see that this yields the
A_{\infty}
equations, we write out the terms one by one.
Given
1\leq i\leq k_{0}
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}\in\mathscr{A}
we use the short hand

\alpha^{1}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{i-1}),\qquad\alpha^{2}=(\alpha_{i},\dots,\alpha_{i+k_{1}-1}),\qquad\alpha^{3}=(\alpha_{i+k_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{k})

whenever
i
is clear from context.
Moreover, we write
\alpha
for
\alpha_{1}\times\dots\times\alpha_{k}
as well.
First observe that Equation (
2.19
) yields

\mathscr{F}_{\text{id}^{i-1}\times D_{k_{1}}\times\text{id}^{k_{0}-i}}(\alpha)=(-1)^{k_{1}|\alpha^{3}|}\alpha^{1}\times\mathscr{F}_{D_{k_{1}}}(\alpha^{2})\times\alpha^{3},

whence

\mathscr{F}_{\partial D_{k}}=(-1)^{|\alpha|+k+\epsilon(\alpha)+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{0}+k_{1}=k\\
i=1,\dots,k_{0}\\
k_{0},k_{1}\geq 2\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|\alpha^{1}|^{\prime}}\mu^{k_{0}}_{\mathscr{A}}(\alpha^{1},\mu^{k_{1}}_{\mathscr{A}}(\alpha^{2}),\alpha^{3}).

The sign here comes from the fact that

k_{1}(k_{0}-i)+k_{1}|\alpha^{3}|+\epsilon(\alpha^{2})+\epsilon(\alpha^{1},\mu^{k_{1}}_{\mathscr{A}}(\alpha^{2}),\alpha^{3})\equiv|\alpha|+k+\epsilon(\alpha)+|\alpha^{1}|+i,

where we have used
[
53
, Lemma 2.9]
to compute
\epsilon(\alpha^{2})+\epsilon(\alpha^{1},\mu^{k_{1}}_{\mathscr{A}}(\alpha^{2}),\alpha^{3})
.
Since
\mathscr{F}
is semi-strict,

\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{k}}=\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}}\text{ and }\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{1}}=\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}.

We thus find that

(d\circ\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}}+\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{k}})(\alpha)=(-1)^{|\alpha|+k}\mu^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}}(\alpha))=(-1)^{|\alpha|+k+\epsilon(\alpha)}\mu^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mu^{k}_{\mathscr{A}}(\alpha)).

Similarly,

(\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{1}}+\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}}\circ d)(\alpha)=(-1)^{|\alpha|+1+\epsilon(\alpha)}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{|\alpha^{1}|+i-1}\mu^{k}_{\mathscr{A}}(\alpha^{1},\mu^{1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\alpha_{i}),\alpha^{3}),

For the curvature terms, first observe that because
\pi^{*}D_{k}
has the extra marked point ordered as the last incoming marked point, we have

\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{*}D_{k}\circ D_{0}}(\alpha)=-\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{*}D_{k}}(\alpha\times\mu^{0}_{\mathscr{A}})=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{k-i+1}\mathscr{F}_{D_{k+1}}(\alpha_{1}\times\dots\times\alpha_{i}\times\mu^{0}_{\mathscr{A}}\times\alpha_{i+1}\times\dots\times\alpha_{k}),

where the sign
(-1)^{k-i}
comes from reordering the marked points.
Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}|+\dots+|\alpha_{i}|-i}\mu^{k+1}_{\mathscr{A}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{i},\mu^{0}_{\mathscr{A}},\alpha_{i+1},\dots,\alpha_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(-1)^{\epsilon(\alpha)+|\alpha|+k-i}\mathscr{F}_{D_{k+1}}(\alpha_{1}\times\dots\times\alpha_{i}\times\mu^{0}_{\mathscr{A}}\times\alpha_{i+1}\times\dots\times\alpha_{k})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\alpha|+\epsilon(\alpha)+1}\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{*}D_{k}\circ_{*}D_{0}}(\alpha),

where the second equality follows from the fact that
\epsilon(\alpha)+\epsilon(\alpha^{1},\mu^{0}_{\mathscr{A}},\alpha_{2})=|\alpha^{2}|^{\prime}+i=|\alpha^{2}|+k
.

When
\mathscr{F}
is operadic, we have that
\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}}(e_{\mathscr{F}},\dots)=\mathscr{F}_{(\pi_{1})_{*}D_{k}}(\dots)=0
, where
\pi_{1}
denotes the map forgetting the first incoming boundary marked point. Being operadic also implies that
\mathscr{F}_{D_{2}}(e_{\mathscr{F}},\alpha)=\mathscr{F}_{D_{2}}(\alpha,e_{\mathscr{F}})=\alpha
, whence unitality of
\mathscr{A}
follows.

Define the pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathscr{A}}:\mathscr{A}^{\otimes 2}\rightarrow\Lambda
by
\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\rangle_{\mathscr{A}}=(-1)^{|\alpha_{2}|}\mathscr{F}_{D_{2,0}}(\alpha_{1}\times\alpha_{2})
, where
D_{2,0}
denotes the surface giving rise to the pairing, that is, the disc with
2
incoming marked points, and no outgoing marked points. Let
P_{k+1}
denote the chain obtained by the composition
D_{2,0}\circ(D_{k}\times\sigma_{\text{id}})
. This represents the moduli space of discs with
k+1
incoming marked points. It admits a
\mathbb{Z}/(k+1)
action by cyclically relabelling the marked points. The action of the generator
\gamma
induces an isomorphism with sign
(-1)^{k}
(obtained by moving the
k+1
st marked point all the way past the others).
Thus,

\mathscr{F}_{P_{k+1}}(\alpha\times\alpha_{0})=(-1)^{k+|\alpha_{0}||\alpha|}\mathscr{F}_{P_{k+1}}(\alpha_{0}\times\alpha),

for
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\times\dots\times\alpha_{k}
. If
\mathscr{F}
is operadic, Equation (
2.19
) implies

\mathscr{F}_{P_{k+1}}(\alpha\times\alpha_{0})=\mathscr{F}_{D_{2,0}}\circ\mathscr{F}_{(D_{k}\times id)}(\alpha\times\alpha_{0})=(-1)^{(k-1)|\alpha_{0}|+\epsilon(\alpha)}\langle\mu^{k}_{\mathscr{A}}(\alpha),\alpha_{0}\rangle_{\mathscr{A}},

whereas

\mathscr{F}_{P_{k+1}}(\alpha_{0}\times\alpha)=(-1)^{(k-1)|\alpha_{k}|+\epsilon(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{k-1})}\langle\mu^{k}_{\mathscr{A}}(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{k-1}),\alpha_{k}\rangle_{\mathscr{A}}.

A short sign computation then shows the result.
∎

Remark 2.41
.

Similar to our construction of the modified chain complex
C_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})
in
3.1
, one can use the higher homotopies to associate an
A_{\infty}
algebra to an open-closed DMFT which is not necessarily semi-strict.

One can view a weakly curved
A_{\infty}
algebra as an uncurved
A_{\infty}
algebra. The same is true for open-closed DMFTs, provided it has the following properties.

Property 2.42
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be an operadic open-closed DMFT with open unit
e_{\mathscr{F}}
and let
\underline{\sigma}\in\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}(x,\dots,x^{\prime})
be any composable sequence. Then, we have the properties

(1)

\mathscr{F}^{d}_{(\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}})}(R^{0}(\alpha\otimes e_{\mathscr{F}}))=0
.

(2)

for any
k\geq 0
,

\mathscr{F}_{(\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\sigma_{i}\circ_{*}D_{k},\sigma_{i-1},\dots,\sigma_{1})}\circ R^{0}\;=\;(-1)^{k|\underline{\sigma}^{<i}|^{\prime}}\mathscr{F}_{(\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i-1},\dots,\sigma_{1})}\circ(\text{id}\otimes\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}})\circ R^{0}

on
\mathscr{F}^{*}(y^{0})\otimes\mathscr{F}(0,k)
.

Property (
1
) can be seen as a stronger version of operadic, as it says that ‘the unit is compatible with any chain of the form
\pi^{*}\sigma
’.
For our open-closed DMFT, these properties are shown in Lemma
3.42
.

Lemma 2.43
.

If
\mathscr{F}
is a weakly curved
r
-dimensional open-closed DMFT, and Property
2.42
holds, then
\mathscr{F}
restricts to a functor
\mathscr{F}\colon\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}_{r}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

The only way that
D_{0}
shows up in the structure equations is through
\mathscr{F}_{(\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\sigma_{i}\circ_{*}D_{0},\dots,\sigma_{1})}
. We thus need to show that this vanishes whenever
\mathscr{F}
is weakly curved.
Applying both Properties
2.42
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{(\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\sigma_{i}\circ_{*}D_{0},\dots,\sigma_{1})}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\;=\;\pm\,\mathscr{F}_{(\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}})}(R^{0}(\alpha\otimes\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}}))
