\displaystyle\;=\;\pm\,c\,\mathscr{F}_{(\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}})}(R^{0}(\alpha\otimes e_{\mathscr{F}}))

\displaystyle\;=\;0

as required.
∎

2.4.
Deformations and bounding cochains

Recall from
[
26
, Definition 3.6.29]
that a weak bounding cochain for a filtered, unital curved
A_{\infty}
algebra
\mathscr{A}
is an element
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathscr{A}
of degree
1
and positive valuation such that

(2.23)

\sum_{k\geq 0}\mu^{k}_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathfrak{b}^{\otimes k})=c\cdot 1,

for some
c\in\Lambda_{>0}
. When
c=0
, the element
\mathfrak{b}
is called a (strong) bounding cochain.
[
26
]
show that given any element
\mathfrak{b}
of degree
1
with positive valuation, one obtains the deformed
A_{\infty}
algebra
(\mathscr{A},\mu_{\mathfrak{b}})
with operations

(2.24)

\mu^{k}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{k}):=\sum_{i_{0},\dots,i_{k}\geq 0}\mu^{i_{0}+\dots+i_{k}+k}(\mathfrak{b}^{\otimes i_{0}}\otimes\alpha_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\alpha_{k}\otimes\mathfrak{b}^{\otimes i_{k}}).

If
\mathfrak{b}
is a weak bounding cochain, then the operations
\mu_{\mathfrak{b}}^{k}
define an (uncurved)
A_{\infty}
structure.

The main result of this section, Corollary
3.2
, asserts that we can similarly deform a curved open-closed DMFT
\mathscr{F}
by an element
\mathfrak{b}
of degree
1
with positive valuation. If
\mathfrak{b}
is a weak bounding cochain and
\mathscr{F}
satisfies certain properties, this defines an (uncurved) open-closed DMFT.

Definition 2.44
.

A
(weak) bounding cochain
for a semi-strict (or operadic) curved open-closed DMFT
\mathscr{F}
is a (weak) bounding cochain for the associated
A_{\infty}
algebra of Lemma
2.40
.

We will need the following preliminary definitions.

Definition 2.45
.

For a tuple
\underline{\sigma}=(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{1})
, define the pullback

(2.25)

\pi^{*}\underline{\sigma}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{<i}|^{\prime}}(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{i+1},\pi^{*}\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i-1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}},\dots,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}}).

Remark 2.46
.

It follows that the iterated lift of the sequence
\underline{\sigma}
satisfies

(2.26)

\pi^{[k]}\underline{\sigma}=\sum_{k_{d}+\dots k_{1}=k}(-1)^{\dagger(\underline{k},\underline{\sigma})}(\pi^{[k_{d}]}\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{[k_{1}]}\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}}^{k-k_{1}}),

for
k\geq 1
, where
\dagger(\underline{k},\underline{\sigma})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(|\underline{\sigma}^{<i}|^{\prime}+|\underline{k}^{<i}|)
.

Lemma 2.47
.

Suppose
\underline{\sigma}
is a composable sequence of chains and
\underline{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})
. Set

\pi^{[\underline{k}]}\underline{\sigma}\coloneqq(-1)^{\dagger(\underline{k},\underline{\sigma})}(\pi^{[k_{d}]}\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{[k_{1}]}\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}}^{k-k_{1}}).

Then, we have for any
i\leq d
that

•

\pi^{[\underline{k}]}((\partial_{oc})_{i}\underline{\sigma})=(-1)^{|\underline{k}^{>i}|}(\partial_{oc})_{i}(\pi^{[\underline{k}]}\underline{\sigma})
, where
(\partial_{oc})_{i}\underline{\sigma}=(\sigma_{d},\dots,\partial_{oc}\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1})
.

•

\ast_{i}\pi^{[\underline{k}]}\underline{\sigma}=(-1)^{|\underline{k}^{>i}|}\pi^{[\underline{k}]}(\ast_{i}\underline{\sigma})
;

•

((\pi^{\underline{k}}\underline{\sigma})^{>i},(\pi^{\underline{k}}\underline{\sigma})^{\leq i})=(-1)^{(|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+|\underline{k}^{\leq i}|)|\underline{k}^{>i}|}(\pi^{\underline{k}^{>i}}(\underline{\sigma}^{>i}),\pi^{\underline{k}^{\leq i}}(\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}))
.

Proof.

The first two claims follow from Lemma
2.19
and the fact

\dagger(\underline{k},\partial_{i}\underline{\sigma})-\dagger(\underline{k},\underline{\sigma})=|\underline{k}^{>i}|=\dagger(\underline{k},\underline{\sigma})-\dagger(\underline{k},\ast_{i}\underline{\sigma}).

The third claim follows from a direct computation of the sign difference.
∎

More generally, for
\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau}=(\sigma_{d}\otimes\tau_{d},\dots,\sigma_{1}\otimes\tau_{1})
and
k=k_{0}+k_{1}
, we define

\pi^{[k_{0},k_{1}]}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau})=\sum\limits_{k^{i}_{1}+\dots+k^{i}_{d}=k_{i}}{(-1)^{\dagger(\underline{k}^{0},\underline{k}^{1},\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau})}(\pi^{[k^{0}_{d}]}\sigma_{d}\otimes\pi^{[k^{1}_{d}]}\tau_{d},\dots,\pi^{[k^{0}_{1}]}\sigma_{1}\otimes\pi^{[k^{1}_{1}]}\tau_{1})},

with

\dagger(\underline{k}^{0},\underline{k}^{1},\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}):=\sum_{i=1}^{d}k^{1}_{i}(|\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau}^{<i}|^{\prime}+|\underline{k}^{<i}|)+k^{0}_{i}(|\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau}^{<i}|^{\prime}+|\underline{k}^{<i}|+|\sigma_{i}|+|k^{0}_{i}|).

Definition 2.48
.

Given an element
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathscr{A}
, with
|\mathfrak{b}|=1
and positive valuation, the associated
deformed curved open-closed DMFT

\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}
is defined by
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}=\mathscr{F}
on objects and by

(2.27)

\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)\,\coloneqq\,\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{k|\alpha|}}{k!}\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{[k]}\underline{\sigma}}(R^{0}(\alpha\otimes\mathfrak{b}^{\otimes k})),

on composable sequences
\underline{\sigma}
of simplices in
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}(y,y^{\prime})
such that
\sigma_{i}\neq\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\circ_{\kappa}D_{0}
for some
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for any
i
. For sequences of simplices containing such compositions, we define
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}_{\underline{\sigma}}
inductively by

(2.28)

\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)\,\coloneqq\,\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{k|\alpha|}}{k!}\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{[k]}\underline{\sigma}}(R^{0}(\alpha\otimes\mathfrak{b}^{\otimes k}))+\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{k|\alpha|}}{k!}\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{[k-1]}\underline{\sigma}^{\prime}}(R^{0}(\alpha\otimes d(\mathfrak{b})\otimes\mathfrak{b}^{\otimes k-1})),

where
\sigma^{\prime}_{j}=\sigma_{j}
for
i\neq j
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{j}=\sigma_{i}^{\prime}
for
j=i
.
Finally, extend linearly to composable sequences of chains.
The associated symmetric monoidal transformations
R
and
L
are defined by
(R^{\mathfrak{b}})^{0}=R^{0}
and

(2.29)

R^{\mathfrak{b}}_{\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau}}(\alpha\otimes\beta)\;=\;\sum\limits_{k\geq 0}{\sum_{k_{0}+k_{1}=k}\frac{(-1)^{k_{0}|\alpha|+k_{1}|\beta|}}{k!}R_{\pi^{[k_{0},k_{1}]}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau})}\Bigl((\alpha\otimes\mathfrak{b}^{\otimes k_{0}})\otimes(\beta\otimes\mathfrak{b}^{\otimes k_{1}})\Bigr)}

for sequences in
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}

and a sum as in (
2.28
) involving compositions with
D_{0}
or
D_{1}
. We define
L^{\mathfrak{b}}
analogously.

In particular,

(2.30)

\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}_{D_{1}}(\alpha)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{k|\alpha|}}{k!}\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{[k]}D_{1}}(R^{0}(\alpha\otimes\mathfrak{b}^{\otimes k}))

and

(2.31)

\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}_{D_{0}}=d(\mathfrak{b})+\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{[k]}D_{0}}(R^{0}(\mathfrak{b}^{\otimes k})).

Remark 2.49
.

Note that the operations
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}_{\sigma}
are only deformed for stable chains and
D_{0}
and
D_{1}
because the lift constructed in (
2.26
) vanishes on unstable chains.

Remark 2.50
.

Recalling the definition
\alpha_{1}\times\alpha_{2}:=(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}||\alpha_{2}|}R^{0}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})
, which agrees in our geometric setting with cross-product of differential forms, the deformed DMFT is given by

\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)\,\coloneqq\,\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{[k]}\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha\times\mathfrak{b}^{\times k}).

In particular, if
\mathscr{F}
is semi-strict or operadic, the deformation of the underlying
A_{\infty}
algebra (as defined in
[
25
, Definition 13.39]
) associated to
\mathscr{F}
agrees with the
A_{\infty}
algebra associated to
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}
. This follows from a sign computation and the observation that we require a factor of
\frac{1}{k!}
because we sum over
k!
many fundamental chains since each insertion of
\mathfrak{b}
can be along any of the boundary segments, as opposed to the definition of the deformation in the
A_{\infty}
algebra setting, where each insertion of
\mathfrak{b}
is fixed on a specific boundary segment.

The following result shows that (weak) bounding cochains suffice to turn a curved open-closed DMFT into an open-closed DMFT with vanishing curvature. In the next subsection we will show how that gives rise to an open-closed DMFT.

Proposition 2.51
.

If
\mathscr{F}
has Property
2.42
(
2
), then
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}
is a curved open-closed DMFT. If
\mathfrak{b}
is a weak bounding cochain,
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}
is weakly curved.

Proof.

This is a lengthy but, given Lemma
2.47
straightforward, computation, which reduces the
A_{\infty}
functor equations for
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}_{\underline{\sigma}}
to (the sum of) the
A_{\infty}
functor equations for
\mathscr{F}_{\pi^{[k]}\underline{\sigma}}
.
∎

From the definition, one verifies directly that if
\mathscr{F}
has Property
2.42
, then so does
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}
. Then, Lemma
2.43
implies

Corollary 2.52
.

If
\mathfrak{b}
is a weak bounding cochain, and
\mathscr{F}
has Property
2.42
, then the deformed open-closed DMFT
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}
induces an curved open-closed DMFT with vanishing curvature.

2.5.
Deforming the differential

In this section we show that a curved open-closed DMFT
\mathscr{F}\colon\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}_{r}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
with vanishing curvature induces an open-closed DMFT.

To this end, recall that a
Maurer–Cartan element

b
for an
A_{\infty}
category
\mathscr{C}
is the data of a Maurer–Cartan element
b_{y}
for each
A_{\infty}
algebra
\mathscr{C}(y,y)
. Maurer–Cartan elements are the same as bounding cochains, but we will use different terminology here to avoid confusing the Maurer–Cartan elements employed here with the bounding cochains for a Lagrangian employed in the previous section.

Given a Maurer–Cartan element
b
, the deformed category
\mathscr{C}^{b}
has the same objects and morphisms as
\mathscr{C}
, but a deformed
A_{\infty}
structure defined by the analogous formula to Equation (
2.24
), where one replaces
b
by
b_{y}
for appropriate
y
. We will only require the definition in the case where
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{\infty}
is the
A_{\infty}
category associated to a dg category.

Definition 2.53
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a dg category, and
b
a Maurer–Cartan element for
\mathscr{C}_{\infty}
. Then
\mathscr{C}^{b}
is the
dg
category associated to the
A_{\infty}
category
\mathscr{C}_{\infty}^{b}
. It has the same objects and morphisms and compositions as
\mathscr{C}
but is equipped with the deformed differential

d^{b}(\alpha)=d(\alpha)+b_{y^{\prime}}\circ\alpha-(-1)^{|\alpha|}\alpha\circ b_{y}.

for
\alpha\in\mathscr{C}(y,y^{\prime})
.

We will from now on drop the subscript when passing from dg categories to
A_{\infty}
categories and simply assume that all
A_{\infty}
categories in this subsection have
\mu^{k}=0
for
k\geq 3
.

Lemma 2.54
.

If
\mathscr{F}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
is an
A_{\infty}
functor and
b
a Maurer–Cartan element for
\mathscr{C}
, then for each
y
, the element

(2.32)

b^{\mathscr{F}}_{y}\;\coloneqq\;\sum_{d\geq 1}\,\mathscr{F}^{d}_{(b_{y},\dots,b_{y})}

is a Maurer–Cartan element for
\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}(\mathscr{F}(y),\mathscr{F}(y))
and the maps

(2.33)

\mathscr{F}^{b}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{1}}\;\coloneqq\;\sum\mathscr{F}_{(b,\dots,b,\sigma_{d},b,\dots,b,\sigma_{1},b,\dots,b)},

where we have just written
b
instead of
b_{y}
for appropriate
y
, define an
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{C}^{b}\to\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
, where
\mathscr{F}^{b}(y)
carries the differential deformed by
b^{\mathscr{F}}_{y}
. If
\mathscr{F}
is unital, so is
\mathscr{F}^{b}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{F}
is an
A_{\infty}
functor, we have
\mu^{1}_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}(b^{\mathscr{F}}_{y})+\mu^{2}_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}(b^{\mathscr{F}}_{y},b^{\mathscr{F}}_{y})\;=\;\mathscr{F}_{\mu^{1}_{\mathscr{C}}(b_{y})+\mu^{2}_{\mathscr{C}}(b_{y},b_{y})}\;=\;0
.
∎

Remark 2.55
.

A functor
\mathscr{F}\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
is also known as a left
A_{\infty}
module. We have chosen to state everything in terms of functors since it simplifies the characterisation of certain properties and is closer to Costello’s set-up. The above example shows that given a left
A_{\infty}
module over
\mathscr{C}
, a Maurer–Cartan element
b
for
\mathscr{C}
induces a left
A_{\infty}
module over
\mathscr{C}^{b}
.

In general, if
\mathscr{C}
is symmetric monoidal,
\mathscr{C}^{b}
might no longer be, since the differential is changed. To ensure this, we can only deform by certain Maurer–Cartan elements of
\mathscr{C}
.

Lemma 2.56
.

We call a Maurer–Cartan element
b
for
\mathscr{C}

symmetric monoidal
if

(2.34)

b_{x\otimes y}\;=\;b_{x}\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}\text{id}_{y}+\text{id}_{x}\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}b_{y}

for any
x,y\in\text{obj}(\mathscr{C})
. In this case the symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathscr{C}
induces one on
\mathscr{C}^{b}
.

Proof.

Since

(\alpha^{\prime}\otimes\beta^{\prime})\circ(\alpha\otimes\beta)=(-1)^{|\alpha^{\prime}||\beta|}(\alpha\circ\alpha^{\prime})\otimes(\beta^{\prime}\circ\beta)

the claim follows from a straightforward computation.
∎

Suppose now
T
is a pre-natural transformation between two
A_{\infty}
functors
\mathscr{F}
,
\mathscr{G}\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
. If
b
is a Maurer–Cartan element for
\mathscr{C}
, then the maps

(2.35)

T^{b}(\underline{\sigma}):=\sum T(b,\dots,b,\sigma_{d},b,\dots,b,\sigma_{1},b,\dots,b)

define a pre-natural transformation between
\mathscr{F}^{b}
and
\mathscr{G}^{b}
, where we have omitted subscripts on the Maurer–Cartan elements for simplicity.

Lemma 2.57
.

If
T
is a natural transformation from
\mathscr{F}
to
\mathscr{G}
, then
T^{b}
is one from
\mathscr{F}^{b}
to
\mathscr{G}^{b}
.

Proof.

Applying the equation
\mu_{\mathscr{Q}}^{1}(T)=0
to tuples of the form
\underline{\sigma}^{b}=(b,\dots,b,\sigma_{d},b,\dots,b,\sigma_{1},b,\dots,b)
. The proof is then an easy verification of signs. We will sketch it here, omitting the signs. First observe that

\sum_{i}\mathscr{G}((\underline{\sigma}^{b})^{>i})\circ T((\underline{\sigma}^{b})^{\leq i})=\mathscr{G}(b,\dots,b)\circ T^{b}(\underline{\sigma})+\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\mathscr{G}^{b}(\underline{\sigma}^{>i})\circ T^{b}(\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}).

Similarly

\sum_{i}T((\underline{\sigma}^{b})^{>i})\circ\mathscr{F}((\underline{\sigma}^{b})^{\leq i})=T^{b}(\underline{\sigma})\circ\mathscr{F}(b,\dots,b)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}T^{b}(\underline{\sigma}^{>i})\circ\mathscr{F}^{b}(\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}).

Next observe that

[\partial,T(\underline{\sigma}^{b})]=[\partial^{b},T^{b}(\underline{\sigma})]-\mathscr{G}(b,\dots,b)\circ T^{b}(\underline{\sigma})+(-1)^{|T|}T^{b}(\underline{\sigma})\circ\mathscr{F}(b,\dots,b).

The terms involving
\mathscr{F}(b,\dots,b)
and
\mathscr{G}(b,\dots,b)
thus cancel.
Next, we find

\sum_{i}T(\partial_{i}\underline{\sigma}^{b})=\sum_{i}T^{b}(\partial_{i}\underline{\sigma})+\sum T(b,\dots,b,\sigma_{d},\dots,\partial b,\dots,\sigma_{1},b,\dots,b),

where the
\partial b
could be anywhere (including to the left and right of
\sigma_{d}
,
\sigma_{1}
). Similarly

\sum_{i}T(\star_{i}\underline{\sigma}^{b})=\sum_{i}T^{b}(\star_{i}\underline{\sigma})+\sum T(\dots\sigma_{d},\dots,b\circ b,\dots,\sigma_{1},\dots)+\sum T(\dots,\sigma_{d},\dots,[b,\sigma_{i}],\dots,\sigma_{1},\dots).

The Maurer–Cartan equation for
b
shows that

\sum T(b,\dots,b,\sigma_{d},\dots,\partial b,\dots,\sigma_{1},b,\dots,b)+\sum T(\dots\sigma_{d},\dots,b\circ b,\dots,\sigma_{1},\dots)=0.

Finally,

T^{b}(\partial^{b}_{i}\underline{\sigma})=T^{b}(\partial_{i}\underline{\sigma})+\sum T(b,\dots,\sigma_{d},\dots,[b,\sigma_{i}],\dots,\sigma_{1},\dots,b)

by definition of the differential on
\mathscr{C}^{b}
.
∎

Definition 2.58
.

We say a pre-natural transformation
T
is
compatible
with the Maurer–Cartan element
b
if
(T^{b})^{0}=T^{0}
.

Lemma 2.59
.

Let
\mathscr{F}\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
, be a monoidal
A_{\infty}
functor and
b
be a symmetric monoidal Maurer–Cartan element for
\mathscr{C}
. Then, the induced functor
\mathscr{F}^{b}\colon\mathscr{C}^{b}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
is a monoidal
A_{\infty}
functor. If
\mathscr{F}
is unital symmetric monoidal, then
\mathscr{F}^{b}
is unital symmetric monoidal. If
\mathscr{F}
is h-split, so is
\mathscr{F}^{b}
.

Proof.

Let
R
and
L
be the two natural transformations giving the symmetric monoidal structure of
\mathscr{F}
. We only discuss the case of
R
as the other case is analogous.
Abbreviate
\mathscr{G}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{F})
, defined in (
2.14
), and
\mathscr{H}:=\mathscr{F}\circ\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}
.
Recalling the definition of
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}
from §
2.2
, we define the Maurer–Cartan element
\widetilde{b}
by
\widetilde{b}_{(x,y)}\coloneqq b_{x}\otimes\text{id}_{y}+\text{id}_{x}\otimes b_{y}
. Then,

\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}(\widetilde{b}_{(x,y)})\;=\;b_{x\otimes y},

which implies
\mathscr{H}^{\widetilde{b}}=\mathscr{F}^{b}\circ\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}
. Due to the unitality of
\mathscr{F}
, we have
\mathscr{G}^{\widetilde{b}}=\otimes_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}\circ(\mathscr{F}^{b}\otimes\mathscr{F}^{b})
and
R^{\widetilde{b}}
defines the desired first part of the symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathscr{F}^{b}
by Lemma
2.57
. The same argument shows that
L^{\widetilde{b}}
yields the other part of the symmetric monoidal structures. Since
\widetilde{b}
is symmetric,
\mathscr{F}^{b}
is symmetric monoidal.
Unitality follows from the fact that
\widetilde{b}_{(x,y)}
is a sum of tensor products containing the identity. Finally, since
b
and thus also
\widetilde{b}
have positive valuation, one can use spectral sequences to prove that
(R^{b})^{0}
and
(L^{b})^{0}
are still quasi-isomorphisms if
R^{0}
and
L^{0}
are.
∎

We now specialise the discussion to our geometric setup of the previous subsections. Thus,
\mathscr{C}=\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}
is the symmetric monoidal dg category of Definition
2.10
.
For any
y\in\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
we write
\widehat{D}_{1}\in\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}(y,y)
for the morphism of the form

\widehat{D}_{1}=\sum_{y_{1},y_{2}}\sigma_{\text{id}}^{y_{1}}\times D_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}}^{y_{2}},

summing over all possible partitions of
y\setminus\{\kappa\}
for arbitrary
\kappa\in y_{o}
with open points in
y_{1}
ordered before those in
y_{2}
.

Lemma 2.60
.

\widehat{D}_{1}
is a symmetric monoidal Maurer–Cartan element for the category
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}
of Definition
2.38
and there exists a canonical inclusion

\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}\;\hookrightarrow\;(\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}_{r})^{\widehat{D}_{1}}

of symmetric monoidal dg categories.

Proof.

The first claim from the fact that
\mu^{1}_{oc}(\widehat{D}_{1})=\widehat{D}_{1}\circ\widehat{D}_{1}=-\mu^{2}_{oc}(\widehat{D}_{1},\widehat{D}_{1})
. For the second we observe that by Definition
2.53
, the differential of
(\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}_{r})^{\widehat{D}_{1}}
is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{oc}^{\widehat{D_{1}}}(\sigma)\;

\displaystyle=\;\partial_{oc}(\sigma)+\widehat{D}_{1}\circ\sigma-(-1)^{|\sigma|}\sigma\circ\widehat{D}_{1}

\displaystyle=\;\partial\sigma,

where
\partial\sigma
denotes the ordinary boundary operator on the singular chain
\sigma
.
∎

We can thus consider the deformed category
(\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}_{r})^{\widehat{D_{1}}}
, and give a curved open-closed DMFT
\mathscr{F}\colon\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}_{r}\to\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
with vanishing curvature, the deformed
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}^{\widehat{D_{1}}}\colon(\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}_{r})^{\widehat{D_{1}}}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
. Since
\widehat{D}_{1}
is symmetric monidal, Lemma
2.59
implies

Corollary 2.61
.

If
\mathscr{F}
is an
r
-dimensional curved open-closed DMFT with vanishing curvature, the
A_{\infty}
functor
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
defined by the composite

\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}\;\hookrightarrow\;(\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{unc}_{r})^{\widehat{D_{1}}}\;\xrightarrow{\mathscr{F}^{\widehat{D}_{1}}}\;\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}

and equipped with the deformed symmetric monoidal structure is an open-closed DMFT.∎

Note that
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
and
\mathscr{F}
do not agree on objects. For example, if
\mathscr{F}(0,1)=(V,d)
is the open sector, then
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(0,1)=(V,\widetilde{d})
where the deformed differential is given by
\widetilde{d}=d+\sum_{i\geq 1}\mathscr{F}^{i}_{\widehat{D_{1}},\dots,\widehat{D_{1}}}.

Corollary 2.62
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be a curved open-closed DMFT with Property
2.42
. If
\mathscr{F}
admits a weak bounding cochain
\mathfrak{b}
, then the deformed open-closed DMFT
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}
induces an open-closed DMFT
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{b}\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}\rightarrow\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}
. If
\mathscr{F}
is strict, semi-strict or operadic, then so is
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{b}
.

Proof.

The first claim follows from Corollary
2.61
and Lemma
2.43
, while the last claim follows directly from the definition of strict, semi-strict or operadic because
\mathscr{F}^{b}_{\underline{\sigma}}
is defined in terms of sequences
\underline{\sigma}^{\prime}
that have length at least the length of
\underline{\sigma}
and we are only adding
\widehat{D_{1}}
.
∎

2.6.
Homotopies

A natural transformation
T
between
A_{\infty}
functors
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
is called a
homotopy
if
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
agree on objects,
T^{0}=0
and
\mu_{\mathscr{Q}}^{1}(T)^{d}=\mathscr{F}^{d}-\mathscr{G}^{d}
for
d\geq 1
.
In particular, a homotopy necessarily has degree
0
.

Definition 2.63
.

A
homotopy

T
between curved open-closed DMFTs
\mathscr{F}^{0}
and
\mathscr{F}^{1}
is a homotopy
T
of
A_{\infty}
functors so that two compositions of the square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}\circ(\mathscr{F}^{0}\otimes\mathscr{F}^{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}^{0}\circ\otimes_{\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}\circ(\mathscr{F}^{1}\otimes\mathscr{F}^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}^{1}\circ\otimes_{\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}\circ(T\otimes T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\circ\otimes_{\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{1}}

differ by the boundary
\mu^{1}(P)
of natural transformation
P
from
\otimes_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}\circ(\mathscr{F}^{0}\otimes\mathscr{F}^{0})
to
\mathscr{F}^{1}\circ\otimes_{\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}}
, and similarly with
R^{(i)}
replaced by
L^{(i)}
and
P
replaced by
P\circ S
, where
S
is the braiding of
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
.

However, if
T
is a homotopy, the deformed natural transformation
T^{b}
need not satisfy
(T^{b})^{0}=0
. Thus,
T^{b}
might not be a homotopy, which is the motivation behind Definition
2.58
. In particular, we have the following observation.

Lemma 2.64
.

Suppose
T
is a homotopy between curved open-closed DMFTs
\mathscr{F}^{0}
and
\mathscr{F}^{1}
with vanishing curvature. If
T
is compatbile with (the Maurer–Cartan element)
\widehat{D_{1}}
, then
T^{\widehat{D_{1}}}
is a homotopy between the open-closed DMFTs
\mathscr{F}^{0,\widehat{D_{1}}}
and
\mathscr{F}^{1,\widehat{D_{1}}}
.∎

Recall that any semi-strict curved open-closed DMFT defines a curved
A_{\infty}
algebra by Lemma
2.40
.

Lemma 2.65
.

A homotopy between two semi-strict curved open-closed DMFTs induces an isomorphism of the induced curved
A_{\infty}
algebras.

The proof is similar to Fukaya’s proof that pseudo-isotopies of
A_{\infty}
algebras induce
A_{\infty}
isomorphisms,
[
28
, Theorem 8.2]
. Homotopies of DMFTs can thus be seen as a weakening of the notion of pseudo-isotopy.

Proof.

Following Fukaya
[
28
, Section 9]
, define for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
the set
\text{Trees}(k)
of (isomorphic classes of) all rooted trees with the following properties: the root vertex
v_{0}
has exactly one output, and the tree has
k
open inputs. Every vertex
v
is labeled with energy
\beta(v)\in[0,\infty)
with
\beta(v)=0
only if
v
has at least three edges. We additionally equip our trees with a choice of total order on the vertices which respects the partial order defined by the tree, where
v^{\prime}<v
if
v^{\prime}
lies on the shortest path from
v
to
v_{0}
. To any
\Gamma\in\text{Trees}(k)
with
d
vertices associate the tuple
\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)=(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{1})
, where
\sigma_{d}
is the chain
D_{|v_{0}|-1}
, and the other
\sigma_{i}
consists of a disjoint union of
D_{|v_{i}|-1}
and
\sigma_{\text{id}}
as required, so that the sequence
\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)
is a composable tuple from
k
open inputs to one open output.

Given a homotopy
T
between
\mathscr{F}^{0}
and
\mathscr{F}^{1}
, denote the associated
A_{\infty}
algebras by
\mathscr{A}_{i}
, noting that their underlying
\Lambda
-modules are the same. Then define the maps
f^{k}\colon\mathscr{A}_{0}^{\otimes k}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}_{1}

by

(2.36)

f^{k}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k})\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma\in Trees(k)\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\epsilon(\alpha)}T_{\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)}(\alpha_{1}\times\dots\times\alpha_{k}),

where
\epsilon(\alpha)
was defined in (
2.21
) and
\alpha\times\beta=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}R^{0}(\alpha\otimes\beta)
.

We claim that
f=(f^{k})_{k\geq 1}
defines an
A_{\infty}
algebra homomorphism. First observe that the terms from the homotopy equation (
2.9
) with
T_{\partial_{i}\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)}
and
T_{\ast_{i}\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)}
cancel with each other after summing over all trees. We then separate out the cases where the length of
\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)
is
1
and where it is
>1
.

If its length is
1
, we have
\sigma(\Gamma)=D_{k}
. First, observe that
f^{1}
in energy zero is given by
T_{\sigma_{\text{id}}}=\text{id}
. Ignoring the terms that cancel, Equation (
2.9
) implies (omitting signs)

[d,T_{D_{k}}]\,+\,T_{\partial_{oc}D_{k}}\;=\;\mathscr{F}^{0}_{D_{k}}\,-\,\mathscr{F}^{1}_{D_{k}}.

This gives rise to the terms
\mu_{\mathscr{A}_{1}}^{1,0}\circ f^{k}
,
f^{k}\circ\mu_{\mathscr{A}_{0}}^{1,0}
,
\mu_{\mathscr{A}_{1}}^{k}\circ(f^{1,0}\otimes\dots\otimes f^{1,0})
and
f^{1,0}\circ\mu_{\mathscr{A}_{0}}^{k}
in the
A_{\infty}
homomorphism equation.
For sequences
\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)
of length
>1
, note that as
\mathscr{F}^{i}
is semi-strict,
\mathscr{F}^{i}_{\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)}=0
. Thus, ignoring signs and cancelling terms,

[d,T_{\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)}]\;=\;\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma_{d}}\circ T_{\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)^{<d}}\,+\,T_{\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)^{>1}}\circ\mathscr{F}^{0}_{\sigma_{1}}.

The term on the left-hand side contributes to
\mu_{\mathscr{A}_{1}}^{1,0}\circ f^{k}
and
f^{k}\circ\mu_{\mathscr{A}_{0}}^{1,0}
. For the term on the right-hand side, note that
\sigma_{d}=D_{|v_{0}|-1}
, so that
\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)^{<d}
consists of
|v_{0}|-1
trees, and

\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma_{d}}\circ T_{\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)^{<d}}\;=\;\mu_{\mathscr{A}_{1}}^{|v_{0}|-1}(f^{k_{1}}(\dots),\dots,f^{k_{|v_{0}|-1}}(\dots)).

For the other term, note that
\sigma_{1}
is a product of
D_{r}
and identity simplices
\sigma_{\text{id}}
, so that

T_{\underline{\sigma}(\Gamma)^{>1}}\circ\mathscr{F}^{0}_{\sigma_{1}}\;=\;f^{k-r}(\dots,\mu_{\mathscr{A}_{0}}^{r}(\dots),\dots),

as required.
∎

3.
A Deligne–Mumford field theory from moduli spaces of stable maps

Suppose
(X,\omega)
is a closed symplectic manifold and
L\subset X
an embedded Lagrangian submanifold equipped with a relative spin structure
(V,\mathfrak{s})
. Define the Novikov ring

(3.1)

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}Q^{\beta_{i}}\,\big|\,a_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\;\beta_{i}\in H_{2}(X,L),\;\lim_{i\to\infty}\omega(\beta_{i})=\infty\right\}

This subsection is devoted to proving Theorem
A
, using the coefficient ring
\Lambda
endowed with the valuation
\nu\colon\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\nu\Bigl(\sum\limits_{j}{a_{j}\,Q^{\beta_{j}}}\Bigr)=\inf\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\\
a_{j}\neq 0\end{subarray}}{\omega(\beta_{j})}.

We first construct a curved open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory associated to a relatively spin Lagrangian submanifold.

Theorem 3.1
.

For any choice of
\omega
-tame almost complex structure
J
, of cubical cobordisms
\{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\}_{\Gamma}
and compatible system of Thom forms
\eta_{\Gamma}
, there exists a semi-strict, operadic curved open-closed Deligne–Mumford field theory

\mathscr{F}_{L}\colon\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}\to\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}

defined using the open-closed Deligne-Mumford moduli spaces of
(X,L,J)
.

Cubical cobordisms and Thom systems were defined in
[
35
]
and are recalled in the next subsection.
Applying the discussion of weak bounding cochains from §
2.4
in the context of curved open-closed DMFTs yields the second part of Theorem
A
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Suppose
L
is equipped with a weak bounding cochain
\mathfrak{b}
for the unital
A_{\infty}
algebra
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{F}}
. Then,
L
admits a semi-strict, operadic
n
-dimensional open-closed DMFT whose associated cohomological field theory recovers the Gromov–Witten theory of
(X,\omega)
.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.2
and Lemma
3.42
, which shows that
\mathscr{F}_{L}
has Property
2.42
, the curved open-closed DMFT
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}_{L}
has vanishing curvature. Thus, the claim follows from Corollary
2.62
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Somewhat surprisingly, all operations of
\mathscr{F}_{L}
are compatible with
D_{1}
except for the open co-pairing. Concretely,
\mathscr{F}_{\underline{\sigma}}=0
whenever
d\geq 2
,
\sigma_{i}=D_{1}
for some
i
and no other
\sigma_{j}
represents the co-pairing. Given a weak bounding cochain
\mathfrak{b}
, it follows from Equation (
2.33
) that the functor
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}
only differs from
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}
for sequences
\underline{\sigma}
involving the co-pairing. The higher homotopies
\mathscr{F}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}_{D_{1},\dots,D_{1}}
also vanish, so the differential on
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{\,\mathfrak{b}}(0,1)
is given by
d+\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}
, which is exactly the Floer differential.

3.1.
A suitable chain model

In order to achieve semi-strictness, we construct a special chain model for the real-valued singular homology on the moduli spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}
. To this end, we consider ‘fattened’ versions of the moduli spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;y,y^{\prime}}
, requiring some preliminary definitions.
Recall from
[
35
, §3]
that by a
stable graph
we mean the combinatorial data underlying a possibly disconnected and nodal Riemann surface with boundary and marked points. In this paper, this comes with the additional data of an ‘orientation’ of each half-edge, denoting whether they are incoming or outgoing.

We fix for each isomorphism class of stable graph a representative and we will work with that throughout the remainder of the paper.

Each stable graph
\Gamma
determines the product moduli space

(3.2)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}=\prod_{v\in V^{s}(\Gamma)}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}_{v}}.

We have the relation

\partial_{\Gamma}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}\cong\frac{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}{\text{Aut}(\Gamma)}.

We define the auxiliary moduli spaces

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}:=I^{E(\Gamma)}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}.

They will be used to define a suitable chain model for the singular homology of the moduli spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}
. Define

(3.3)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}^{+}\coloneqq\bigsqcup\limits_{\Gamma\to\mathsf{a}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}.

While, we do not glue the moduli spaces together, this construction is similar to the outer-collaring of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}
as used in
[
27
, Chapter 17]
and
[
8
, §3.3.1]
, considering the moduli spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
as spikes on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}
. Note that we have additional ‘spikes’ because we consider our half-edges to be oriented. Readers who will find Construction
3.5
too algebraic can equally well think of the moduli spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}^{+}
as actually glued along their common boundary strata and of the chains we take as stratified chains on that ‘spiky moduli space’. To help that intuition, we include the following picture.

Remark 3.4
.

Figure 1.
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{+}_{0,0;(0,0),(4,0)}

The usual moduli space of spheres with
4
marked points
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g=0,k=4}\cong\mathbb{CP}^{1}
has 3 ‘special points’ where the configuration can be viewed as two spheres clutched at a single node. In the usual outer collaring, one would add one spike (intervals) to each special point. Since we have to keep track of the orientations of the half-edges, we instead have to add
4
spikes to each special point, making a total of
12
spikes for the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{+}_{0,0;(0,0),(4,0)}
as drawn above.

We extend the forgetful and clutching maps on the moduli spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}^{+}
as follows.

(E1)

The clutching
\Gamma
of two graphs
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
induces an embedding

(3.4)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}.

given by the identity on the moduli spaes
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma_{i}}
and by the canonical inclusion map

(3.5)

I^{E(\Gamma_{1})}\times I^{E(\Gamma_{2})}\hookrightarrow I^{E(\Gamma)}

induced by the embedding
E(\Gamma_{1})\sqcup E(\Gamma_{2})\hookrightarrow E(\Gamma)
onto the boundary stratum
\{t_{e}=1\}_{e\in E(\psi)}
.

(E2)

The inclusion
\rho\colon\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h+1;k-1,\ell}
of the stratum with a boundary node of type (E) defines an embedding
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\rho(\Gamma)}
. Moreover
E(\Gamma)
embeds canonically into
E(\rho(\Gamma))
so we obtain a smooth real analytic map

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\rho(\Gamma)}

by including into
\{t_{e}=1\}
, where
e
is the (interior) edge connecting the ‘collapsed boundary’ vertex to the rest of the graph.

(E3)

For a dual graph
\Gamma
, let
\pi(\Gamma)
denote the (possibly stabilised) dual graph obtained by forgetting the respective marked point. Then, the map

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\,\to\,\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\pi(\Gamma)}

is given by the natural map
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi(\Gamma)}
in the second factor, and by the following map
I^{E(\Gamma)}\rightarrow I^{E(\pi(\Gamma))}
between cubes: if
T\subset\Gamma
is a maximal subgraph that is contracted, then
T
is either attached by a single edge
e
to the rest of
\Gamma
or by two edges
e_{1},e_{2}
. In the first case, we forget
I^{E(T)}\times I^{\{e\}}
and in the second case we map
t\in I^{E(T)}\times I^{\{e_{1},e_{2}\}}
to

t_{\bar{e}}:=1-\prod\limits_{e\in E(T)\cup\{e_{1},e_{2}\}}{(1-t_{e})}

where
\bar{e}
is the edge onto which
T
is contracted as described pictorially by

Compare this map with the usual local expression of the forgetful map given by
(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto z_{1}z_{2}
. In the map above, we have to compose with
1-t
because we use outer-collarings and not inner-collarings.

We can now construct the chain model we use for the definition of
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
and ultimately in §
3
.

Construction 3.5
.

Fix a representative
\Gamma
for each isomorphism class
[\Gamma]
of open-closed stable graph as in
[
35
, Definition 4.13]
. Define the category
\mathscr{S}
to have

•

objects
(\Gamma,E,f_{\Gamma})
, where
\Gamma
is a stable graph and
E\subset E(\Gamma)
is a subset of edges,

•

morphisms from
(\Gamma,E)\to(\Gamma^{\prime},E^{\prime})
given by (orientation-preserving) morphisms
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
in the sense of
[
35
, Definition 4.14]
so that any edge contracted by
f
lies in
E
and
E^{\prime}
consists of the images of those edges in
E
that are not contracted by
f
.

Given
\Gamma
, we let
\mathsf{a}_{\Gamma}
be the topological data of the unique corolla (i.e. graph without edges)
\Gamma
contracts to. We denote said by corolla by
\mathsf{a}
as well and let
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}
be the full subcategory on objects
(\Gamma,E)
with
\mathsf{a}_{\Gamma}=\mathsf{a}
.
Define the functor
\mathscr{C}\colon\mathscr{S}\to\text{Ch}_{\mathbb{R}}
by setting

\mathscr{C}(\Gamma,E):=C^{an}_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{E}=0\}})

and letting
\mathscr{C}(f)=(\text{id}_{I^{E^{\prime}}}\times\psi^{f})_{*}
be the pushforward by the identity and the associated clutching map.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any
\mathsf{a}
we have
H_{*}(\text{colim}_{\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}}\mathscr{C})\cong H_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}};\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Define the ‘reduced’ functor
\overline{\mathscr{C}}\colon\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}\to\text{Ch}_{\mathbb{R}}
by
\overline{\mathscr{C}}(\Gamma,E)=\mathscr{C}(\Gamma,E(\Gamma))
and
\overline{\mathscr{C}}(f)=\psi^{f}_{*}
. Then, there exist canonical natural transformations
\eta\colon\overline{\mathscr{C}}\to\mathscr{C}|_{\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}}
and
\xi\colon\mathscr{C}|_{\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}
, induced by the inclusions
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{E(\Gamma)}=0\}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{E}=0\}}
and the canonical retraction
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{E}=0\}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{E(\Gamma)}=0\}}
respectively. These are natural homotopy inverses in the sense that the homotopies are compatible with the morphisms of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}
. In particular, this shows that

(3.6)

H_{*}(\text{colim}_{\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}}\mathscr{C})\cong H_{*}(\text{colim}\,\overline{\mathscr{C}}).

Let
\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathsf{a}}\subset\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}
be the full subcategory on objects
(\Gamma,E(\Gamma))
. Note that
\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathsf{a}}
has a terminal object given by
\mathsf{a}
itself. Moreover, there exists a right inverse
R\colon\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}\to\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathsf{a}}
the inclusion
\iota
, given by
R(\Gamma,E)=(\Gamma,E(\Gamma))
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\circ\iota\circ R=\overline{\mathcal{C}}
, it follows that

H_{*}(\text{colim}\,\overline{\mathscr{C}})\cong H_{*}(\text{colim}_{\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathsf{a}}}\,\overline{\mathscr{C}})\cong H_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}};\mathbb{R}),

where the last isomorphism is due to the fact that
\mathsf{a}
is terminal in
\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathsf{a}}
and by Lemma
2.9
.
∎

Definition 3.7
.

We define the symmetric monoidal category
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
enriched in cochain complexes to have the same objects as
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
with morphisms given by the cochain complexes

\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}^{*}(y,y^{\prime})=\bigoplus\limits_{\mathsf{a}\to y,y^{\prime}}\text{colim}_{\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}}\mathscr{C}_{-*},

where
\mathscr{C}
is the functor defined in Construction
3.5
. The composition maps

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\otimes\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}(y,y^{\prime})\to\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}(y,y^{\prime\prime})

are induced by the composition maps (
E1
)- (
E3
) and the Alexander--Whitney choice of Eilenberg--Zilber morphism (if in the stable range).
1
1

1

Given the explicit description of this choice of Eilenberg–Zilber morphism, one sees easily that analyticity is preserved.

This is well-defined as the clutching maps are local embeddings. Our choice of Eilenberg–Zilber morphism ensures that the compositions are strictly associative.

The category
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
has the same objects as
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
with morphisms given by

(3.7)

\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{*}(y,y^{\prime})\coloneqq\bigoplus\limits_{\mathsf{a}\to y,y^{\prime}}\text{colim}_{\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{a}}}\mathscr{C}_{r}^{*}

for
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, where

(3.8)

\mathscr{C}_{r}^{*}(\Gamma,E)\coloneqq C^{an}_{-*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma};{\det}^{\otimes r})

with the ‘signed’ differential as above. The argument of Lemma
3.6
carries over verbatim, showing that this recovers (cohomologically graded) singular homology with the local coefficients in
{\text{det}}^{\otimes r}
. Thus, a simplex
\widetilde{\sigma}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
is given by a simplex
\sigma\colon\Delta^{m}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime})
together with a trivialisation of

(3.9)

\det(\sigma)\coloneqq\sigma^{*}{\det}^{\otimes r}[r\chi+rd|y^{\prime}_{c}|+r|y^{\prime}_{o}|].

We now define the pull-back
\pi^{*}:\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{*}(y,y^{\prime})\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}^{*}(y\sqcup*,y^{\prime})
on this modified chain complex. Suppose
\sigma\colon\Delta^{k}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
is a simplex and let
\Gamma^{+}
be a stable graph which contracts to
\Gamma
after forgetting a last incoming boundary marked point. Then, by taking an analytic triangulation of the resolution of the fibre product
\Delta^{k}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{+}}
, one obtains a chain
\pi^{*}_{\Gamma^{+}}\sigma
. The proof of Lemma
2.14
carries over verbatim to show this is well-defined.

Definition 3.8
.

Define the chain
\pi^{*}\sigma
by
\pi^{*}\sigma:=\sum_{\Gamma^{+}\to\Gamma}\pi^{*}_{\Gamma^{+}}\sigma,
where we sum over all stable graphs
\Gamma^{+}
that map to
\Gamma
when forgetting the last incoming boundary marked point.

The proof of Lemma
2.19
shows that
\pi^{*}\sigma
has Properties (
1
)-(
4
). Thus, we can define the symmetric monoidal dg category
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
as in Definition
2.10
using the dg category
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{r}
of Definition
3.7
instead of
C_{*}^{an}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})
.

Lemma 3.9
.

There exist a symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
quasi-equivalence
C_{-*}^{an}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
.

Proof.

Define the functor
\mathscr{I}:C_{-*}^{an}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc})\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}
by
\mathscr{I}^{1}(\sigma)=\sigma
, while for
d\geq 2
, if
\underline{\sigma}
contains any unstable chains, set
\mathscr{I}^{d}(\underline{\sigma})=0
. Otherwise, if
\underline{\sigma}
only contains stable chains, define

\mathscr{I}^{d}(\underline{\sigma}):=\sigma_{d}\times\dots\times\sigma_{1}\times I^{d-1}\in C^{an}_{-*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma};{\det}^{\otimes r})

where
\Gamma
denotes the stable graph obtained by concatenating all
\sigma
and
I^{d-1}\hookrightarrow I^{E(\Gamma)}
is the (diagonal) inclusion of the edges separating the
\sigma_{i}
.
∎

3.2.
Correspondences

We introduce the notion of a pull-push operation obtained from a suitable correspondence and define the correspondences required for our curved open-closed DMFT.

Definition 3.10
.

To a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}:=X\xleftarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
with
g
a submersion and
Y
and
Z
oriented (respectively
g
co-oriented) orbifolds, we associate the linear operation

(3.10)

\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}}\colon\Omega^{*}_{c}(X)\to\Omega^{*}(Z):\alpha\mapsto g_{*}f^{*}\alpha,

If we have instead
Y
an oriented global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{E},s)
with corners equipped with a Thom form
\tau
, we can define
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}}
by

\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\alpha)=g_{*}(f^{*}(\alpha)\wedge s^{*}\tau)

In Theorem
3.15
, Proposition
3.16
and Lemma
3.19
, we construct the correspondences required for
\mathscr{F}_{L}
. To show compatibility, we require the following notion of a morphism of correspondences.

Definition 3.11
.

A
morphism

\pi\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}

of correspondences
is a morphism of global Kuranishi charts
\pi\colon\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
so that
\mathcal{E}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
\tau=\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\tau^{\prime}
, while
f^{\prime}\circ\pi=f
and
g^{\prime}\circ f=g
.

Definition 3.12
.

We call a correspondence
X\leftarrow(\mathcal{K},\tau)\to Y

degenerate
if it admits a morphism to a correspondence of strictly lower virtual dimension.

Definition 3.13
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{C}=X\xleftarrow{f}(\mathcal{K},\tau)\xrightarrow{g}Z
.
If
\vartheta\colon Z\to Z^{\prime}
is a submersion, we define the
pushforward

\vartheta_{*}\mathfrak{C}
to be the correspondence
X\xleftarrow{f}(\mathcal{K},\tau)\xrightarrow{\vartheta g}Z^{\prime}
. Similarly, if
\theta\colon X^{\prime}\to X
is a submersion, we define the pullback
\theta^{*}\mathfrak{C}
to be
X^{\prime}\xleftarrow{f}(X^{\prime}\times_{X}\mathcal{K},\tau)\xrightarrow{g}Z
.

We observe the following properties of these operations. They make the proofs of the functoriality of
\mathscr{F}
cleaner and more geometric.

Proposition 3.14
.

The operations associated to correspondences satisfy the following properties.

(1)

We have
\mathfrak{q}_{\vartheta_{*}\mathfrak{C}}=\vartheta_{*}\circ\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{\theta^{*}\mathfrak{C}}=\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ\theta^{*}
for any correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
and any maps
\vartheta
and
\theta
as above.

(2)

Suppose
\mathfrak{C}_{i}:=X_{i}\xleftarrow{f_{i}}(\mathcal{K}_{i},\tau_{i})\xrightarrow{g_{i}}Z_{i}
is a correspondence for
i=\{0,1\}
and
Z_{0}=X_{1}
. Then, the composition
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\circ\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}_{0}}
is the operation associated to the fibre product correspondence

\mathfrak{C}_{1}\times_{Z_{0}}\mathfrak{C}_{0}\;\coloneqq\;X_{0}\xleftarrow{f_{0}p_{0}}\,(\mathcal{K}_{1}\times_{Z_{0}}\mathcal{K}_{0},\tau_{1}\times\tau_{0})\,\xrightarrow{g_{1}p_{1}}Z_{1},

where
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
are the canonical projections of the fibre product.

(3)

Suppose
\mathfrak{C}:=X\xleftarrow{f}(\mathcal{K},\tau)\xrightarrow{g}Z
is a correspondence and
\phi
an orientation-reversing diffeomorphism of
Y
so that
g\circ\phi=g
,
f\circ\phi=f
and
\widetilde{\phi}^{*}\tau=\tau
. Then
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}}=0
.

(4)

Given three correspondences
\mathfrak{C}_{0},\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
we have
(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}_{0}}\times\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}})\times\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}=\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}_{0}}\times(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\times\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}})
.

(5)

If a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
is degenerate, then
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}}
vanishes.

Proof.

These properties follow from a straightforward verification using the properties of the pushforward,
[
53
, Proposition 2.1]
.
∎

We recall here the cubical cobordisms constructed in
[
35
]
that are the main ingredient for achieving strict compatibility of (some of) the chain-level operations. A variation of the construction yields the correspondences that will be used to construct the higher homotopies of the curved open-closed DMFT.

Theorem 3.15

(
[
35
, Theorem 3.11]
)
.

Given a choice of unobstructed auxiliary datum
\alpha_{\mathsf{a}}
for each
\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
, there exists for each stable map graph
\Gamma
a
smooth
global Kuranishi chart
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}}
for
I^{E(\Gamma)}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X,L)
so that the following holds

(1)

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\mathsf{a}}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathsf{a}}
for any
\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
.

(2)

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}}
is orientable.

(3)

for any edge
e
of
\Gamma
we have a local smooth embedding

(3.11)

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim\,}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;(\pm 1)\,\partial_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{e}}}

of degree

d_{1}(\Gamma,\Gamma_{e})=|\{\phi\in\text{Aut}(\Gamma)\mid f\circ\phi=f\},

where
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma_{e}
is the underlying contraction of graphs.

(4)

The restriction to the other boundary face induces a smooth embedding

(3.12)

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}}|_{\{t_{e}=1\}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim\,}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;(\pm 1)\,\widetilde{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{1}}}\times_{Y}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{2}}}

where
\Gamma_{1}*_{e}\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma
and
Y=X
or
L
, depending on whether
e
is an interior or a boundary edge.

(5)

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}}
admits a smooth submersive lift
ev_{\Gamma}\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}}\to X^{k}\times L^{\ell}
of the evaluation map on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(X,L)
and the (local) embeddings of (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) intertwine these lifts.

Proposition 3.16
.

Given a system of (enhanced) cubical cobordisms
\{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\}_{\Gamma}
and a choice of Thom form
\eta_{\mathsf{a}}
for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}}
so that
s_{\mathsf{a}}^{*}\eta_{\mathsf{a}}
is compactly supported, there exists a system of Thom forms
\eta_{\Gamma}
for
\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma}
with the following properties.

1)

The pullback
s_{\Gamma}^{*}\eta_{\Gamma}
is compactly supported in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
.

2)

If
\Gamma=\mathsf{a}
, then
\eta_{\Gamma}=\eta_{\mathsf{a}}
.

3)

It is compatible with boundary restrictions. More precisely, for any edge
e
of
\Gamma
, we have that

(3.13)

\eta_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}\sim\eta_{\Gamma_{e}}|_{\partial_{\Gamma}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{e}}},

and

(3.14)

\eta_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=1\}}\sim\eta_{\Gamma_{1}}\times\eta_{\Gamma_{2}}|_{\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\times_{Y}\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{2}}}

if
e
separates
\Gamma
into
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
with
Y=X
or
L
depending on
e
, and

(3.15)

\eta_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=1\}}\sim\eta_{\Gamma\setminus e}|_{Y\times_{Y^{2}}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma\setminus e}}

if
e
is a non-separating node and
\Gamma\setminus e
is the graph given by cutting the edge
e
. Finally, if the edge corresponds to a collapsed boundary circle, then

(3.16)

\eta_{\Gamma}\mid_{\{t_{e}=1\}}\sim\eta_{\Gamma\setminus e}|_{L\times_{X}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma\setminus e}}.

While these results holds for all stable map graphs, we only consider graphs
\Gamma
whose unstable vertices (after forgetting the degree) are of type
(0,1)
, i.e. encode discs. In this case, we can construct relative orientations for
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\text{st}}}
.

Construction 3.17

(Orientation)
.

Suppose first
\Gamma
is a stable map graph whose complete contraction
\mathsf{a}_{\Gamma}
is stable after forgetting the degree. In this case, the forgetful map
\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\to I^{E(\Gamma^{\text{st}})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\text{st}}}
has a canonical relative orientation defined as follows. This map factors through the base space
\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
, and since
\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
has a canonically relative orientation by
[
35
, Lemma 2.29]
, it suffices to consider

\mathfrak{f}_{\,\Gamma}\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\to I^{E(\Gamma^{\text{st}})}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\text{st}}}

instead. Using that a disc bubble creates a boundary of codimension
1
, we obtain an open
\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
onto a collar neighbourhood of
\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, through which
\mathfrak{f}_{\Gamma}
factors.
For chains ending in leaves, there is a unique choice of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
For interior chains of unstable vertices, there are two choices of graphs
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
, which only differ in their degree distribution and which both yield the same graph
\widehat{\Gamma}
after contracting a single boundary edge.
Thus we have a homotopy commutative square of open immersions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}}

which covers

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\text{st}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\text{st}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}^{\text{st}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\text{st}}}}

of open embeddings, where the forgetful maps of the lower triangle have the same fibres. Thus, a relative orientation of
\mathfrak{f}_{\,\widehat{\Gamma}}
induces canonically a relative orientation of
\mathfrak{f}_{\Gamma}
. Proceeding in this fashion, we end up with a graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}
obtained by contractions from
\Gamma
and which is stable, whence
\mathfrak{f}_{\,\widetilde{\Gamma}}
has a canonical relative orientation, inducing therefore one on
\mathfrak{f}_{\,\Gamma}
.

If
\mathsf{a}_{\Gamma}
is not unstable, it is a disc with one outgoing marked point and at most one incoming marked point. In this case, we can use the same argument as in the previous case to reduce the problem to orienting
\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{\Gamma}}
, i.e., choosing a trivialisation of

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(D)\otimes\bigotimes_{i}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{z_{i}}\partial\mathbb{D})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\text{Aut}(\mathbb{D}))^{\vee}.

We use the orientation of
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(D)
induced by the relative spin structure; see
[
35
, §B]
or
[
18
]
. The remaining parts are canonically oriented.

These will be the correspondences used for the operations associated to stable simplices. For the operations associated to unstable simplices, note that by the nondegeneracy assumption in Definition
2.31
, we only have to specify a correspondence for the unique
0
-dimensional simplex. These correspondences are described in the following definition.

Definition 3.18
.

The
unstable operation

\mathscr{F}_{\overline{\mathsf{a}}}
, associated to an unstable tuple
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
, is defined using the following correspondences.

U1)

(Identity) If
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
represents spheres/discs with one incoming and one outgoing interior marked point, the correspondences are

X\;\xleftarrow{\text{id}_{X}}\;X\;\xrightarrow{\text{id}_{X}}\;X\qquad\text{and}\qquad L\;\xleftarrow{\text{id}_{L}}\;L\;\xrightarrow{\text{id}_{L}}\;L.

U2)

(Co-unit) If
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
represents spheres with one incoming marked point, respectively discs with only one incoming boundary marked point, the correspondences are

X\;\xleftarrow{\text{id}_{X}}\;X\;\to\;\text{pt},\qquad\text{respectively}\qquad L\;\xleftarrow{\text{id}_{L}}\;L\;\to\;\text{pt}.

U3)

(Unit) If
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
corresponds to a sphere/disc with one outgoing interior/boundary marked point, the correspondences are

\text{pt}\;\xleftarrow{}\;X\;\xrightarrow{\text{id}_{X}}\;X\qquad\text{and}\qquad\text{pt}\;\xleftarrow{}\;L\;\xrightarrow{\text{id}_{L}}\;L.

U4)

(Pairing) If
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
represents a sphere with two incoming marked points or a disc with two incoming boundary marked points, then the correspondences are

X\times X\;\xleftarrow{\Delta_{X}}\;X\;\xrightarrow{}\;\text{pt}\qquad\text{and}\qquad L\times L\;\xleftarrow{\Delta_{L}}\;L\;\xrightarrow{}\;\text{pt}.

U5)

(Co-pairing) If
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
represents a sphere with two outgoing marked points the correspondence is

\text{pt}\;\xleftarrow{}\;(TX^{\oplus 2}_{\epsilon},\pi_{X}^{*}TX^{\oplus 2},\Delta_{TX^{\oplus 2}},\tau_{X}^{\oplus 2})\;\xrightarrow{\exp\times\exp}\;X\times X,

where
\epsilon>0
is smaller than the injectivity radius of the chosen Riemannian metric on
X
,
\pi_{X}
is the projection map and
\tau_{X}
is a Thom form of
TX
with support in the
\epsilon
-disc bundle.

If
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
represents a disc with two outgoing boundary marked points, the correspondence
\mathfrak{C}^{0}_{0,2}
is given by

\text{pt}\;\xleftarrow{}(TL^{\oplus 2}_{\epsilon},\pi_{L}^{*}TL^{\oplus 2},\Delta_{TL^{\oplus 2}},\tau_{L}^{\oplus 2})\;\xrightarrow{\exp\times\exp}\;L\times L,

with
\epsilon,\pi_{L}
and
\tau_{L}
as in the closed case.

U6)

(Restriction) if
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
represents a disc with one incoming interior marked point, the associated correspondence is

X\;\xleftarrow{j}\;L\;\to\;\text{pt},

where
j
is the inclusion of
L
.

U7)

(Inclusion) if
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
represents a disc with one outgoing interior marked point, the associated correspondence is

\text{pt}\;\leftarrow\;(TX_{\epsilon}|_{L},\pi_{X}^{*}(TX|_{L}),\Delta,\tau_{X}|_{TX|_{L}})\;\xrightarrow{\exp}\;X.

U8)

If the curve that
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
represents has no marked point, then we associate the empty correspondence
\text{pt}\leftarrow\emptyset\to\text{pt}
to it.

We define
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}
to be zero if
\sigma
is a degenerate simplex in an unstable moduli space.

We can now turn to the correspondences required for the higher homotopies. The proof is a variation of the proof of Theorem
3.15
.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
\{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\}_{\Gamma}
be a system of cubical cobordisms as in Theorem
3.15
. Then, given any sequence
\underline{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{1})
of stable map graphs and truly unstable corollas, there exists smooth global Kuranishi charts
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
with corners with a submersion to
I^{d-1}\times\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}I^{E(\Gamma_{d-j})}
with the following properties.

1)

\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
admits a smooth submersion to the base space

\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\;\coloneqq\;\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d}}\times\dots\times\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{1}}
.

2)

\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
admits smooth invariant submersions
ev^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\to Y^{+}_{\Gamma_{d}}
and
ev^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\to Y^{-}_{\Gamma_{1}}
.

3)

\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}|_{\{s_{i}=0\}}\simeq\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{i+1}\circ\Gamma_{i},\dots,\Gamma_{1}}
as correspondences over
Y^{-}_{\Gamma_{1}}
and
Y^{+}_{\Gamma_{d}}
.

4)

\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}|_{\{s_{i}=1\}}\simeq\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{i+1}}\times_{Y^{+}_{\Gamma_{i}}}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{i},\dots,\Gamma_{1}}
as (unoriented ) correspondences over
Y^{-}_{\Gamma_{1}}
and
Y^{+}_{\Gamma_{d}}
.

5)

\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}\simeq\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d},\dots,(\Gamma_{i})_{e},\dots,\Gamma_{1}}
for any edge
e\in E(\Gamma_{i})
.

6)

\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}|_{\{t_{e}=1\}}\simeq\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{i}^{\prime\prime},\Gamma^{\prime}_{i},\dots,\Gamma_{1}}|_{\{s_{i}=0\}}
for any edge
e\in E(\Gamma_{i})
separating
\Gamma_{i}
into
\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}
and
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}
(where we add identity corollas as necessary).

7)

\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}=\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{1}}
if
d=1
.

8)

If
\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{1}
are all stable, then
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}=I^{d-1}\times\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d}}\times_{Y_{d-1}^{+}}\dots\times_{Y_{1}^{+}}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{1}}
.

Here we use the coordinates
s_{1},\dots,s_{d-1}
on
I^{d-1}
.

Note that (
1
) implies that we have a smooth map
\text{st}\colon\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\to\prod_{i=1}^{d}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\text{st}}_{i}}
, where we take
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\text{st}}_{i}}
to be a point if
\Gamma_{i}
does not have a well-defined stabilisation.

Proof.

In this proof a graph will mean a stable map graph or a disjoint union of truly unstable corollas. The upshot of the construction is that different stable graphs can have different
stabilisation patterns
, that is, which evaluation maps are stabilised can change when composing two graphs. The easiest example is the coproduct in energy zero: the two outgoing marked points are stabilised; however, when forgetting one (and recovering the identity) the remaining marked point is no longer stabilised in our definition. This mismatch has to be reconciled by a cobordism. We will construct them here compatibly.

We first have to define some notation.
To a graph
\Gamma
and a marked point
j
of
\Gamma
, we associate the number

(3.17)

\delta_{\Gamma}(j)\,\coloneqq\,\begin{cases}1\quad&\text{if }ev_{\Gamma,j}\text{ is stabilised}\\
0\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Here an evaluation map is stabilised according to Theorem
3.15
or Definition
3.18
.
Then, given a sequence
\underline{\Gamma}
as in the statement with outgoing marked points labelled by
y^{d}_{c}\sqcup y^{d}_{o}
and incoming marked points labelled by
y_{c}^{0}\sqcup y_{o}^{0}
, we define the functions
\chi^{\pm}_{j}\colon I^{d-1}\to I
by

(3.18)

\chi_{\underline{\Gamma},j}^{-}(s)\;=\;\sum_{k=1}^{d}\delta_{\Gamma_{k}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{1}}(j)\,s_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}(1-s_{i})

for
j\in y_{c}^{0}\sqcup y_{o}^{0}
, where we set
s_{d}\equiv 0
, and

(3.19)

\chi_{\underline{\Gamma},j}^{+}(s)\;=\;\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\delta_{\Gamma_{d}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{d-k}}(j)\,s_{d-k-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}(1-s_{d-i})

for
j\in y^{d}_{c}\sqcup y^{d}_{o}
with
s_{0}\equiv 1
. The key property that the maps
\chi^{\pm}_{\underline{\Gamma},j}
satisfy is that

(3.20)

\chi^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma},j}(s)\;=\;\delta_{\Gamma_{k}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{1}}(j)\qquad\qquad\text{if }s_{1},\dots,s_{k-1}=0,\,s_{k}=1,

and

(3.21)

\chi^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma},j}(s)\;=\;\delta_{\Gamma_{d}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{d-k}}(j)\qquad\qquad\text{if }s_{d-1},\dots,s_{d-k}=0,\,s_{d-k-1}=1.

More generally, letting
\chi^{\pm}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
be the product over all respective marked points, we have for
s_{k}=1
, that

(3.22)

\chi^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma}}(s)=\chi^{-}_{\Gamma_{k},\dots,\Gamma_{1}}(s_{k-1},\dots,s_{1})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\chi^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma}}(s)=\chi^{+}_{(\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{k})}(s_{d-1},\dots,s_{k}).

We now define for
1\leq i\leq d
the functions

(3.23)

\chi^{+,i}_{\underline{\Gamma}}(s)\coloneqq\chi^{+}_{\Gamma_{i},\dots,\Gamma_{1}}(s_{i-1},\dots,s_{1})\qquad\qquad\chi^{-,i}_{\underline{\Gamma}}(s)\coloneqq\chi^{-}_{\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{i}}(s_{d-1},\dots,s_{i}).

Given this, we define the thickening

\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\subset I^{d-1}\times\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\widehat{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d-i}}}

to be the parametrised fibre product of tuples
(s,(u_{d},\dots,u_{1}))
where the global Kuranishi chart
\widehat{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d-i}}}
is the maximal stabilisation possible (although we only stabilise its evaluation maps according to the rules of Theorem
3.15
, respectively Definition
3.18
) and for any edge
e
between
\Gamma_{i}
and
\Gamma_{i+1}
we have

(3.24)

\exp_{u_{i}(z_{i,e})}\Bigl(\chi^{+,i}_{\underline{\Gamma},e}(s)\xi_{i,e}\Bigr)\;=\;\exp_{u_{i+1}(z_{i+1,e})}\Bigl(\chi^{-,i+1}_{\underline{\Gamma},e}(s)\xi_{i+1,e}\Bigr),

where
z_{i,e}
and
z_{i+1,e}
are the marked points on the respective graph that are clutched to
e
and
\xi_{j,e}
is the tangent vector associated to that marked point, coming from the stabilisation of the respective global Kuranishi chart. We let
\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
and
s_{\underline{\Gamma}}
be the pullback of
\bigoplus\limits_{i=0}^{d-1}\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d-i}}
and the product of the obstruction sections, and we let the covering group be
G_{\underline{\Gamma}}=\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}G_{d-1}
. Since the outgoing evaluation map is always a submersion, the resulting global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
is a smooth global Kuranishi chart and it satisfies the first two as well as the penultimate property of the assertion. The remaining two properties follow from the fact that the functions
\chi_{\underline{\Gamma}}^{\pm,i}
have the property (
3.22
) as well as the fact that
\chi^{\pm}_{\underline{\Gamma},j}\equiv\delta_{\Gamma_{d}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{1}}(j)
if
\delta_{\Gamma_{d}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{k}}
does not depend on
k=1,\dots,d
.
∎

3.3.
Construction

We define
\mathscr{F}_{L}
on objects to be the completed tensor product

(3.25)

\mathscr{F}_{L}^{*}(y)\;\coloneqq\;\Omega^{*}(X^{y_{c}}\times L^{y_{o}};\Lambda)\;=\;\Omega^{*}(X^{y_{c}}\times L^{y_{o}})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\Lambda

of differential forms with the Novikov ring. The differential is given by

(3.26)

d_{y}(\alpha T^{\beta})\;\coloneqq\;(-1)^{|\alpha|}d_{dR}(\alpha)T^{\beta}

for
\alpha\in\Omega^{*}(X^{y_{c}}\times L^{y_{o}};\Lambda)
.
To define
\mathscr{F}
on morphisms we will use the correspondences constructed in the previous subsection. However, they are only relatively oriented over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
and do not yet take any information about chains on
