\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}
into account. The point of the next lemma is that we can modify these global Kuranishi charts using morphisms of
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}
so that we obtain oriented correspondences. The definition of these operations is a considerable generalisation of the
\mathfrak{q}
-operations of
[
53
]
and requires the resolution of singularities discussed in §
A
. Throughout the remainder of the section, we write
\beta\in H_{2}
if we do not want to specify whether
\beta
is a relative homology class in
H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
or lies in
H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
.

Lemma 3.20
.

To a stable simplex
\sigma\colon\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}}
and
\beta\in H_{2}
we can associate a well-defined map

(3.27)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}^{\beta}_{\sigma}\colon\Omega^{*}(X^{y_{c}}\times L^{y_{o}})\to\Omega^{*}(X^{y^{\prime}_{c}}\times L^{y^{\prime}_{o}})

\displaystyle\alpha\mapsto(-1)^{\delta_{0,h}\,w_{\mathfrak{s}}(\beta)}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{\Gamma}=\beta\\
\Gamma^{\text{st}}=\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}\end{subarray}}{c_{\Gamma}\,(ev^{+}_{\Gamma,\sigma})_{*}\Bigl((ev^{-}_{\Gamma,\sigma})^{*}\alpha\wedge p_{\sigma}^{*}s_{\Gamma}^{*}\eta_{\Gamma}\Bigr)},

of degree

(3.28)

\deg(\mathfrak{q}^{\beta}_{\sigma})=-\mu_{L}(\beta)+|\sigma|

where the sum is over all marked prestable graphs of total degree and type
\mathsf{a}
and the factor is given by

(3.29)

c_{\Gamma}\coloneqq\frac{|\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma^{\text{st}})|}{|\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma^{ps})|},

where
\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma)
is the automorphism group of
\Gamma
preserving the distribution into incoming and outgoing marked points. We call
\mathfrak{q}^{\beta}_{\sigma}
the
\mathfrak{q}
-operation associated to
\sigma
of energy
\beta
.

Proof.

It will be useful to consider the pull-push operation without the sign and separately for each graph
\Gamma
, so we write

(3.30)

\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}(\alpha)\coloneqq(ev^{+}_{\Gamma,\sigma})_{*}\Bigl((ev^{-}_{\Gamma,\sigma})^{*}\alpha\wedge p_{\sigma}^{*}s^{*}\eta_{\Gamma}\Bigr)

for the (not yet defined) part of the sum. For a stable map graph
\Gamma
of total degree
\beta
and with stabilisation
\Gamma^{\text{st}}=\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}
, define

(3.31)

\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma,\sigma}\,\coloneqq\,\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}}}\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert},

where the map

(3.32)

\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}}

is given by the stabilisation map
\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}}
as well as the composition

\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\to I^{E(\Gamma)}\to I^{E(\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma})}
.
Then,
\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma,\sigma}
is canonically a smooth orbifold over

\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma,\sigma}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}}}\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert}

with a smooth structure away from the singular points, which lie in codimension
2
. Since any unstable vertices of
\Gamma
are discs with only boundary marked points, there exists a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma})\

\displaystyle\cong\ \hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert})

\displaystyle\cong\ \hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{\Gamma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\lVert\sigma\rVert)

(3.33)

\displaystyle\cong\ \hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y_{\sigma}^{+})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\det(\sigma))\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mu_{L}(\beta_{\Gamma}))\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(|\sigma|)

over the locus of regular points, where
Y_{\sigma}^{+}=X^{y^{\prime}_{c}}\times L^{y_{c}}
. Note that
\mu_{L}(\beta_{\Gamma})
is even, so its position in the tensor product of orientation lines is irrelevant.
By the definition of local system coefficient,
\sigma
carries the data of a trivialisation of
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\det(\sigma))
. Thus, the isomorphism (
3.3
) yields a trivialisation of the orientation bundle of the smooth locus of
\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma}
.

Let
p_{\sigma}\colon\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma,\sigma}\to\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
be the canonical projection. Set

(3.34)

\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma}\,\coloneqq\,\Bigl(\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma,\sigma},p_{\sigma}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma},p_{\sigma}^{*}s_{\Gamma}\Bigr).

Since the evaluations maps
ev^{+}_{\Gamma}
are jointly submersive with
\mathfrak{f}
, the pullbacks
ev_{\Gamma,\sigma}^{\pm}
of the evaluation maps
ev^{\pm}_{\Gamma}
are still smooth and
ev^{+}_{\Gamma,\sigma}
is a relative submersion.
By Theorem
A.22
with
\mathcal{W}=\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert}
,
\mathcal{V}=\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}}
and
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
and the orientation determined by the order of the factors in (
3.31
), the map
\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}
, defined by (
3.30
), is well-defined. Hence, so is
\mathfrak{q}^{\beta}_{\sigma}
.

Finally, to compute the degree of
\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}
, recall that, due to the definition of
\text{det}^{\otimes n}
, the map
\sigma
is defined on
\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert}
, where

(3.35)

\lVert\sigma\rVert=|\sigma|-n\chi+2n|y^{\prime}_{c}|+n|y_{o}^{\prime}|

with
\chi
being the Euler characteristic of the curves whose moduli
\sigma
maps to. Thus,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}(\alpha)|

\displaystyle=|\alpha|-\Bigl(\text{vdim}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}(X,L)\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{oc}(y,y^{\prime})}\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert})-2n|y_{c}^{\prime}|-n|y^{\prime}_{o}|\Bigr)

\displaystyle=|\alpha|-\Bigl(\lVert\sigma\rVert+n\chi+\mu_{L}(\beta_{\mathsf{a}})-2n|y_{c}^{\prime}|-n|y^{\prime}_{o}|\Bigr)

\displaystyle=|\alpha|-|\sigma|-\mu_{L}(\beta_{\mathsf{a}})

as claimed.
∎

Remark 3.21
.

Note that the definition of (
3.27
) is also well-defined if the domain of
\sigma
is an analytic map on an analytic manifold with corners.

The operations of Lemma
3.20
are not quite sufficient as they are not defined on elements of the morphism spaces of
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}
, cf. Definition
3.7
. Thus, we have to show that these operations are independent under pushforwards by contractions in a suitable sense. The next lemma is essential to deal with the fact that the stabilisation map contracts unstable components, whence certain canonical squares are only Cartesian away from strata of positive codimension.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\psi\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a proper smooth map between smooth étale Lie groupoids with corners of the same dimension. If
\deg(\psi)=1
and
h\colon X\to Z
is a submersion to a smooth manifold so that
h^{\prime}\coloneqq h\psi
is a submersion as well, then

h^{\prime}_{*}\psi^{*}=h_{*}

as maps
\Omega^{*}_{c}(X)\to\Omega^{*-k}(Z)
.

Proof.

By Sard’s theorem for orbifolds,
[
13
, Theorem 4.1]
, the restriction
\psi_{z}:h^{\prime-1}(z)\rightarrow h^{-1}(z)
is a degree-
1
map for an open dense set
U\subset Z
. By the degree formula for orbifolds,
[
7
, Theorem 1.1]
, we have
(h_{*}\alpha)_{z}=(h^{\prime}_{*}\psi^{*}\alpha)_{z}
for
z\in U
and
\alpha\in\Omega_{c}^{*}(X)
. By continuity, the two differential forms agree everywhere.
∎

Lemma 3.23
.

Suppose
\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
is stable. Then, the map

\mathfrak{q}^{\beta}\,\colon\,\Omega^{*}(X^{y_{c}}\times L^{y_{0}})\times\bigoplus\limits_{\overline{\Gamma}}C^{an}_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}})\;\to\;\Omega^{*}(X^{y^{\prime}_{c}}\times L^{y^{\prime}_{0}})\;:\;(\sigma,\alpha)\mapsto\mathfrak{q}^{\mathsf{a}}_{\sigma}(\alpha)

of Lemma
3.20
descends to a well-defined map

(3.36)

\mathscr{F}^{\beta}\colon\Omega^{*}(X^{y_{c}}\times L^{y_{0}})\times\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}^{*}(y,y^{\prime})\to\Omega^{*}(X^{y^{\prime}_{c}}\times L^{y^{\prime}_{0}})

of degree
-\mu_{L}(\beta_{\mathsf{a}})
.

Proof.

It suffices to treat the case where
\mathsf{a}
consists of a single element. The general case is analogous. Thus the claim reduces to showing that if
\sigma\colon\Delta^{m}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}}
has image contained in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}}|_{t_{e}=0}
, then
q_{\Gamma,\sigma}=0
if in the stabilisation of the underlying prestable graph
\Gamma^{ps}
several edges are combined to form
e
. But in this case the map
\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\to I^{E(\Gamma^{\text{st}})}
has a corner singularity (Definition
A.7
) over
\{t_{e}=0\}
, whence the associated operation vanishes by construction. Therefore, the claim follows up to sign from
[
35
, Lemma 4.22]
, combined with Lemma
3.24
.
To see that the sign is
1
, we observe that the outer rectangle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{\Gamma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}\Bigl(\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}}\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert}\Bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{\sigma}}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}})^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\bar{\partial}_{\Gamma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

commutes because both squares commute up to sign
(-1)^{\text{ind}(\bar{\partial}_{\Gamma})}
. It now follows from a straightforward verification that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y_{\sigma}^{+})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\det(\sigma))\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(|\sigma|)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\psi(\Gamma),\psi_{*}\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y_{\psi_{*}\sigma}^{+})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\det(\psi_{*}\sigma))\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(|\psi_{*}\sigma|)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes\eqref{eq:iso-of-det}\otimes\text{id}}

commutes, yielding the desired result.
∎

Lemma 3.24
.

Suppose
X
is a Lie groupoid admitting a rel–
C^{\infty}
action by a finite group
G
. If
f^{\pm}\colon X\to Y^{\pm}
are two rel–
C^{\infty}

G
-invariant maps so that
f^{+}
is a submersions, let
\underline{f}^{\pm}\colon[X/G]=[G\times X_{1}\rightrightarrows X_{0}]\to Y^{\pm}
be the induced maps. Then,

(3.37)

\underline{f}^{+}_{*}((\underline{f}^{-})^{*}\alpha)=\frac{1}{|G|}\,f^{+}_{*}({f^{-}}^{*}\alpha)

for any
\alpha\in\Omega^{*}(Y^{-})
.

Proof.

Note first that
{f^{-}}^{*}\alpha
is
G
-invariant for any
\alpha\in\Omega^{*}(Y^{-})
. Thus the only difference between the two pushforward is given by the choice of partition of unity, cf.
[
52
, Example 4.16]
. Given a partition of unity
\rho
for
X
, we may first replace it by its average over
G
. Due to the fact that
G
intertwines source and target map this yields a well-defined
G
-invariant partition of unity
\varrho
on
X
. Then, set
\underline{\rho}:=\frac{1}{|G|}\varrho
. This is a well-defined partition of unity because
\underline{s}^{*}\varrho
is
G
-invariant. Hence,
\underline{t}_{*}\underline{s}^{*}\varrho=|G|t_{*}s^{*}\varrho=|G|
.
∎

For the unstable operations, we write
\overline{\mathsf{a}}
for the unique
0
-dimensional simplex mapping to the coarse moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\overline{\mathsf{a}}}
and let

(3.38)

\mathscr{F}_{\overline{\mathsf{a}}}^{0}\coloneqq\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{\mathsf{a}}}}\colon\mathscr{F}(y)\to\mathscr{F}(y^{\prime})

be the operation associated to the respective correspondence in Definition
3.18
. We set
\mathscr{F}_{\overline{\mathsf{a}}}^{\beta}
for
\beta
with
\omega(\beta)>0
.
We then extend
\mathscr{F}
linearly to chains, that is, for
\sigma=\sum\limits_{i}{t_{i}\,\sigma_{i}}
and
\beta\in H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
we set

(3.39)

\mathscr{F}_{\sigma}^{\beta}\coloneqq\sum\limits_{i}{t_{i}\,\mathscr{F}^{\beta}_{\sigma_{i}}}.

Definition 3.25
.

For
\beta\in H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
with
\omega(\beta)>0
, let

\mathscr{F}^{\beta}_{D_{1}}\coloneqq\sum\limits_{\Gamma}{\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma}}\colon\Omega^{*}(L)\to\Omega^{*}(L)

where we are summing over stable map graphs of type
(\beta,0,1)
with one incoming and one outgoing boundary marked point and
\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma}
is the operation associated to the correspondence
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
of Theorem
3.15
with the canonical orientation induced by the relative spin structure on
L
. Similarly,

\mathscr{F}^{\beta}_{D_{0}}\coloneqq\sum\limits_{\Gamma}{\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma}}\in\Omega^{*}(L)

is given by a sum over stable map graphs of type
(\beta,0,1)
with one outgoing boundary marked point and
\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma}
being the operation associated to the correspondence
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
.

Remark 3.26
.

These operations do not require resolutions of singularities since we need not take the fibre product with a chain.

We now define the higher homotopies of the
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}_{L}
. Recall that these take the form of linear maps

(3.40)

\mathscr{F}_{\underline{\sigma}}^{d}\colon\mathscr{F}(y^{0})\,\to\,\mathscr{F}(y^{d}).

for composable sequences
\underline{\sigma}=(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{1})
in
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}
.
As in the case of the previous operations, the operations (
3.40
) are induced by correspondences. To make the notation uniform for stable and unstable operations, we define
truly unstable
corollas, that is, graphs without edges whose vertex has energy. In this case we let
\mathcal{K}_{\overline{\mathsf{a}}}
be the corresponding global Kuranishi chart correspondence of Definition
3.18
. Given a disjoint union
T
of truly unstable corollas and a stable (map) graph
\Gamma
so that the incoming/outgoing marked points of
T
correspond to the outgoing/incoming marked points of
\Gamma
, we define the composition
T\circ\Gamma
(respectively
\Gamma\circ T
) to be the stable (map) graph obtained by applying the respective operation associated to the truly unstable vertices of
T
described in Definition
2.1
, e.g., forgetting a marked point our clutching two marked points in
\Gamma
to form an edge. Given this, it is clear what a stable (map) graph with truly unstable vertices is. In contrast to a composition of stable (map) graphs the composition
T\circ\Gamma
does not always create new edges. If
T
and
\Gamma=T^{\prime}
is a disjoint union of truly unstable corollas, then we can similarly define
T\circ T^{\prime}
. Note that this is again a truly unstable corolla. To make the notation more streamlined, we define for a graph
\Gamma
with
k^{\pm}
interior and
\ell^{\pm}
boundary outgoing/incoming marked points the manifolds

Y^{\pm}_{\Gamma}\coloneqq X^{k^{\pm}}\times L^{\ell^{\pm}}

and similarly if the marked points are labelled by finite ordered sets.

Lemma 3.27
.

For any
d\geq 1
and any objects
y^{0},\dots,y^{d}
of
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}
, there exists a well-defined linear map

\mathscr{F}^{d}=\sum\limits_{\beta}{\mathscr{F}^{d,\beta}Q^{\beta}}\;\colon\;\mathscr{F}^{*}(y^{0})\otimes\bigotimes\limits_{i=1}^{d}{\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}^{*}(y^{i-1},y^{i})}\;\longrightarrow\;\mathscr{F}(y^{d})

of degree
0
.

Proof.

Suppose
\sigma_{i}\colon\Delta^{\lVert\sigma_{i}\rVert}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{i}}
is an analytic simplex for each
1\leq i\leq d
, where the incoming marked points of
\overline{\Gamma}_{i}
are labelled by
y^{i-1}
and its outgoing marked points by
y^{i}
. Note that
\overline{\Gamma}_{i}
may be a collection of truly unstable corolla, in which case we can assume
\lVert\sigma_{i}\rVert=0
. Fix also
\beta\in H_{2}(X,L;\mathbb{Z})
, respectively
H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
if all simplices map to moduli spaces of closed curves. Let
\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{1}
be stable map graphs, respectively disjoint unions of truly unstable corolla if
\overline{\Gamma}_{i}
is such, so that
\sum\limits_{i}{\beta_{\Gamma_{i}}}=\beta
. Then,
\underline{\sigma}
induces an analytic map
\Delta^{\lVert\underline{\sigma}\rVert}:=\prod\limits_{i\leq d}{\Delta^{m_{d-i}}}\to\prod\limits_{i\leq d}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{i}}}
and we can form the fibre product

\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\;\coloneqq\;\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\times_{\prod\limits_{i\leq d}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{i}}}}\Delta^{\lVert\underline{\sigma}\rVert}.

Define the orientation line

(3.41)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)\coloneqq\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\det(\sigma))\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(|\sigma|)=\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\Delta^{\lVert\sigma\rVert})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\det(\sigma))\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(-n\chi+n|y^{\prime}_{o}|+2n|y_{c}^{\prime}|).

Over the smooth locus of this fibre product, the fibre product
\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}
has a canonical orientation, induced by the isomorphism

(3.42)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}})\;\cong\;\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y_{\sigma_{d}}^{+})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(I^{\{d-1\}})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{d-1})\cdots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(I^{\{1\}})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{1})

and the given trivialisations that are part of the data of the simplices (i.e., coming from the local coefficients). Note that this is not the standard fibre product orientation, even in the case where
\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}
is an (unparametrised) fibre product.

Using the resolution of singularities, Theorem
A.22
, on
\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}
as in the proof of Lemma
3.20
, we obtain a well-defined linear map

(3.43)

\mathfrak{q}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\;\colon\;\Omega^{*}(X^{y_{c}^{0}}\times L^{y^{0}_{o}};\Lambda)\;\to\;\Omega^{*}(X^{y_{c}^{d}}\times L^{y^{d}_{o}};\Lambda)

which (up to replacing
\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}
by a non-singular space) is given by

\alpha\;\mapsto\;(ev_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}^{+})_{*}((ev_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}^{+})*\alpha\wedge p_{\underline{\sigma}}^{*}s_{\underline{\Gamma}}^{*}\eta_{\underline{\Gamma}})

where
\eta_{\underline{\Gamma}}
is the pullback of the product of Thom forms. Then,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{q}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)|=|\alpha|-\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}})+2n|y_{c}^{d}|+n|y_{o}^{d}|=|\alpha|+|\underline{\sigma}|+1-d,

recalling that
|\sigma_{i}|=-m-2n\chi+2n|y^{i}_{c}|+n|y^{i}_{o}|
. Thus,

\mathscr{F}^{d,\beta}_{\underline{\sigma}}\;\coloneqq\;\mathfrak{q}^{\beta}_{\underline{\sigma}}\;\coloneqq\;(-1)^{\epsilon}\sum\limits_{\underline{\Gamma}}{c_{\underline{\Gamma}}\mathfrak{q}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}},

is a linear maps of degree
|\underline{\sigma}|
, where

(3.44)

\epsilon\coloneqq\sum\limits_{i}{\delta_{0,h_{i}}w_{\mathfrak{s}}(\beta_{i})}\qquad\qquad c_{\underline{\Gamma}}\coloneqq\prod\limits_{i}{c_{\Gamma_{i}}},

with
c_{\Gamma_{i}}=1
if
\Gamma_{i}
is truly unstable and given by the formula in (
3.29
) otherwise. Clearly, the map
\mathscr{F}^{d,\beta}_{\underline{\sigma}}
is linear. We extend it linearly to chains on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{d}}\times\dots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{1}}
. By the same argument as in Lemma
3.23
, applied to each
\sigma_{i}
separately, it descends to the claimed linear map.
∎

We show in §
3.4
that
\mathscr{F}
satisfies the
A_{\infty}
functor equations (
2.18
) and that it is semi-strict and operadic. Here, we show first that
\mathscr{F}
interacts well with the symmetric monoidal structures, is unital and has Property
2.42
, which is important for being able to deform it to an uncurved open-closed DMFT.

Lemma 3.28
.

\mathscr{F}
is unital.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\text{id}}^{\times y}}=\text{id}_{\mathscr{F}(y)}
. Suppose then
\underline{\sigma}=(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{1})
is a composable sequence in
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}
with
d>1
and
\sigma_{j}=\sigma_{\text{id}}
for some
j
.
Given graphs
\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{1}
lying over the simplices, the gluing
\Gamma_{d}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{j}
has the same stabilisation pattern as
\Gamma_{d}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{j+1}
and
\Gamma_{j}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{1}
has the same stabilisation pattern as
\Gamma_{j-1}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{1}
.
We first show that the map
\chi^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
is degenerate. By definition,

\chi^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma}}(s)\;=\;\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\delta_{\Gamma_{i}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{1}}\,s_{i}\prod_{\ell=1}^{i-1}(1-s_{\ell})\;+\;\delta_{\Gamma_{i}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{1}}(s_{j}+s_{j+1}(1-s_{j}))\prod_{\ell=1}^{j-1}(1-s_{\ell})\\
+\;(1-s_{j})(1-s_{j+1})\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\delta_{\Gamma_{i}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{1}}\,s_{i}\prod_{\ell=j+2}^{i-1}(1-s_{\ell}),

where
\delta_{\Gamma}
is defined by (
3.17
) and the first sum does not depend on
s_{j}
and
s_{j+1}
. Since

(1-s_{j})(1-s_{j+1})=1-(s_{j}+s_{j+1}(1-s_{j})),

it follows that
\chi^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma}}(s)
can be written as the sum

\chi^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma}}(s)=f(s^{\prime})+(1-s_{j})(1-s_{j+1})g(s^{\prime})

where
s^{\prime}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+2},\dots,s_{d-1})
. Thus,
\chi^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
factors through the map
I^{d-1}\to I^{d-2}:s\mapsto(s^{\prime},s_{j}s_{j+1})
, which is degenerate. It follows from a similar computation that
\chi^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
factors through this map as well. While the maps
\chi^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma},i}
(
\chi^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma},i}
) do not depend on
s_{j}
or
s_{j+1}
for
i<j
(respectively
i>j+1
), the map
\chi^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma},j}
does. However, since we are not stabilising
\mathcal{K}_{T_{\text{id}}}
, this does not matter and the above argument shows that
\chi^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma},i}
factors through
s\mapsto(s^{\prime},(1-s_{j})(1-s_{j+1}))
as well. The same is true for
\chi^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma},j}
and
\chi^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma},j+1}
. This shows that
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}
is degenerate, whence
\mathscr{F}_{\underline{\sigma}}=0
by the degeneracy property in Definition
2.31
.
∎

3.4.
A_{\infty}
functor relations

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
3.1
by showing that the operations
\mathscr{F}^{d}
defined above satisfy the
A_{\infty}
functor relations.

Proposition 3.29
.

\mathscr{F}
satisfies Equation (
2.18
). That is,

(3.45)

(-1)^{|\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}|}(d_{y^{d}}\circ\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}-(-1)^{|\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}|}\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}\circ d_{y_{0}})+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}{(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+1}\mathscr{F}^{d-i}_{\underline{\sigma}^{>i}}\circ\mathscr{F}^{i}_{\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}}}\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+1}\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\partial_{oc}\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{d}}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}{(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+1}\mathscr{F}^{d-1}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{i+1}\circ\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1}}},

for any
d\geq 1
and any
\sigma_{i}\in\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}(y^{i-1},y^{i})
.

The proof has several steps and will occupy the rest of the subsection. We first state two consequences.

Corollary 3.30
.

\mathscr{F}
is semi-strict and operadic.

Proof.

Due to Proposition
3.14
(
5
), the proof boils down to showing that
\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}
is a degenerate correspondence whenever
d>2
and
\underline{\sigma}
is a sequence of chains in
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}^{*,\text{st}}
or
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}^{*,\text{opr}}
.

It suffices to prove that
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}=I\times\mathcal{K}
for some global Kuranishi chart
\mathcal{K}
so that
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\text{st}}}
and the evaluation maps factor through the projection to
\mathcal{K}
. The key point is that the functions
\chi^{\pm}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
are constant whenever
\delta_{\Gamma_{j}\circ\dots\Gamma_{1}}(\kappa)
and
\delta_{\Gamma_{d}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{j}}(\kappa)
do not depend on
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, where
\kappa\in y
labels an incoming marked point and
\kappa^{\prime}\in y^{\prime}
an outgoing one. It follows from our definition of
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
in Theorem
3.15
that this is the case whenever
\Gamma
admits a stabilisation or when each component of
\Gamma
has only one outgoing marked point.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.29
.

Suppose
\sigma_{i}\in\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}^{*}(y,y^{\prime})
is represented by an analytic simplex
\sigma_{i}\colon\Delta^{\lVert\sigma_{i}\rVert}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}_{i}}

\overline{\Gamma}_{i}
is a disjoint union of truly unstable corolla. Fix

, respectively

if all

\mathscr{S}_{d}=\left\{\underline{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{1})\;\big|\;\sum_{i=1}^{d}\beta_{\Gamma_{i}}=\beta,\,\Gamma_{i}^{\text{st}}=\overline{\Gamma}_{i}\right\}.

\displaystyle\equiv\;1+n|y_{o}^{d}|+|\underline{\sigma}_{i}|^{\prime}\mod{2}.

Writing

(3.47)

(ignoring orientations). Here we write

for the vertical boundary of the stabilisation map
\text{st}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\overline{\Gamma}}}
; this corresponds to
\partial^{\text{st}}_{+}\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}

.

See Definition 4.1 and Proposition 4.3 op. cit. for his definition of vertical boundary.

Let us now turn to the vertical boundary of

. It follows from its description as a parametrised fibre product, there are three types of contribution to the vertical boundary

•

The first is of the form

Recall the notation

\underline{\sigma}^{>i}\coloneqq(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{i+1})\qquad\quad\qquad\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}\coloneqq(\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1}).

We will use the same notation for sequences of graphs. Moreover, we abbreviate

Note that

We are thus only left with the
\{t_{e_{v}}=1\}

\partial^{\mathscr{F}}
as well as the curvature. We first discuss the case of

.

Lemma 3.31
.

\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma}
satisfies Equation (
3.45
) when
\sigma
is a stable chain.

Proof.

The strata
\partial_{e}\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\coloneqq\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e_{v}}=1\}}
are of three different types. We discuss them first in the case of
d=1
, since the general case can then be derived more easily. In this case we have a single graph
\Gamma
and the boundary strata

(i)

\partial_{e}\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\cong\mathcal{T}_{T}\times_{L}\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}

(ii)

\partial_{e}\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\cong\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times_{L}\mathcal{T}_{T}

(iii)

\partial_{e}\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\cong\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}\times_{L}\mathcal{T}_{T}

where in the first two cases
T
(after contracting all edges) encodes a disc with one incoming and one outgoing marked point, attached to
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma\setminus\{v\}
either at the incoming or the outgoing marked point of
T
and in (
iii
), the tree (after contracting all edges) encodes a disc with only an outgoing marked point. By our orientation convention (
3.17
), it suffices to consider the case when
T
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
have no edges. We will suggestively write
\mathsf{a}_{1}
and
\mathsf{a}_{0}
, respectively to distinguish between the two types of vertices. For the proof here, the perspective of
[
51
, §B]
is useful. Observe in particular that for
\mathsf{a}_{1}
we have a canonical isomorphism

(3.52)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{1}})\;\cong\;\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mu_{L}(\beta))\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{-})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(sc)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(tr)^{\vee},

where we have fixed the outgoing marked point at
\infty
(identifying the disc with the upper half-plane), which leaves the two actions of positive scaling and positive translation, indicated by
sc
, respectively
tr
here. We will henceforth omit the trivial even-rank orientation line as we are only interested in the sign. The isomorphism with
L
is induced by the evaluation map at the outgoing (or equivalently incoming) marked point. As observed in
[
51
]
the linear gluing identifies the
inward-pointing
normal vector with the positive scaling of the action, while positive translation corresponds to the infinitesimal variations of the marked point on the disc bubble; which are thus tangential to the boundary stratum.
This shows that in Case (
i
) we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{1}})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}^{\prime},\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{+})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(sc)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(tr)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(sc)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)\eqref{eq:iso-to-det}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2)}

The map
(1)
has orientation sign
(-1)^{n+1}
because we moved
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})
past
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)
and
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{+})
past
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(sc)^{\vee}
. Meanwhile,
(2)
has orientation sign
(-1)
because
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})
is oriented with respect to the outward point normal vector and the discussion above. Thus, the vertical morphism on the left has orientation sign
(-1)^{n+1}
.
As before, the same sign appears if
\Gamma
(and thus
\Gamma^{\prime}
) is an arbitrary graph. Then, by Lemma
3.33
, the orientation sign of the equivalence between
\partial_{e}\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma}
and
(L^{i-1}\times\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{1}}\times L^{|y^{\prime}_{o}|-i})\times_{Y_{\sigma}^{+}}\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\sigma}
is
(-1)^{\epsilon_{\eqref{incoming-d}}}
with

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\eqref{incoming-d}}\;

\displaystyle=\;n+1+n(i-1)

\displaystyle=ni+1

By our sign convention, we thus have

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\alpha|+n\chi+\lVert\sigma\rVert}(ev_{\Gamma,\sigma}^{+}|_{\partial_{e}})_{*}((ev^{-}_{\Gamma,\sigma})^{*}(\alpha)\wedge\lambda_{\Gamma,\sigma}|_{\partial_{e}})\,

\displaystyle=\,(-1)^{|\alpha|+|\sigma|+1}\mathfrak{q}_{T\circ_{i}\Gamma^{\prime},D_{1}\circ_{i}\sigma}(\alpha).

Since the coefficient
c_{\Gamma}
, defined in (
3.29
), only depends on the prestable graph underlying
\Gamma
, we see that
c_{\Gamma}=c_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
for
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
in the above equation.

In Case (
ii
) we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}^{\prime},\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{-})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(sc)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(tr)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{-})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(sc)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(tr)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{-})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(tr)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3)}

Here
(1)
has orientation sign
(-1)^{|\sigma|+n\ell^{+}+1}
because we moved
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})
past the other orientation lines. Note that the orientation sign of
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(0)
is
1
because the identification uses the map
-dev_{z_{+}}
. The map
(2)
has orientation sign
(-1)
because
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})
is oriented with respect to the outward point normal vector and the discussion above. Finally,
(3)
is orientation-preserving. In total, we see that the vertical morphism on the right has orientation sign
(-1)^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{\eqref{outgoing-d}}}
with

\epsilon^{\prime}_{\eqref{outgoing-d}}=n|y^{\prime}_{o}|+|\sigma|.

Returning to the case where
\Gamma
(and thus
\Gamma^{\prime}
) is an arbitrary graph, it follows from Lemma
3.33
that the orientation sign of the equivalence between
\partial_{e}\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\sigma}\times_{Y_{\sigma}^{-}}(L^{i-1}\times\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{1}}\times L^{|y_{o}|-i})
is
(-1)^{\epsilon_{\eqref{outgoing-d}}}
with

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\eqref{outgoing-d}}\;

\displaystyle=\;\epsilon_{\eqref{outgoing-d}}^{\prime}+n(|y_{o}|-i)(\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{1}})-n)

\displaystyle\equiv\;n|y^{\prime}_{o}|+|\sigma|+n(|y_{o}|-i)\mod{2}.

Then, by Definition
3.25
, we have

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\alpha|+n|y^{\prime}_{o}|+|\sigma|}(ev_{\Gamma,\sigma}^{+}|_{\partial_{e}})_{*}((ev^{-}_{\Gamma,\sigma})^{*}(\alpha)\wedge\lambda_{\Gamma,\sigma}|_{\partial_{e}})\;

\displaystyle=\;(-1)^{|\alpha|}\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma^{\prime}\circ_{i}T,\sigma\circ_{i}D_{1}}(\alpha).

due to the fact that
\mathscr{F}
is compatible with
D_{1}
over
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}^{\text{st}}
. As before, we have
c_{\Gamma}=c_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
.

Finally, in Case (
iii
), we have to consider
\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\pi^{*}\sigma}
, or, equivalently the fibre product
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}}}\Delta^{m}
, where the graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
has an additional incoming marked point,
ordered before all other incoming marked points
(although it appears on the boundary after the
i
th marked point) and the map
\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
factors through the map forgetting said marked point. Thus,
\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\pi^{*}\sigma}
is oriented via the isomorphism

\displaystyle\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\pi^{*}\sigma})\;

\displaystyle\cong\;\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\pi^{*}\sigma)

(3.53)

\displaystyle\cong\;\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma).

where we use the isomorphism
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\pi^{*}\sigma)\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})
, cf. Construction
2.13
. We write
\Gamma^{\prime}_{-}
for the graph obtained by forgetting the additional marked point. The second isomorphism has sign
(-1)^{\star}:=(-1)^{n|y^{\prime}_{o}|+|\sigma|}
because we permute
\mathbb{R}
past
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\pi^{*}\sigma})
. To distinguish the normal direction from this
\mathbb{R}
-factor, we denote the normal direction by
\mathbb{R}_{\partial}
in the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R}_{\partial})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}^{+},\pi^{*}\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{0}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R}_{\partial})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(L)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(sc)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(tr)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R}_{\partial})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(sc)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(tr)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(T_{-})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(tr)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y^{+}_{\sigma})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{\star}\eqref{eq:orientation-of-lift}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(3)}

where the map
(1)
has orientation sign
1
as does
(2)
, using the same arguments as above and because we moved
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R}_{\partial})
past
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{R})
. Finally,
(3)
is orientation-preserving. In total, we see that the vertical morphism on the left has orientation sign
(-1)^{\epsilon_{\eqref{curv-d}}}
, where

(3.54)

\epsilon_{\eqref{curv-d}}=n|y^{\prime}_{o}|+|\sigma|.

Since
\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{0}})-n\equiv 0
, Lemma
3.33
implies that

\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}^{+},\pi^{*}\sigma}\times_{X^{y_{c}}\times L^{*\sqcup y_{o}}}(X^{y_{c}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}_{0}}\times L^{y_{o}})\simeq(-1)^{\epsilon_{\eqref{curv-d}}}\partial\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma,\sigma}-

Thus, the associated operation (
3.46
) becomes

(3.55)

(-1)^{|\alpha|+|\sigma|-n|y^{\prime}_{o}|}(ev_{\Gamma,\sigma}^{+}|_{\partial_{e}})_{*}((ev^{-}_{\Gamma,\sigma})^{*}(\alpha)\wedge\lambda_{\Gamma,\sigma}|_{\partial_{e}})\;=\;(-1)^{|\alpha|}\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma^{\prime}\circ_{*}T,\pi^{*}\sigma\circ_{*}D_{0}}(\alpha).

In this case, the underlying prestable graph of
\Gamma^{\prime}
differs from the one of
\Gamma
and we have to invoke
[
35
, Lemma 4.23]
to see that

(3.56)

\sum\limits_{\pi_{j}^{*}\Gamma}{c_{\Gamma}}=c_{\Gamma^{\prime}}.

Summing over all suitable graphs
\Gamma
completes the proof.
∎

The next lemma is used in the above proof to show that ‘unstable’ boundaries in the sum over all graphs
\Gamma
of a given stabilisation cancel.

Lemma 3.32
.

Let
\Gamma
be a stable map graph and
\Gamma^{\prime}
be obtained from
\Gamma
by an orientation-preserving map
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
, which contracts a single unstable edge. Then,

(3.57)

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\Gamma}\in\mathscr{P}\mathscr{S}^{+}(\Gamma)\end{subarray}}{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in E(\widehat{\Gamma})\\
\widehat{\Gamma}_{e}\cong_{+}\Gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{c_{\Gamma}\,\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}(\cdot)}}\,=\,\sum\limits_{\widehat{\Gamma}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}\mathscr{S}^{+}(\Gamma^{\prime})}{\sum\limits_{\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G}^{+}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})/\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma)}{c_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\,\mathfrak{q}_{\widehat{\Gamma}^{\prime},\sigma}|_{\partial_{\Gamma}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}^{\prime}}}(\cdot)}}

for any analytic chain
\sigma\in C^{an}_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\overline{\Gamma}})
.

Proof.

Note first that

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\Gamma}\in\mathscr{P}\mathscr{S}^{+}(\Gamma)\end{subarray}}{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in E(\widehat{\Gamma})\\
\widehat{\Gamma}_{e}\cong_{+}\Gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}(\cdot)}}=|\mathscr{P}\mathscr{S}^{+}(\Gamma)|\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in E(\Gamma)\\
\Gamma_{e}=\Gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}(\cdot)}

and similarly for the right hand side of Equation (
3.57
). Over the smooth locus, the map
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma,\sigma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}\to\partial_{\Gamma}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\sigma}
is the pullback of a local embedding of degree

d_{1}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})=|\{\phi\in\text{Aut}(\Gamma)\mid f\circ\phi=f\}|

by Theorem
3.15
, where
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is the underlying contraction. (Since the graphs
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
admit a stabilisation, we have
d_{1}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})=1
in this case. But this equation holds more generally.) Since the contracted half-edges have no orientation sign, we have

d_{1}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})=|\{\phi\in\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma)\mid f\circ\phi=f\}|.

Moreover,

(3.58)

d_{2}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})\coloneqq|\{e\mid\Gamma_{e}\cong_{+}\Gamma^{\prime}\}|=|\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma^{\prime})\backslash\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G}_{m}^{+}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})|.\\

Finally, setting
d_{3}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}):=|\mathscr{G}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})/\text{Aut}(\Gamma)|
, we have
d_{3}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})
-many boundary strata of
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
that come from a local embedding of
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
restricted to a face of the cube
I^{E(\Gamma)}
. Since
d_{1}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})
does not depend on the choice of contraction
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
, we have

|\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G}_{m}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})|=|\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G}_{m}^{+}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})/\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma)|\,\frac{|\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma)|}{d_{1}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})}.

Because contractions are surjective, the action of
\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma^{\prime})
on
\mathscr{S}\mathscr{G}_{m}^{+}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})
is free, whence

\frac{d_{1}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})d_{2}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})}{d_{3}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})}=\frac{|\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma)|}{|\text{Aut}^{+}(\Gamma^{\prime})|}.

Together with the first reduction, this allows us to conclude using the equality in (
3.29
) up to sign. Since the difference between
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
are only unstable vertices, their orientations agree, whence the claim follows.
∎

Lemma 3.33
.

Suppose we have submersions
X\to Z
and
Y\to Z
as well as maps
X\to V\times W
, where
X
and
Y
are oriented orbifolds with corners and
Z,V,W
are oriented closed manifolds. Then, equipped with the respective fibre product orientation, the isomorphisms

(3.59)

(V\times X\times W)\times_{V\times Z\times W}Y\;\xrightarrow{\phi}\;X\times_{Z}Y\;\xleftarrow{\varphi}\;X\times_{V\times Z\times W}(V\times Y\times W)

have orientation signs

(3.60)

\epsilon(\phi)=(-1)^{\dim V(\dim X-\dim Z)}\qquad\qquad\epsilon(\varphi)=(-1)^{\dim W(\dim Y-\dim Z)}

Proof.

We use a trick from
[
41
]
to simplify the computation. Since the question is local, we may assume
X=X^{\prime}\times Z
and
Y=Z\times Y^{\prime}
respectively, in which case
X\times_{Z}Y
is oriented as
X^{\prime}\times Y
, respectively
X\times Y^{\prime}
. The signs now follow immediately from the fact that
\dim X^{\prime}=\dim X-\dim Z
.
∎

Lemma 3.34
.

\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma}
satisfies (
3.45
) when
\sigma=D_{0}
or
D_{1}
. Explicitly,

d_{(0,1)}\circ\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}}=-\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}\circ D_{0}}

and

\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}\circ d_{(0,1)}+d_{(0,1)}\circ\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}=-\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}\circ D_{1}}+\mathscr{F}_{D_{2}\circ_{1}D_{0}}-\mathscr{F}_{D_{2}\circ_{2}D_{0}}.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}}=\sum\limits_{\Gamma}{(ev^{+}_{\Gamma})(s_{\Gamma}^{*}\eta_{\Gamma})}
and that, since
|D_{0}|
is even,
d_{(0,1)}(\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}})=d(\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}})
. By Stokes’ formula,

d_{(0,1)}\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}}=d\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}}=0+(-1)^{n}\sum\limits_{\Gamma}{(ev^{+}_{\Gamma}|_{\partial\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}})(s_{\Gamma}^{*}\eta_{\Gamma}|_{\partial\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}})}

where the index set runs over stable map graphs of type
(0,1)
with one outgoing boundary marked point. By the same arguments as in the proof of Lemma
3.31
, we can reduce to computing the sign in the equivalence
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=0\}}\simeq\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{T}\times_{L}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{0}}
, where
e
is an edge of
\Gamma
,
T
is a stable map graph of type
(0,1)
with one incoming and one outgoing boundary marked point and
\Gamma_{0}
is a stable map graph of type
(0,1)
with one outgoing boundary marked point. By our orientation convention, we can in turn reduce this to the sign computation in
[
35
, Theorem 3.5]
, which yields the sign
(-1)^{n+1}
. This could also be obtained by a computation of a diagram as above.
For the second claim, we have

d_{(0,1)}\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}(\alpha)\,=\,(-1)^{|\alpha|+1}d\sum\limits_{\Gamma}{(ev^{+}_{\Gamma})({ev^{-}_{\Gamma}}^{*}(\alpha)\wedge s_{\Gamma}^{*}\eta_{\Gamma})Q^{\beta_{\Gamma}}}\\
=\,-\sum\limits_{\Gamma}{(ev^{+}_{\Gamma})({ev^{-}_{\Gamma}}^{*}(d_{(0,1)}\alpha)\wedge s_{\Gamma}^{*}\eta_{\Gamma})Q^{\beta_{\Gamma}}}\,-\,(-1)^{n+1}\sum\limits_{\Gamma}{(ev^{+}_{\Gamma}|_{\partial\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}})({ev^{-}_{\Gamma}}^{*}(\alpha)\wedge s_{\Gamma}^{*}\eta_{\Gamma}|_{\partial\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}})Q^{\beta_{\Gamma}}.}

By the same argument as before and our orientation convention in (
3.17
) we have to determine the orientation sign between
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{e}=1\}}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{T}\times_{L}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}
, where
T
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
are two graphs obtained from
\Gamma
by cutting the edge
e
and where
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
are single vertices. The graph
T
is of type
(0,1)
with one incoming and one or two outgoing boundary marked points, while
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
has one outgoing marked point and one incoming, respectively no incoming, boundary marked point. We write
e=e_{1}
in the first case and
e=e_{2}
in the second one. In the second case, we also have that the boundary node is either ordered before the incoming and after the outgoing marked point on
\Gamma^{\prime}
, in which case we say
e_{2}
is of type
+
, or it is ordered after the incoming marked point.
[
35
, Theorem 3.5(1)]
yields the signs

•

(-1)^{n+1}
for
e_{1}
,

•

(-1)^{n+1}
for
e_{2}
of type
+
,

•

(-1)^{n}
for
e_{2}
of type
-
.

Write
\mathscr{F}_{D_{2}}=\sum\limits_{\beta}{\mathfrak{q}_{D_{2}}^{\beta}Q^{\beta}}
and
\mathscr{F}_{D_{0}}=\sum_{\beta}\mathfrak{q}_{D_{0}}^{\beta}Q^{\beta}
. With the notation from Definition
3.25
, one has

\displaystyle(-1)^{n+1}\sum\limits_{\Gamma}{(ev^{+}_{\Gamma}|_{\partial\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}})_{*}({ev^{-}_{\Gamma}}^{*}(\alpha)\wedge s_{\Gamma}^{*}\eta_{\Gamma}|_{\partial\mathcal{T}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}})Q^{\beta_{\Gamma}}.}\,

\displaystyle\,=\,\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega(\beta^{\prime})>0\\
\omega(\beta^{\prime\prime})>0\end{subarray}}{\mathfrak{q}_{D_{1}}^{\beta^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{q}_{D_{1}}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}(\alpha))Q^{\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}}}\,+\,\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega(\beta^{\prime})\geq 0\\
\omega(\beta^{\prime\prime})>0\end{subarray}}{\mathfrak{q}_{D_{2}}^{\beta^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{q}_{D_{0}}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}},\alpha)Q^{\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}}}\,-\,\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega(\beta^{\prime})\geq 0\\
\omega(\beta^{\prime\prime})>0\end{subarray}}{\mathfrak{q}_{D_{2}}^{\beta^{\prime}}(\alpha,\mathfrak{q}_{D_{0}}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}})Q^{\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}}}

Since we constructed
\mathscr{F}
to be compatible with
D_{0}
and
D_{1}
, it follows that

\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}\circ d_{(0,1)}+d_{(0,1)}\circ\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}=-\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}\circ D_{1}}+\mathscr{F}_{D_{2}\circ_{1}D_{0}}-\mathscr{F}_{D_{2}\circ_{2}D_{0}}

as claimed.
∎

Lemma 3.35
.

\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma}
satisfies Equation (
3.45
) when
\sigma
is an unstable chain.

Proof.

It follows from Stokes’ theorem and the fact that
L
and
X
are closed that
[d_{y},\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma}]=0=\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\partial_{oc}\sigma}
whenever
\sigma
is a
0
-dimensional unstable simplex, while both sides vanish whenever
\sigma
has higher dimension. It remains thus to consider the case with boundary marked points, where we have to check that
\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\partial_{oc}\sigma}=0
.

•

For the identity, we have
\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\partial_{oc}\sigma}=\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\sigma_{\text{id}}\circ D_{1}}-\mathscr{F}^{1}_{D_{1}\circ\sigma_{\text{id}}}=\mathscr{F}^{1}_{D_{1}}-\mathscr{F}^{1}_{D_{1}}=0
, as required.

•

For the co-unit (
U2
), we have that
\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\partial_{oc}\sigma}(\alpha)=\pm\mathscr{F}_{\sigma\circ D_{1}}(\alpha)=\pm\int_{L}\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}(\alpha)
for any
\alpha\in\Omega^{*}(L)
. We prove this vanishes for each energy
\beta
separately. By definition, the correspondences involved are of the form
L\xleftarrow{}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\text{pt},

where
\Gamma
is a stable map graph of type
(\beta,0,1)
with one incoming and one outgoing boundary marked point. By Theorem
3.15
, this factors through the correspondence
L\xleftarrow{}\mathcal{K}_{\pi_{*}\Gamma}\rightarrow\text{pt}
and is thus degenerate. By Proposition
3.14
(
5
) the associated operation vanishes.

•

For the unit (
U3
),
\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\partial_{oc}\sigma}=\pm\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}\circ\sigma}=\pm\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}(1)
, which vanishes by a similar argument as for the co-unit.

•

For the pairing (
U4
),
\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\partial_{oc}\sigma}(\alpha)=\mathscr{F}_{\sigma\circ(\sigma_{\text{id}}\times D_{1})}(\alpha)+\mathscr{F}_{\sigma\circ(D_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}})}(\alpha)
. For each
\Gamma
contributing to
\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}
, we get two contributions to the above, given by the correspondences

L\times L\xleftarrow{ev^{-}_{\Gamma}\times ev^{+}_{\Gamma}}\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\text{pt}\qquad\text{and}\qquad L\times L\xleftarrow{ev^{+}_{\Gamma}\times ev^{-}_{\Gamma}}\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\text{pt}.

Consider the map
\tau:\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma}
exchanging the two marked points. This is an isomorphism with sign
\text{sign}(\tau)=-1
. Whence the two contributions cancel and
\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\partial_{oc}\sigma}=0
.

•

For the co-pairing (
U5
),
\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\partial_{oc}\sigma}=-\mathscr{F}_{(\sigma_{\text{id}}\times D_{1})\circ\sigma}-\mathscr{F}_{(D_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}})\circ\sigma}
. For each
\Gamma
contributing to
\mathscr{F}_{D_{1}}
, we get two contributions to the above, given by the correspondences

\text{pt}\leftarrow\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma}\xrightarrow{ev^{-}_{\Gamma}\times ev^{+}_{\Gamma}}L\times L\qquad\text{and}\qquad\text{pt}\leftarrow\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma}\xrightarrow{ev^{+}_{\Gamma}\times ev^{-}_{\Gamma}}L\times L.

Again, since they are related by a factor
(-1)
, the associated operations cancel.

∎

We now discuss the
A_{\infty}
functor equation in the case of
d>1
. To make the notation less cluttered, write
T_{\text{id}}
for any (finite) disjoint union of corollas that encode the identity via Definition
3.18
. We call
T_{\text{id}}
an
identity forest
.

For any edge
e\in E(\Gamma_{i})
as in Case (
i
) we have by the computation in Lemma
3.31
and by Lemma
3.19
(
3
)

(3.61)

(\partial_{e}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}})_{\underline{\sigma}}\;=\;(-1)^{\delta_{\eqref{incoming-d}}}\,\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d},\dots,T\sqcup T_{\text{id}},\Gamma^{\prime}_{i},\dots,\Gamma_{1},\sigma_{d},\dots,\text{pt},\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1}}|_{\{s_{i+1}=0\}},

where

\displaystyle\delta_{\eqref{incoming-d}}\;

\displaystyle=\;\epsilon_{\eqref{incoming-d}}+n|y_{o}^{d}|+|\underline{\sigma}^{>i}|^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\;nk+1+n|y_{o}^{d}|+|\underline{\sigma}^{>i}|^{\prime}

with
k
the marked point lying on the unstable tree
T
and
the last term comes from the way we orient
\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}
.
Thus, the associated term in Equation (
3.46
) is

(3.62)

(-1)^{|\alpha|+\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}})}(ev^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\partial_{e}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}})_{*}({ev^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}}^{*}(\alpha)\wedge p_{\underline{\sigma}}^{*}s_{\underline{\Gamma}}^{*}\eta_{\underline{\Gamma}})\\
=\;(-1)^{|\alpha|+|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}}\mathfrak{q}_{(\Gamma_{d},\dots,T\circ_{k}\Gamma_{i},\dots,\Gamma_{1}),(\sigma_{d},\dots,D_{1}\circ_{k}\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1})}(\alpha).

In Case (
ii
), we have

(\partial_{e}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}})_{\underline{\sigma}}\;=\;(-1)^{\delta_{\eqref{outgoing-d}}}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma^{\prime}_{i},T_{\text{id}}\sqcup T^{\prime},\dots,\Gamma_{1};\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{i},D_{1},\dots,\sigma_{1}}|_{\{s_{i}=0\}},

where the exponent of the sign is given by

\delta_{\eqref{outgoing-d}}\;\equiv\;n|y^{d}_{o}|+|\underline{\sigma}^{>i}|^{\prime}+n(|y^{i-1}_{o}|-k)+|\sigma_{i}|.

Together with the sign from (
3.46
), it follows that

(3.63)

(-1)^{|\alpha|+\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}})}(ev^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\partial_{e}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}})_{*}({ev^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}}^{*}(\alpha)\wedge p_{\underline{\sigma}}^{*}s_{\underline{\Gamma}}^{*}\eta_{\underline{\Gamma}})\\
=\;(-1)^{|\alpha|+|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+|\sigma_{i}|+1}\mathfrak{q}_{(\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{i}\circ_{k}T^{\prime},\dots,\Gamma_{1}),(\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{i}\circ_{k}D_{1},\dots,\sigma_{1})}(\alpha).

Finally, in Case (
iii
) we have

\displaystyle(\partial_{e}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}})_{\underline{\sigma}}\;

\displaystyle=\;(-1)^{\delta_{\eqref{curv-d}}}\,\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{i+1},\pi_{j}^{*}\Gamma^{\prime}_{i},T_{\text{id}}\sqcup T^{\prime\prime},\Gamma_{i-1},\dots,\Gamma_{1}}|_{\{s_{i}=0\}},

\displaystyle=\;(-1)^{\delta_{\eqref{curv-d}}}\,\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{i+1},\pi_{j}^{*}\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}\circ_{*}T^{\prime\prime},\Gamma_{i-1},\dots,\Gamma_{1}},

where the left hand side is equipped with the boundary orientation,

\delta_{\eqref{curv-d}}\;=\;1+n|y^{d}_{o}|+|\underline{\sigma}^{\geq i}|^{\prime}

and
\pi_{j}^{*}\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}
is the graph
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}
with an additional boundary marked point ‘after’ the boundary marked point labelled by
j
.
This sign follows from the same argument as was used to compute the exponent
\epsilon_{\eqref{curv-d}}
in Equation (
3.54
). However, the horizontal map in the diagram above said equation comes with the sign
(-1)^{n|y^{d}_{o}|+1+\sum_{j=i}^{d}|\sigma_{j}|^{\prime}}
because the isomorphism (
3.4
) has this sign. Thus, the corresponding operation is

(3.64)

(-1)^{|\alpha|+\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}})}(ev^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\partial_{e}\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}})_{*}({ev^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}}^{*}(\alpha)\wedge p_{\underline{\sigma}}^{*}s_{\underline{\Gamma}}^{*}\eta_{\underline{\Gamma}})\\
=\;(-1)^{|\alpha|+|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+1}\mathfrak{q}_{(\Gamma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\Gamma_{i}\circ_{*}T^{\prime\prime},\dots,\Gamma_{1}),(\sigma_{d},\dots,(-1)^{|\sigma_{i}|}\pi^{*}\sigma_{i}\circ_{*}D_{0},\dots,\sigma_{1})}(\alpha).

Using the definition of the
A_{\infty}
structure on
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}
from Definition
2.22
, the relations of (
3.45
) become

(-1)^{|\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}|}(d_{y^{d}}\circ\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}-(-1)^{|\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}|}\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}\circ d_{y^{0}})+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}{(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+1}\mathscr{F}^{d-i}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{i+1}}\circ\mathscr{F}^{i}_{\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1}}}\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{d}(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}}\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\partial\sigma_{i}-D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,\sigma+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\sigma\,\widehat{\circ}\,D_{1}+(-1)^{|\sigma|}\pi^{*}\sigma\circ_{*}D_{0},\dots,\sigma_{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}{(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}}\mathscr{F}^{d-1}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\sigma_{i+1}\circ\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1}}}=0.

On the other hand, multiplying Equation (
3.46
) with
(-1)^{|\alpha|}
yields

(-1)^{|\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}|}(d_{y^{d}}\mathfrak{q}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)\ -(-1)^{|\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}|}\ \mathfrak{q}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}(d_{y^{0}}\alpha))\ +\ (-1)^{1+\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}})}(ev^{+}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\partial\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}})_{*}({ev^{-}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}}^{*}(\alpha)\wedge p_{\underline{\sigma}}^{*}s_{\underline{\Gamma}}^{*}\eta_{\underline{\Gamma}})\ =\ 0.

Thus, summing over all suitable sequences
\underline{\Gamma}
of graphs, the last term becomes

\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+1}\mathscr{F}^{d-i}_{\underline{\sigma}^{>i}}(\mathscr{F}^{i}_{\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}}(\alpha))+\sum_{i=1}^{d}(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}}\mathscr{F}^{d}_{(\sigma_{d},\dots,\partial_{oc}\sigma,\dots,\sigma_{1})}(\alpha)+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}}\mathscr{F}^{d-1}_{*_{i}\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)

by Equation (
3.4
) for the first term, Equations (
3.48
),(
3.62
), (
3.63
) and (
3.64
) for the second term and Equation (
3.4
) for the last term.
∎

3.5.
Symmetric monoidal structure

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
3.1
by showing that
\mathscr{F}
admits a symmetric monoidal structure. The strategy of the proof is to define the required natural transformations using certain loci inside the hypercubes
I^{d-1}
over which the (global Kuranishi charts defining the) higher homotopies live.

Proposition 3.36
.

The
A_{\infty}
functor
\mathscr{F}_{L}
is unital symmetric monoidal.

Proof.

The first observation is that for our curved open-closed DMFT, the terms in Equation (
2.13
) and Equation (
2.14
) are given by correspondences obtained from certain loci inside the product
\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}
, which is equipped with a canonical map to
I^{2(d-1)}
.
To this end, define the subspaces

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}

\displaystyle=\{0\leq s_{k}\leq t_{k}\leq 1\}

\displaystyle D_{k}

\displaystyle=\{0\leq s_{k}=t_{k}\leq 1\}

\displaystyle C_{k}

\displaystyle=\{s_{k}=0\}\cup\{t_{k}=1\}

\displaystyle\bm{1}_{k}

\displaystyle=\{s_{k}=t_{k}=1\}

\displaystyle\bm{0}_{k}

\displaystyle=\{s_{k}=t_{k}=0\}

of
I^{2}
with coordinates
s_{k},t_{k}
, which are real analytic submanifolds with corners.
In particular,

\displaystyle\partial\Delta_{k}

\displaystyle=C_{k}\sqcup D_{k}

(3.65)

\displaystyle\partial C_{k}=\partial D_{k}

\displaystyle=\bm{1}_{k}\sqcup\bm{0}_{k}.

Notation 3.37
.

Given an object
y
of
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}
, we write
\mathfrak{o}(y)=\mathfrak{o}(X^{y_{c}}\times L^{y_{o}})
.

Definition 3.38
.

The pre-natural transformation
R^{d}
is defined by

R^{0}(\alpha\otimes\beta)\;=\;(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}\alpha\times\beta,

where
\times
is the usual cross product of differential forms, by
R^{1}=0
, and for
d\geq 2
by

R^{d}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes\underline{\tau})(\alpha\otimes\beta)=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}\sum_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}c_{\underline{\Gamma}}c_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}(\alpha\times\beta)

where
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}=\sqcup_{i}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}(i)
is the disjoint union of the correspondences

\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}(i)\,:=\,\left(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}\right)\times_{I^{2(d-1)}}\left(D_{d-1}\times\dots\times D_{i+1}\times\Delta_{i}\times C_{i-1}\times\dots\times C_{1}\right)

for
1\leq i\leq d-1
, oriented by the isomorphism

(3.66)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}(i))

\displaystyle\;\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(x^{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(y^{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(D_{d-1})\dots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{i+1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(t_{i})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{i})\dots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(C_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(s_{i}).

Lemma
3.41
asserts that
R
is a natural transformation.
To simplify the notation, abbreviate

\mathscr{G}(\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau})\,\coloneqq\,\otimes_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{F})(\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau})

and

\mathscr{H}(\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau})\,\coloneqq\,\mathscr{F}\circ\otimes_{\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}}(\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau})=\sum\limits_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}{c_{\underline{\Gamma}}c_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}}\,\mathfrak{q}_{\underline{\Gamma}\sqcup\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\sigma}\times\underline{\tau}}

for composable sequences in
\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}
. Roughly, these functors are induced by the operations associated to the following correspondences.

Definition 3.39
.

Define the correspondences

\mathcal{D}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}\,=\,\left(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}\right)\times_{I^{2(d-1)}}\Bigl(D_{d-1}\times\dots\times D_{1}\Bigr)

oriented via the isomorphism

(3.67)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{D}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})\,\cong\,\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(x^{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(y^{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(D_{d-1})\cdots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(D_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{1}),

and

\mathcal{C}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}:=\left(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}\right)\times_{I^{2(d-1)}}\left(C_{d-1}\times\dots\times C_{1}\right),

oriented by

(3.68)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{C}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})\,\cong\,\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(x^{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(y^{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(C_{d-1})\cdots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(C_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{1}).

Lemma 3.40
.

As maps
\Omega^{*}(X^{x^{0}_{c}}\times L^{x^{0}_{o}};\Lambda)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Omega^{*}(X^{y^{0}_{c}}\times L^{y^{0}_{o}};\Lambda)\rightarrow\Omega^{*}(X^{x^{d}_{c}+y^{d}_{c}}\times L^{x^{d}_{o}+y^{d}_{o}};\Lambda)
, we have that

(3.69)

\mathscr{H}(\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau})\circ R^{0}_{x^{0},y^{0}}(\alpha\otimes\beta)=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}\sum\limits_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}{c_{\underline{\Gamma}}c_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}}\,\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{D}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}(\alpha\times\beta)

and

(3.70)

R^{0}_{x^{d},y^{d}}\circ\mathscr{G}(\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau})(\alpha\otimes\beta)=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}\sum\limits_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}{c_{\underline{\Gamma}}c_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}}\,\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{C}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}(\alpha\times\beta)

More generally

R^{d-j}(\underline{\sigma}^{>j},\underline{\tau}^{>j})\circ\mathscr{G}^{j}(\underline{\sigma}^{\leq j},\underline{\tau}^{\leq j})=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}\sum\limits_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}{c_{\underline{\Gamma}}c_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}}\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{>j},\underline{\sigma}^{>j};(\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime})^{>j},\underline{\tau}^{>j}}}\circ\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{C}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\leq j},\underline{\sigma}^{\leq j};(\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime})^{\leq j},\underline{\tau}^{\leq j}}}(\alpha\times\beta)

Proof.

The map
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}\to I^{d-1}
is a submersion due to the fact that the parametrised fibre products in the definition of
\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
are by submersions. Thus, the fibre products in Definition
3.39
are well-defined correspondences required in Definition
3.10
and we can associate the operation
\mathfrak{q}
to it.
The first claim then follows from the construction of the higher homotopies (Lemma
3.19
), the way they are oriented, cf. (
3.42
), and the definition of
R^{0}
.
To prove the second claim, fix sequences
\underline{\Gamma}
and
\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
of graphs lying over
\underline{\sigma}
and
\underline{\tau}
respectively. We have to show
