\sum_{\underline{p}}(-1)^{\dagger(|\underline{\sigma}+\underline{p}|,|\underline{\tau}+\underline{p}^{c}|)}R^{0}_{x^{d},y^{d}}\circ\mathfrak{q}_{\ast_{\underline{p}}\underline{\Gamma},\ast_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma}}\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\mathfrak{q}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}(\alpha\otimes\beta)=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{C}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}(\alpha\times\beta)

where we define
\mathfrak{q}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}
analogous to how we defined
\mathscr{F}^{\circ\underline{p}}
.
Fix a sequence
\underline{p}=(0=p_{0}<p_{1}<\dots<p_{r}<p_{r+1}=d)
. Define
\underline{\tau}^{(i)}:=(\tau_{p_{i}},\dots,\tau_{p_{i-1}+1})
for
i\leq r+1
and similarly for
\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
. Then,
\mathfrak{q}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}
is the operation associated to the correspondence

\mathcal{K}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}\;:=\;\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime(r+1)},\underline{\tau}^{(r+1)}}\times_{Y^{y_{p_{r}}}}\dots\times_{Y^{y_{p_{1}}}}\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime(1)},\underline{\tau}^{(1)}}

carrying the cross product of Thom forms.

On the other hand, set
\mathcal{C}^{\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}:=\left(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}\right)\times_{I^{2(d-1)}}C^{\underline{p}}
, where

C^{\underline{p}}:=\left\{x\in C_{d-1}\times\dots\times C_{1}\mid\forall i\in\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\}:t_{i}=1,\;\forall j\notin\{p_{1},\dots,p_{r}\}:s_{j}=0\right\}.

Since the canonical isomorphism
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(X\times_{Z}Y)\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(X)\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Z)^{\vee}\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y)
yields the fibre product orientation, the isomorphism

\mathcal{K}_{\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\Gamma},\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}\;\cong\;\mathcal{C}^{\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}

has orientation sign
(-1)^{|y^{d}||\mathscr{F}_{\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma}}|+\dagger(|\underline{\sigma}+\underline{p}|,|\underline{\tau}+\underline{p}^{c}|)}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle R^{0}_{x^{d},y^{d}}\circ\left(\mathscr{F}(\ast_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma})\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\mathscr{F}^{\circ\underline{p}}(\underline{\tau})\right)(\alpha\otimes\beta)

\displaystyle=R^{0}_{x^{d},y^{d}}\circ\left(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{K}_{\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\Gamma},\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma}}}\otimes\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{K}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}\right)(\alpha\otimes\beta)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\epsilon_{1}}\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{K}_{\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\Gamma},\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma}}}(\alpha)\times\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{K}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}(\beta).

where
\epsilon_{1}=(|\mathscr{F}_{\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma}}|+|\alpha|)(|\mathscr{F}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\tau}}|+|\beta|)+|\mathscr{F}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\tau}}||\alpha|
.
It follows from the properties of the pushforward, that

(f\times g)_{*}(\alpha\times\beta)=(-1)^{\text{rdim}(f)(\dim Z-|\beta|)}f_{*}(\alpha)\times g_{*}(\beta)

for any submersions
f
and
g\colon Z\to Z^{\prime}
. We thus find that

(3.71)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{K}_{\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\Gamma},\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma}}}(\alpha)\times\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{K}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}(\beta)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\epsilon_{2}}\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{K}_{\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\Gamma},\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}(\alpha\times\beta),

where
\epsilon_{2}=|\mathscr{F}_{\star_{\underline{p}}\underline{\sigma}}|(|y_{d}|+|\mathscr{F}^{\circ\underline{p}}_{\underline{\tau}}|+|\beta|)
. Combining all previous equations yields the second equality. The third equation follows by a very similar computation.
∎

Lemma 3.41
.

R
defines a natural transformation of degree
0
from
\mathscr{G}
to
\mathscr{H}
.

Proof.

Abbreviate
\underline{a}:=(\sigma_{d}\otimes\tau_{d},\dots,\sigma_{1}\otimes\tau_{1})
. Then, Equation (
2.9
) becomes

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\underline{a}|^{\prime}}\Bigl(d_{x^{d}\sqcup y^{d}}\circ R^{d}(\underline{a})-(-1)^{|\underline{a}|^{\prime}}

\displaystyle R^{d}(\underline{a})\circ d_{x^{0},y^{0}}\Bigr)\;=\;-\sum_{j=0}^{d}(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}}\mathscr{H}(\underline{a}^{>j})\circ R^{j}(\underline{a}^{\leq j})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{d}R^{d-j}(\underline{a}^{>j})\circ\mathscr{G}(\underline{a}^{\leq j})+\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}}R^{d-1}(\star_{j}\underline{a})

(3.72)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{d}(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}}\Bigl(R^{d}((\partial^{oc}_{j}\underline{\sigma})\otimes\underline{\tau})+(-1)^{|\sigma_{j}|}R^{d}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes(\partial^{oc}_{j}\underline{\tau}))\Bigr)=0.

Since
d_{y}(\alpha)=(-1)^{|\alpha|}d\alpha
by definition,
R^{0}
is a chain map, which shows the claim for
d=0
.
For
d=1
, the equation becomes

\mathscr{H}(\sigma\otimes\tau)\circ R^{0}=R^{0}\circ\mathscr{G}(\sigma\otimes\tau).

Let
\underline{\Gamma}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
be graphs as ‘lying’ over the respective simplices
\sigma
and
\tau
. Given the orientations determined by the isomorphism (
3.42
), we have

\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}\sqcup\Gamma^{\prime},\sigma\times\rho}\;=\;(-1)^{n|y^{\prime}_{o}||\sigma|}\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\rho}.

It follows from the properties of the pushforward, that

(f\times g)_{*}(\alpha\times\beta)=(-1)^{\text{rdim}(f)(\dim Z-|\beta|)}f_{*}(\alpha)\times g_{*}(\beta)

for any submersions
f
and
g\colon Z\to Z^{\prime}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma\sqcup\Gamma^{\prime},\sigma\times\rho}(R^{0}_{x,y}(\alpha\otimes\beta))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma\sqcup\Gamma^{\prime},\sigma\times\rho}(\alpha\times\beta)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|+n|y^{\prime}_{o}||\sigma|}(\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}\times\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\rho})(\alpha\times\beta)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|+(|\rho|+|\beta|)\sigma|}\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}(\alpha)\times\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\rho}(\beta)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|+(|\rho|+|\beta|)|\sigma|+(|\alpha|+|\sigma|)(|\beta|+|\rho|)}R^{0}_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}(\alpha)\otimes\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\rho}(\beta))

\displaystyle=R^{0}_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}\circ(\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma,\sigma}\otimes\mathfrak{q}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\rho})(\alpha\otimes\beta)

as required.

Suppose now
d\geq 2
and fix
1\leq i\leq d
as well as sequences of graphs
\underline{\Gamma}
lying over
\underline{\sigma}
and
\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
lying over
\underline{\tau}
. By Stokes’ Theorem, we have

(3.73)

(-1)^{|\gamma|}d\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{R}^{d}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}(\gamma)\;=\;(-1)^{|\gamma|}\mathfrak{q}_{\mathcal{R}^{d}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}(d\gamma)\,+\,(-1)^{\text{vdim}(\mathcal{R}^{d}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})}\mathfrak{q}_{\partial\mathcal{R}^{d}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}}(\gamma).

The boundary is given by

\displaystyle\partial\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}(i)\;

\displaystyle=\;\partial^{v}(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})\times_{I^{2(d-1)}}\left(D_{d-1}\times\dots\times D_{i+1}\times\Delta_{i}\times C_{i-1}\times\dots\times C_{1}\right)

(3.74)

\displaystyle\sqcup(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})\times_{I^{2(d-1)}}\partial\Bigl(D_{d-1}\times\dots\times D_{i+1}\times\Delta_{i}\times C_{i-1}\times\dots\times C_{1}\Bigr)

as unoriented correspondences by
[
41
]
. The first component of this disjoint union is similar to the terms determined in §
3.4
and it yields the last three terms of (
3.5
). Observe that

\text{vdim}(\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})\equiv\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}\sqcup\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{a}})+1\equiv|\underline{a}|^{\prime}+n|x^{d}_{o}|+n|y^{d}_{o}|,

which in particular implies that
R
has degree
0
. Summing over all suitable sequences of graphs, the last term in Equation (
3.73
) gives rise to the following operations, due to the way we orient
\mathcal{R}^{d}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}
.

I)

Using the same computation as below Equation (
3.47
), we see that the first component in (
3.5
) yields one one hand the operations
(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}}R^{d}(\partial_{j}\underline{a})
from the boundary of the simplices
\sigma_{j}
and
\tau_{j}
. On the other hand, it yields the terms

•

(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}}R^{d}((-D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,_{j}\underline{\sigma})\otimes\underline{\tau})
and
(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}+|\sigma_{j}|}R^{d}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes(-D_{1}\,\widehat{\circ}\,_{j}\underline{\tau}))
by the same computation as before Equation (
3.62
)

•

(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}}R^{d}((-1)^{|\sigma_{j}|}(\underline{\sigma}\,\widehat{\circ}\,_{j}D_{1})\otimes\underline{\tau})
and
(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}+|\sigma_{j}|}R^{d}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes((-1)^{|\tau_{j}|}\underline{\tau}\,\widehat{\circ}\,_{j}D_{1}))
by the same computation as before Equation (
3.63
)

•

(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}}R^{d}((\sigma_{d},\dots,(-1)^{|\sigma_{j}|}\pi^{*}\sigma_{j}\circ_{*}D_{0},\dots,\sigma_{1})\otimes\underline{\tau})
as well as

(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}+|\sigma_{j}|}R^{d}(\underline{\sigma}\otimes(\tau_{d},\dots,(-1)^{|\tau_{j}|}\pi^{*}\tau_{j}\circ_{*}D_{0},\dots,\tau_{1}))
by the same computation as before Equation (
3.64
).

The remaining operations associated to boundary strata in
\partial^{v}(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})
cancel by Lemma
3.32
.

II)

We now turn to the remaining components of the boundary. For
j>i
, we have
\partial D_{j}=\textbf{1}_{j}-\textbf{0}_{j}
by Equation (
3.5
) so that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})\times_{I^{2(d-1)}}\Bigl(D_{d-1}\times\dots\partial D_{j}\times\dots\times D_{i+1}\times\Delta_{i}\times C_{i-1}\times\dots\times C_{1}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\{s_{j}=1\}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}|_{\{t_{j}=1\}})\times_{I^{2(d-2)}}\Bigl(D_{d-1}\times\dots\widehat{D_{j}}\times\dots\times D_{i+1}\times\Delta_{i}\times C_{i-1}\times\dots\times C_{1}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\;\;\sqcup(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\{s_{j}=0\}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}|_{\{t_{j}=0\}})\times_{I^{2(d-2)}}\Bigl(D_{d-1}\times\dots\widehat{D_{j}}\times\dots\times D_{i+1}\times\Delta_{i}\times C_{i-1}\times\dots\times C_{1}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\,\mathcal{D}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{>j},\underline{\sigma}^{>j};(\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime})^{>j},\underline{\tau}^{>j}}\times_{Y_{x^{j}}\times Y_{y^{j}}}\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\leq j},\underline{\sigma}^{\leq j};(\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime})^{\leq j},\underline{\tau}^{\leq j}}(i)\,\sqcup\,\mathcal{R}_{\ast_{j}\underline{\Gamma},\ast_{j}\underline{\sigma};\ast_{j}\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\ast_{j}\underline{\tau}}(i)

as unoriented correspondences. The sign in front of the first operation is
(-1)^{\epsilon_{1}}
, where

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv|\underline{a}|^{\prime}+n|x^{d}_{o}|+n|y^{d}_{o}|+|\underline{a}^{>j}|^{\prime}+1+n|x^{d}_{o}|+n|y^{d}_{o}|\mod{2}

\displaystyle\equiv|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}+1.

The second operation on the right hand side appears with sign
(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}}
,
where the extra
+1
is from the fact that it occurs as
\textbf{0}_{j}
occurs with sign
-1
in
\partial D_{j}
.

III)

For
j<i
, we have similarly
\partial C_{j}=\textbf{1}_{j}-\textbf{0}_{j}
so that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})\times_{I^{2(d-1)}}\Bigl(D_{d-1}\times\dots\times D_{i+1}\times\Delta_{i}\times C_{i-1}\times\dots\times\partial C_{j}\times\dots\times C_{1}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\,\mathcal{R}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{>j},\underline{\sigma}^{>j};(\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime})^{>j},\underline{\tau}^{>j}}(i)\times_{Y_{x^{j}}\times Y_{y^{j}}}\mathcal{C}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\leq j},\underline{\sigma}^{\leq j};(\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime})^{\leq j},\underline{\tau}^{\leq j}}\,\sqcup\,\mathcal{R}_{\ast_{j}\underline{\Gamma},\ast_{j}\underline{\sigma};\ast_{j}\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\ast_{j}\underline{\tau}}(i)

as unoriented correspondences. The sign for the first operation is
(-1)^{\epsilon_{1}}
with

\epsilon_{1}\,\equiv\,|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}+1+|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}+1\,\equiv\,0.

The term
|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}+1
comes from the fact that we have to move
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(s_{i})
forward appear before
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{j})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{j})
. The sign in front of the second operation is
(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}}
.

IV)

The final component is

(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})\times_{I^{2(d-1)}}\Bigl(D_{d-1}\times\dots\times D_{i+1}\times(C_{i}\sqcup D_{i})\times C_{i-1}\times\dots\times C_{1}\Bigr)

This yields the remaining terms by Lemma
3.40
. To understand, the sign we use the coordinates
s_{i}
and
t_{i}
to orient
\Delta_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Observe that the canonical isomorphism

\quad\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{R}^{d}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}(i))\;\cong\;\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(x^{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(y^{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(D_{d-1})\dots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{i+1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(s_{i})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(t_{i})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{i})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{i})\dots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(C_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\tau_{1})

has sign
(-1)^{|\underline{a}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}+1+1}
.
The normal vectors to the one-dimensional strata of
C_{i}
are given by
t_{i}
and
-s_{i}
respectively, while the normal vector to
D_{i}
is given by
s_{i}-t_{i}
.
The term with
D_{i}
thus appears with the sign
(-1)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
in Equation (
3.73
), where

\epsilon^{\prime}\,\equiv\,|\underline{a}|^{\prime}+n|x^{d}_{o}|+n|y^{d}_{o}|+|\underline{a}^{>i}|^{\prime}+1+n|x^{d}_{o}|+n|y^{d}_{o}|+|\underline{a}^{\leq i}|^{\prime}\,\equiv\,1\mod{2}.

Similarly the term with
C_{i}
appears with the sign
1
.
Summing over
1\leq i\leq d-1
, we are left with the operations coming from the correspondences
\mathcal{D}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma};\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}
, appearing
with signs
-1
and
1
respectively.

The result then follows by summing over all terms and using Lemma
3.40
. ∎

By switching the roles of
s
and
t
in the definition of the loci
D_{i},C_{i}
and
\Delta_{i}
, we obtain a natural transformation
L
between
\mathscr{F}^{d}\circ\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\otimes_{\mathscr{D}}\circ(\mathscr{F}\overline{\otimes}\mathscr{F})
as required. It follows from the construction that this is symmetric. By the same argument as in Lemma
3.28
, one shows that the defining correspondence is degenerate whenever
\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{\text{id}}
or
\tau_{i}=\sigma_{\text{id}}
for
1\leq i\leq d
. To prove that
\mathscr{F}
is unital symmetric monoidal, let
\underline{\sigma}
,
\underline{\tau}
be such that for each
i
either
\sigma_{i}
or
\tau_{i}
is the identity morphism. By the proof of Lemma
3.28
, the correspondence
\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\tau}}
factors through a correspondence of codimension
d
. The correspondence used to define
R^{d}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}
is of codimension
d-1
in
\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\tau}}
, and thus factors through a correspondence of codimension
1
, so it is degenerate. Hence
R^{d}_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\tau}}=0
for such
\underline{\sigma}
,
\underline{\tau}
.
∎

We can now prove that
\mathscr{F}
has the property required for showing that deformations by a bounding cochain lead to an uncurved open-closed DMFT.

Lemma 3.42
.

\mathscr{F}
satisfies Property
2.42
.

Proof.

We only have to consider the case where
\sigma_{i}
is stable or
\sigma_{i}=\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\circ D_{j}
for
j=0,1
.
In the either case, Property (
1
) follows from the fact that forgetting an incoming marked point does not change stabilisation patterns, whence

\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}}}(R^{0}(\alpha\otimes e_{\mathscr{F}}))\;=\;\pm\,\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\sigma_{i},\dots,\sigma_{1}}(\alpha)=0

due to the degeneracy condition. For the second property, we have to show that

(3.75)

\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i-1}\times D_{k},\dots,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}}}\circ R^{0}\;=\;(-1)^{k|\underline{\sigma}^{<i}|^{\prime}}\mathscr{F}^{d}_{\sigma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i-1}\times D_{k},\dots,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{\text{id}}}\circ R^{0}\circ(\text{id}\otimes\mathscr{F}_{D_{k}}(\gamma)).

By Lemma
3.19
, this reduces to showing that for graphs
\Gamma_{i}
lying over
\sigma_{i}
and
\Gamma
lying over
D_{k}
we have an isomorphism

(3.76)

\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{(\Gamma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\Gamma_{i},\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma_{i-1},\dots,\Gamma_{1})}|_{\{s_{i}=1\}}\;\cong\;\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{(\Gamma_{d},\dots,\pi^{*}\Gamma_{i},\dots,\Gamma_{1})}\times_{L}\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}

of (unoriented) correspondences, where we have suppressed the identity trees from the subscript. We may assume without loss of generality that
\Gamma_{d}\circ\dots\circ(\pi^{*}\Gamma_{i}\circ_{*}\Gamma^{\prime})\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{1}
is connected. Write
\underline{\widehat{\Gamma}}
for the sequence of graphs on the left hand side of (
3.76
) and
\underline{\widetilde{\Gamma}}
for the sequence of graphs on the right hand side, that is, without
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Since
s_{i}=1
, the maps
\chi^{\pm}_{\underline{\widehat{\Gamma}}}
factor through the map
p\colon I^{d}\to I^{d-1}
that forgets the
s_{i}
coordinate (and renumbers the other coordinates).
We have to show that for an incoming marked point
\kappa
of
\Gamma_{1}
and an outgoing marked point
\kappa^{\prime}
of
\Gamma_{d}
, we have
\chi^{-}_{\underline{\widehat{\Gamma}};\kappa}(s)=\chi^{-}_{\underline{\widetilde{\Gamma}};\kappa}(p(s))
and
\chi^{+}_{\underline{\widehat{\Gamma}};\kappa^{\prime}}(s)=\chi^{+}_{\underline{\widetilde{\Gamma}};\kappa^{\prime}}(p(s))
.
Observe that one only has to consider the case where
\Gamma_{i}
has a stable component since both sides of (
3.75
) vanish otherwise. If
\Gamma_{j}\circ\dots\circ\pi^{*}\Gamma_{i}
is a stable map graph for any
i\leq j\leq d
, then the claim follows from the fact that we constructed the global Kuranishi charts to be compatible with stable clutching and that
\Gamma_{j}\circ\dots\circ(\pi^{*}\Gamma_{i}\circ_{*}\Gamma^{\prime})
is always a stable map graph.
This is in particular the case if
\Gamma_{i}
has positive energy. If
\Gamma_{i}
has zero energy, then forgetting outgoing marked points (or clutching them with another marked point) does not change the stabilisation pattern because
\int_{X}\alpha=\int_{TX}\pi_{TX}^{*}\alpha\wedge\eta_{X}
. Forgetting incoming marked points also does not change the stabilisation patter of
\Gamma_{i}
in this case and the remaining case to consider is if
\Gamma_{i-1}\circ\dots\circ\Gamma_{1}
contains a copairing corolla that interacts with
\Gamma_{i}
. But then, the respective component of
\pi^{*}\Gamma_{i}\circ\Gamma_{i-1}
either remains stable or loses an incoming marked point. In either case, composing
\pi^{*}\Gamma_{i}
with
\Gamma^{\prime}
at the added marked point does not change the stabilisation pattern, whence we obtain the isomorphism in (
3.76
).
The sign follows from the definition of the orientation of the higher homotopies, Equation (
3.42
).
∎

3.6.
Independence

In this section we can prove independence up to homotopy.

Theorem 3.43
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be closed and
L\subset X
be an embedded relatively spin Lagrangian.

(1)

The curved open-closed DMFT of Theorem
3.1
is independent of the choice of auxiliary data up to homotopy of curved open-closed DMFTs.

(2)

The open-closed DMFT of Corollary
3.2
is independent of the choice of the gauge equivalence class of weak bounding cochain and auxiliary data up to homotopy of open-closed DMFTs.

We are only defining the notion of gauge equivalence of bounding cochains in our specific context. To this end, recall that any curved open-closed DMFT
\mathscr{F}
given by Theorem
3.1
satisfies
\mathscr{F}_{L}(y)=\Omega^{*}(X^{y_{c}}\times L^{y_{o}};\Lambda)
. Thus, its associated curved
A_{\infty}
algebra
\mathscr{A}
has underlying vector space
\Omega^{*}(L;\Lambda)
and we define gauge equivalence for such
A_{\infty}
algebras.

Definition 3.44
.

Two weak bounding cochains
\mathfrak{b}_{0}
for
\mathscr{A}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{1}
for
\mathscr{A}_{1}
are
gauge-equivalent
if there exists a pseudo-isotopy, that is, an
A_{\infty}
structure on
\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}:=\Omega^{*}(L\times[0,1];\Lambda)
, from
\mathscr{A}_{0}
to
\mathscr{A}_{1}
and a weak bounding cochain
\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}
for
\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}
such that
j_{i}^{*}\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}=\mathfrak{b}_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
, where
j_{i}\colon L\hookrightarrow L\times[0,1]
is the inclusion at
\{i\}
.

Proof.

We recall first the choices that are required for the construction of the curved DMFT, in the logical dependency in which they have to be made. The first two choices are

(1)

an
\omega
-tame almost complex structure
J
.

(2)

an unobstructed auxiliary datum
\alpha_{\mathsf{a}}
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}^{J}(X,L)
as in
[
35
, Definition 2.20]
for each
\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
.

Given this, Theorem
3.15
yields a system
\{\mathcal{K}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\}_{\Gamma}
of cubical cobordisms, with Lemma
3.19
providing the global Kuranishi charts for the higher homotopies. The final choice is a system of compatible Thom forms, guaranteed by Proposition
3.16
.
We first discuss the case where we fix some tame
J
but choose the auxiliary data and the Thom forms arbitrarily.
Then, Theorem
3.1
yields two curved open-closed DMFTs
\mathscr{F}^{0}
and
\mathscr{F}^{1}
.
By definition, they agree on objects.
Write
\mathcal{K}^{i,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
for the global Kuranishi chart of Theorem
3.15
, respectively Lemma
3.19
, used to define
\mathscr{F}^{i}
.
We introduce the following definition.

Definition 3.45
.

Let
Y
be a smooth manifold, possibly with corners. A
system of cubical cobordisms over
Y
is a system
\{\mathcal{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\}_{\Gamma}
of global Kuranishi charts equipped with submersions to
Y\times I^{E(\Gamma)}
so that the properties of Theorem
3.15
hold with fibre products over
X^{k}\times L^{\ell}
replaced by fibre products over
Y\times X^{k}\times L^{\ell}
.

Lemma 3.46
.

There exists a system
\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\bullet}=\{\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\}_{\Gamma}
of cubical cobordisms over
[0,1]
so that
\mathcal{K}^{i,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}\simeq\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}|_{\{i\}}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
, where these equivalences are functorial with the equivalences of the system. We can equip it with a system of Thom forms extending the Thom forms on
\mathcal{K}^{0,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\bullet}
and
\mathcal{K}^{1,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

We first recall that
\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\Gamma}
is obtained from a double sum thickening constructed in
[
35
, Proposition 2.57]
by not adding the perturbation terms coming from
\alpha^{0}
and
\alpha^{1}
but taking their convex sum over
[0,1]
as in the proof of
[
35
, Theorem 4.1]
, where this step is explained in detail. Then, the constructions in said proof carry over verbatim, except for replacing fibre products over
X^{k}\times L^{\ell}
by fibre products over
I\times X^{k}\times L^{\ell}
.
∎

The proof of Lemma
3.19
now carries over verbatim to give correspondences
\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
equipped with submersion to
I\times I^{d-1}
as well as Thom forms for composable sequences
\underline{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{1})
of graphs.
However, the restriction
\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}|_{\{s_{i}=1\}}
is given by

\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{>i}}\times_{I\times X^{y^{i}_{c}}\times L^{y^{i}_{o}}}\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\leq i}}

instead of

(3.77)

\mathcal{K}^{1}_{(\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{i+1})}\times_{Y^{+}_{\Gamma_{i}}}\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{(\Gamma_{i},\dots,\Gamma_{1})}\;\sqcup\;\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{(\Gamma_{d},\dots,\Gamma_{i+1})}\times_{Y^{+}_{\Gamma_{i}}}\mathcal{K}^{0}_{(\Gamma_{i},\dots,\Gamma_{1})},

which would be the required boundary structure for a homotopy of open-closed DMFTs.
However, these global Kuranishi charts also appear as boundary strata of

(3.78)

(\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{>i}}\times_{Y^{+}_{i}}\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\leq i}})\times_{I^{2}}\left\{(t_{1},t_{0})|t_{1}\geq t_{0}\right\},

where
t_{i}
denotes the parameter
t
on each factor. By adding these correction terms, we will define a homotopy between
\mathscr{F}^{0}
and
\mathscr{F}^{1}
.

To this end, define for a tuple
\textbf{i}=(d\geq i_{r}>\dots>i_{1}\geq 1)
the global Kuranishi chart

\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma}}\;\coloneqq\;\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{>i_{r}}}\times_{Y^{+}_{i_{r}}}\dots\times_{Y^{+}_{i_{1}}}\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\leq i_{1}}}\,\times_{I^{r+1}}\left\{t\in I^{r+1}:t_{r}\geq\dots\geq t_{0}\right\}.

If
i
is the empty sequence, we simply have
\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\underline{\Gamma}}
.
Given a composable sequence
\underline{\sigma}
of analytic simplices and a sequence
\underline{\Gamma}
of graphs lying over it, equip
\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}
with the orientation

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}})\cong\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(Y_{d}^{+})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(s_{d-1})\dots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(s_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(t).

and

\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}:=\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma}}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\underline{\overline{\Gamma}}}}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\Delta^{\lVert\sigma_{j}\rVert}

with the fibre product orientation.
In other words, its orientation line is given by

(3.79)

\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma}})\;\cong\;\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(y^{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{d})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(s_{d-1})\dots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{i_{r}+1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(t_{r})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{i_{1}+1})\dots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(t_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{i_{1}})\dots\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(\sigma_{1})\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(t_{0}),

where we use the notation
\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(y_{d})=\hskip 0.8pt\mathfrak{o}(X^{y^{d}_{c}}\times L^{y^{d}_{o}})
and we write
\mathfrak{q}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}
for operation associated to this correspondence.

Lemma 3.47
.

The maps

T^{d}\,\colon\,\bigotimes\limits_{j=1}^{d}\overline{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}(y^{j},y^{j-1})\otimes\mathscr{F}^{0}(y^{0})\;\to\;\mathscr{F}^{1}(y^{d})

defined by

(3.80)

T^{d}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)\;=\;\sum_{\underline{\Gamma},\textbf{i}}c_{\underline{\Gamma}}\,\mathfrak{q}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)\,Q^{\beta(\underline{\Gamma})}

for
d\geq 1
and with
T^{0}=0
define a homotopy of
A_{\infty}
functors from
\mathscr{F}^{0}
to
\mathscr{F}^{1}
.

Proof.

By Stokes’ Theorem,

(3.81)

(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}|^{\prime}}d_{y^{d}}\mathfrak{q}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)\;-\;\mathfrak{q}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}(d_{y^{0}}\alpha)\;+\;(-1)^{1+n|y^{d}_{o}|+|\underline{\sigma}|^{\prime}}\,\mathfrak{q}_{\partial\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}}(\alpha)\;=\;0,

since
\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}^{d}{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}})\equiv n|y^{d}_{o}|+|\underline{\sigma}|^{\prime}\mod{2}
. Summing over all sequences
\underline{\Gamma}
and all
i
the last term on the left side yields the following operations.

I)

Arguing exactly as in (
I
), we obtain the term
(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}+1}T_{(\sigma_{d},\dots,\partial_{oc}\sigma_{j},\dots,\sigma_{1})}(\alpha)
from the boundary of the simplices
\Delta^{\lVert\sigma_{j}\rVert}
and the boundary strata coming from disc bubbling, which are discussed in Lemma
3.31
.

II)

By the discussion before Equation (
3.4
), the vertical boundary of
\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}
(over
I^{d-1}
) yields the term
(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq j}|^{\prime}+1}T_{\ast_{j}\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)
, noting that
\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}})=\text{vdim}(\mathcal{K}_{\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}})+1
.

III)

Consider now
\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\{s_{j}=1\}}
for
j\notin\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}
. Choose
p\geq 0
so that
i_{p}<j<i_{p+1}
and define
\textbf{i}^{\prime}:=(i_{r}>\dots>i_{p+1}>j>i_{p}>\dots i_{1})
. By the equivalent of Lemma
3.19
, there exists an equivalence

\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i}^{>p},\underline{\Gamma}^{>j},\underline{\sigma}^{>j}}\times_{I\times Y_{j}}\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i}^{\leq p},\underline{\Gamma}^{\leq p},\underline{\sigma}^{\leq j}}\;\simeq\;\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\{s_{j}=1\}}.

The orientation sign comparing the fibre product orientation with the boundary orientation is
(-1)^{n|y^{d}_{o}|+|\underline{\sigma}^{>j}|^{\prime}+1}
. On the other hand, we have by definition that

\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i}^{>p},\underline{\Gamma}^{>j},\underline{\sigma}^{>j}}\times_{I\times Y_{j}}\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i}^{\leq p},\underline{\Gamma}^{\leq p},\underline{\sigma}^{\leq j}}\;=\;\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i}^{\prime},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\{t_{p-1}=t_{p}\}}.

In this case the orientations differ by
(-1)^{n|y^{d}_{o}|+|\underline{\sigma}^{>j}|^{\prime}}
due to the fact that the normal vector of
\{(s,t)\in I^{2}\mid s\leq t\}
is given by
s-t
. Thus, the operations associated to these correspondences cancel.

IV)

If
t_{j}=1
for some
j\in\{1,\dots,r\}
, then
t_{j^{\prime}}=1
for any
j^{\prime}\geq j
, whence

\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\{t_{j}=1\}}\;\simeq\;\mathcal{K}^{1}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{>i_{j}},\underline{\sigma}^{>i_{j}}}\times_{Y_{j}}\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i}^{\leq j},\underline{\Gamma}^{\leq i_{j}},\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i_{j}}}

where
\textbf{i}^{\leq j}=(i_{j-1}>\dots>i_{1})
. Thus, the associated operation is
(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i_{j}}|^{\prime}}\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\underline{\sigma}^{>i_{j}}}\circ T_{\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i_{j}}}(\alpha)
if
j>0
, respectively
\mathscr{F}^{1}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)
if
j=0
.

V)

Similarly,

\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}|_{\{t_{j}=0\}}\;\simeq\;\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i}^{>j},\underline{\Gamma}^{>i_{j}},\underline{\sigma}^{>i_{j}}}\times_{Y_{j}}\mathcal{K}^{0}_{\underline{\Gamma}^{\leq i_{j}},\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i_{j}}}

where
\textbf{i}^{\leq j}=(i_{r}-i_{j}>\dots>i_{j+1}-i_{j})
. Therefore, the associated operation appearing in Equation (
3.81
) is
(-1)^{|\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i_{j}}|^{\prime}+1}T_{\underline{\sigma}^{>i_{j}}}\circ\mathscr{F}^{0}_{\underline{\sigma}^{\leq i_{j}}}(\alpha)
if
j<r
, respectively
-\mathscr{F}^{0}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)
if
j=r
.

Adding these terms one exactly obtains the relation a homotopy is required to satisfy.
∎

Lemma 3.48
.

T
is a homotopy of symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
functors in the sense of Definition
2.63
.

Proof.

The natural transformation
P
of degree
-1
with

\mu^{1}(P)\;=\;(T\circ\otimes_{\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}})\circ R^{0}\,-\,R^{1}\circ(\otimes_{\text{Ch}_{\Lambda}}\circ(T\otimes T))

is defined by
P^{d}=0
for
d\leq 1
.
The maps
P^{d}
for
d\geq 2
are constructed by combining the correspondences above with the correspondences in Definition
3.38
. To this end, define for
\textbf{i}=(d>i_{r}\dots>i_{r}\geq 1)
the subspace
I(\textbf{i})\coloneqq I^{d-1-r}
. Given composable sequences
\underline{\sigma}
and
\underline{\tau}
of length
d
in
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}\mathscr{C}}_{n}
and sequences of graphs
\underline{\Gamma}
and
\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
lying over them, we define for
1\leq\kappa\leq d-1-r
the correspondence

\mathcal{P}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma},\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}}(\kappa):=(\mathcal{K}^{{}^{,}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma},\underline{\sigma}}\times\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\underline{\Gamma}^{\prime},\underline{\tau}})\times_{I(\textbf{i})^{2}}(D_{d-1-r}\times\dots\times D_{\kappa}\times\Delta_{\kappa}\times C_{\kappa-1}\times\dots\times C_{1}).

The same computations as in the proof of Lemma
3.47
now show the claim.
∎

Suppose now we have made two different choices
J_{0}
and
J_{1}
of almost complex structures and two different choices of auxiliary data that define curved open-closed DMFTs
\mathscr{F}^{0}
and
\mathscr{F}^{1}
respectively.
Choose a path
\{J_{\kappa}\}_{\kappa}
of almost complex structures starting at
J_{0}
and ending at
J_{1}
. By the same argument as in
[
36
]
, we can find an auxiliary datum
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\mathsf{a}}
for each
\mathsf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
that yields auxiliary data for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}^{J_{0}}(X,L)
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}^{J_{1}}(X,L)
simultaneously and which allows one to construct a global Kuranishi chart
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathsf{a}}
for the ‘cobordism’
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{a}}^{\{J_{\kappa}\}}(X,L)
, admitting a submersion to
[0,1]
. Applying the constructions of this proof to the charts
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathsf{a}}
we obtain a homotopy from
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{0}
to
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{1}
, the curved open-closed DMFTs obtained from the restrictions of the global Kuranishi charts
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}_{\mathsf{a}}}
(and the Kuranishi charts built from them) to
0
and
1
respectively. Finally, we can use the theorem for the different choices of auxiliary data for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J_{0}}_{\mathsf{a}}(X,L)
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{J_{1}}(\mathsf{a})(X,L)
to obtain homotopies from
\mathscr{F}^{0}
to
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{0}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}^{1}
to
\mathscr{F}^{1}
respectively. This completes the proof of (
1
).

We will now incorporate bounding cochains into the homotopies. To this end, let
\mathscr{F}^{0}
,
\mathscr{F}^{1}
be two curved open-closed DMFTs obtained from two different choices of auxiliary data and
\omega
-tame almost complex structures. Let
\mathscr{A}_{i}
be the curved
A_{\infty}
algebra associated to
\mathscr{F}^{i}
by Lemma
2.40
.
The last paragraph of the previous proof yields global Kuranishi charts
\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
interpolating between the global Kuranishi charts used to define
\mathscr{F}^{0}
and
\mathscr{F}^{1}
. In particular, taking
\Gamma
to be a stable map graph lying over
\sigma=D_{k}
we obtain correspondences

L^{k}\times[0,1]\leftarrow\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma,D_{k}}\rightarrow L\times[0,1].

Composing this with the diagonal map
L^{k}\times[0,1]\rightarrow L^{k}\times[0,1]^{k}
yields the correspondence

(L\times[0,1])^{k}\,\xleftarrow{\widetilde{e}v^{-}}\,\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma,D_{k}}\,\xrightarrow{\widetilde{e}v^{+}}\,L\times[0,1].

Similar to the proof of Lemma
2.40
, these correspondences equip
\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}\coloneqq\Omega^{*}(L\times[0,1];\Lambda)
with the structure of a curved
A_{\infty}
algebra. Given a gauge equivalence
\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}
between
\mathfrak{b}^{0}
and
\mathfrak{b}^{1}
, we deform the homotopy
T
constructed in Lemma
3.47
as follows. Each
\mathcal{K}^{01}_{\textbf{i},\pi^{[k]}\underline{\Gamma},\pi^{[k]}\underline{\sigma}}
has
k
additional boundary marked points, with evaluation maps to
L\times[0,1]
. Therefore, taking
(T^{\widetilde{b}})^{0}=0
and

T^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha)\;=\;\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}T_{\pi^{[k]}\underline{\sigma}}(\alpha\times\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}^{\times k}).

defines a well-defined pre-natural transformation and similar for the deformation of
P
of Lemma
3.48
. The same argument as in the proof of Lemma
3.47
show that
T^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}}
is a homotopy of
A_{\infty}
functors. Similarly, deforming the natural transformation
P
of Lemma
3.48
shows this is a homotopy of symmetric monoidal
A_{\infty}
functors. Together with Lemma
2.64
, this completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.49
.

We can use the correspondences
\mathcal{K}^{01,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.6}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{\Gamma}
to define a curved open-closed DMFT
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
with
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}(y)=\Omega^{*}(X^{y_{c}}\times L^{y_{o}}\times[0,1];\Lambda)
. Analogously, we call this a
pseudo-isotopy
of curved open-closed DMFTs. The curved open-closed DMFT of Theorem
3.1
is thus also independent of choices up to pseudo-isotopy.

Appendix A
Integration along singular submanifolds

In this section we prove a major technical ingredient for the definition of the open-closed DMFT associated to a Lagrangian submanifold. The main result is Theorem
A.22
, from which we obtain that our generalised
\mathfrak{q}
-operations are well-defined.

A.1.
Preliminaries on analytic spaces

A key ingredient of the proof is the resolution of singularities of an analytic space as defined/constructed in
[
11
]
. We recall here the relevant definitions and extend them to the setting of our main application in §
3
. More details can be found in
[
10
]
.

Definition A.1
.

A
real analytic manifold with corners

V
is a Hausdorff space with an atlas
\{(\phi_{i},U_{i})\}_{i\in I}
of homeomorphisms
\phi_{i}\colon U_{i}\to V_{i}\subset(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n}
, where
V_{i}
is an open subspace of
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n}
so that the transition functions extend to real analytic functions in a neighbourhood of
\phi_{i}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})
for any
i,j\in I
.

Definition A.2
.

A
analytic map
between real analytic varieties with corners is a continuous map that in local coordinates extends to a real analytic map between affine real analytic varieties.

This is a adaption of the notion of a smooth map between manifolds with corners in
[
41
]
.

Example A.3
.

The standard simplex
\Delta^{n}
is given by the intersection of
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n+1}
with the zero locus of
f(x)=1-\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}
and thus admits the structure of a real analytic manifold with corners.

Definition A.4
.

Let
V
be a real analytic manifold with corners. A subspace
Z\subset V
is
analytic
if for each
z\in Z
there exists an open neighbourhood
U
of
z
in
V
and an analytic function
f\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
so that
Z\cap U=f^{-1}(\{0\})
.

Let
Z\subset V
be an analytic subspace of a real analytic manifold
V
with corners. We call
x\in Z

smooth (of dimension
k
)
if there exists a neighbourhood
U\subset Z
of
x
so that
Z\cap U
is a real analytic submanifold (of dimension
k
) of
V
. We call
x

singular
otherwise. We write
Z^{sm}
and
Z^{sing}
for the set of smooth, respectively singular points of
Z
. The
dimension
of an analytic subspace is the supremum over the dimensions of its smooth points. As
Z^{sm}
is analytic, so is
Z^{sing}
.

Remark A.5
.

If
\varphi\colon X\to Y
is an analytic map between real analytic manifolds with corners and
Z\subset Y
is a closed analytic subspace, then the preimage
\varphi^{-1}(Z)
is an analytic subspace of
X
. In particular, the fibre product
X\times_{Z}Y
of real analytic maps is an analytic subspace.

However, the fact that we allow corners means that fibre product can be ill-behaved as the following example shows.

Example A.6
.

Consider the analytic function
f\colon[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1]
given by
f(t)=t_{1}t_{2}-1
. Then the fibre product
\{1\}\times_{[0,1]}[0,1]^{2}
is given by
\{(1,1)\}
. .

This is due to the fact that a map between a manifold with corners need not be a submersion at a boundary point
x
even if its differential at
x
is surjective (between the tangent spaces of the respective strata). The issue is that the maps we (have to) consider are only
weakly smooth
in the language of
[
41
]
, cf. Definition 3.2 and Example 3.5(f) op. cit..

Definition A.7
.

We say that a weakly smooth map
f\colon M\to N
between manifolds with corners has a
corner singularity
over a submanifold
Q\subset N
if
df(x)
is surjective for
x\in f^{-1}(Q)
but
\dim(f^{-1}(Q))<\dim(M)-{\text{codim}}(Q)
, where
\dim
on the left hand side denotes the Lebesgue dimension.

The blow-down construction below will not identify corner singularities as the fibre product locally extends to a transverse fibre product of analytic manifolds without corners. By restricting the class of maps we consider, we ensure that corner singularities arise in codimension
\geq 2
.

Definition A.8
.

An analytic map
\varphi\colon V\to W
between analytic manifolds with corners is
admissible
if near each
x\in V
there exists a neighbourhood
U
so that
\varphi|_{U}
is the composition of a smooth map in the sense of
[
41
, Definition 3.2]
with a map of the form
f_{k}\times\text{id}
, where
f_{k}\colon[0,\infty)^{k}\to[0,\infty]
is the multiplication
f_{k}(x)=x_{1}\cdots x_{k}
. We call
\varphi

strongly smooth
if it is smooth in the sense of
[
41
, Definition 3.2]
.

Lemma A.9
.

Suppose a real analytic Lie group
G
acts analytically on a real analytic manifold
Y
with corners and
\varphi\colon X\to Y
is an analytic map. Then

V:=\{(x,g,y)\in X\times G\times Y\mid g\cdot\varphi(x)=y\}

is an analytic subspace of
X\times G\times Y
. The map

\sigma_{V}\colon G\times V\to V:(h,(x,g,y))\mapsto(x,hg,h\cdot y)

is analytic.

Proof.

By assumption, the map
\sigma\colon G\times Y\to Y:(g,y)\mapsto g^{-1}\cdot y
is real analytic, so the first claim follows from Remark
A.5
. The second claim follows from the fact that
\sigma_{V}
is the restriction of the analytic action of
G
on
X\times G\times Y
.
∎

Definition A.10
.

A
real analytic Lie groupoid with corners

X=[X_{1}\rightrightarrows X_{0}]
is a Lie groupoid so that all
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are real analytic varieties with corners and all structure maps are analytic. A morphism between two such Lie groupoids
X\to Y
is
analytic
if the maps
X_{0}\to Y_{0}
and
X_{1}\to Y_{1}
are analytic.

We define the category of
real analytic orbifolds with corners
to have objects given by real analytic étale proper Lie groupoids with corners and morphisms
f=(R,\widetilde{f})
given by zigzags
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}

where
R
is a refinement, and both
R
and
f
are analytic.

Example A.11
.

Suppose
G
is a real analytic group acting analytically and almost freely on a real analytic manifold
V
with corners. Then
[V/G]
is a real analytic orbifold with corners.

The definition of an analytic subgroupoid of a real analytic orbifold is analogous to the one of an analytic subspace.

Corollary A.12
.

Suppose
X=[V/G]
,
Y=[W/H]
and
Z=[U/K]
are analytic orbifolds with corners and
X\to Z
and
Y\to Z
are admissible analytic maps. Then the orbifold fibre product
X\times_{Z}Y
is an analytic subgroupoid of
X\times K\times Y
.

Lemma A.13
.

The moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,*,\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})
of
[
35
, §2.1]
is a real analytic orbifold with corners on which
\text{PGL}_{\mathbb{R}}(N+1)
acts analytically.

Proof.

In fact, it is real algebraic but we will only show real analyticity in order to save on definitions. Let
\mathfrak{c}
be the anti-complex linear involution on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2g+h-1,2k+|\ell|}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},2m)
induced by complex conjugation on
\mathbb{C}P^{N}
. Then
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}^{\,*,\,m}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},\mathbb{R}P^{N})
is a codimension
0
submanifold of the fixed point locus
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2g+h-1,2k+|\ell|}(\mathbb{C}P^{N},2m)^{\mathfrak{c}}
. The first claim now follows from the discussion of the deformation theory of curves with boundary in
[
46
, §3.3]
. The second assertion is immediate.
∎

Corollary A.14
.

The moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,h;k,\ell}
is a real analytic orbifold with corners.

Definition A.15
.

A
relative real analytic manifold with corners

X/B
consists of a smooth manifold
X
and a submersion
q\colon X\to B
to a real analytic manifold with corners. A
relative analytic subspace
of
X/B
is the preimage
q^{-1}(Z)
of an analytic subspace
Z\subset B
.

Definition A.16
.

A map
\varphi\colon X/B\to X^{\prime}/B^{\prime}
between real analytic varieties with corners is
analytic
if
\varphi
is smooth and the induced map
B\to B^{\prime}
is analytic.

The following definition is not the most general but sufficient for our purposes.

Definition A.17
.

A
relative real analytic orbifold with corners
consists of a relative real analytic manifold with corners
X/B
equipped with a smooth proper group action by a real analytic group
G
so that the group action on
X
is almost free and the action on
B
is analytic. We write
\mathcal{X}
for the associated orbifold.

This definition is inspired by the theory of rel–
C^{\infty}
manifolds although it does not require it. The point is that for our main use of resolution of singularities, the singular manifold we study is a relatively smooth manifold over a singular fibre product of analytic maps. We thus only have to apply the resolution of singularities to said fibre product and not to the actual space. This will become clear in Theorem
A.22
.

A.2.
Integration over relative real analytic orbifolds

We will now prove the main theorem of this section, Theorem
A.22
, which is essential for the construction in §
3
of the DMFT associated to a Lagrangian submanifold.

Definition A.18
.

Let
V
be a real analytic manifold with corners and
Z\subset V
be a closed analytic subspace. An
embedded resolution of singularities
of
Z
is a proper analytic map
\sigma_{Z}\colon\widetilde{V}\to V
so that

•

\sigma_{Z}
is given by a locally finite sequence of blow-ups of
V
with final exceptional hypersurface
3
3

3

As we are in the real analytic setting, we prefer not to use the word divisor, reserving it for submanifolds of real codimension
2
.

E\subset\widetilde{V}
,

•

the strict transform
\widetilde{Z}:=\overline{\sigma_{Z}^{-1}(Z\setminus Z^{sing})}
is an analytic submanifold of
V
,

•

the restriction
\sigma_{Z}|_{\widetilde{Z}\setminus E}\colon\widetilde{Z}\setminus E\to Z\setminus Z^{sing}
is an isomorphism,

•

\widetilde{Z}
and
E
have normal crossing intersections.

This is usually only defined in the case where
\partial V=\emptyset
. However, the local construction of the blow-up in the real analytic category generalises to the setting of analytic manifolds with corners. In particular, we may arrange for the blow-down map to preserve corner strata.

By
[
12
, Theorem 1.1]
, there exists a resolution of singularities for any compact
4
4

4

The same is true for any open subspace of
Z
with compact closure if
Z
itself is not compact.
analytic subspace
Z
of a real analytic manifold
V
. The resolution
\sigma_{Z}
is
universal
in the sense that any isomorphism
U\to U^{\prime}
between open subspaces of analytic sets
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
lifts to an isomorphism
\sigma_{Z}^{-1}(U)\to\sigma_{Z^{\prime}}^{-1}(U^{\prime})
.

Proposition A.19
.

Let
V
be a real analytic manifold with corners and
Z\subset V
be an analytic subspace. If
Z
is a relatively compact open subset of an analytic space
Z^{\prime}\subset V
, it admits a universal (embedded) resolution of singularities.

Proof.

The proof of
[
12
, Theorem 1.1]
relies on the resolution of singularities of a marked ideal,
[
12
, Theorem 1.3]
. A
marked ideal
is a tuple
\underline{\mathcal{I}}=(V,N,E_{0},\mathcal{I},d)
, where
N\subset V
is an analytic submanifold,
E_{0}
is a hypersurface in
M
with normal crossing singularities,
\mathcal{I}
is a coherent sheaf of ideals on
N
and
d
is a non-negative integer. This definition can be made verbatim in the setting with corners. Given
Z\subset V
we let
\mathcal{I}_{Z}\subset\mathcal{O}_{V}
be its ideal sheaf in
V
and set
\underline{\mathcal{I}}_{Z}:=(V,V,\emptyset,\mathcal{I}_{Z},1)
. Since the proof of
[
12
, Theorem 1.3]
is phrased purely in terms of a marked ideal with the only geometric inputs, such as
[
12
, Lemma 3.1]
being local and thus generalising directly, we obtain the claim.
∎

Corollary A.20
.

Suppose
Z\subset V
satisfies the condition of Proposition
A.19
and a compact real analytic Lie group
G
acts almost freely and analytically on
V
, preserving
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
. Then we can choose the resolution of singularities to be
G
-equivariant, i.e.,
\widetilde{V}
admits an analytic
G
-action preserving
\widetilde{Z}
and
E
and
\sigma_{Z}
is
G
-equivariant.

Proof.

We will use the universality of the resolution in Proposition
A.19
. If
Z
is
G
-invariant, then the isomorphism
\phi_{g}\colon V\to V
associated to
g\in G
induces an isomorphism
Z\to Z
and thus lifts uniquely to an isomorphism
\widetilde{\phi}_{g}\colon\widetilde{Z}\to\widetilde{Z}
. In particular, this yields an analytic
G
-action on
\widetilde{Z}
. To see that this action extends to an analytic action on
\widetilde{V}
, one can use the functoriality statement of
[
12
, Theorem 1.3]
on the action map
G\times V\to V
.∎

Proposition A.21
.

Suppose
\sigma_{Z}\colon\widetilde{V}\to V
is an embedded resolution of singularities of an analytic subspace
Z
.

(1)

The preimage
\sigma_{Z}^{-1}(S)
of a corner stratum
S
of
V
is a corner stratum of
\widetilde{V}
of the same dimension. In particular,

(A.1)

\partial\widetilde{Z}=\sigma_{Z}^{-1}(Z\cap\partial V).

(2)

For any corner stratum
S\subset\partial V
, the restriction

(A.2)

\sigma_{Z}|_{\sigma_{Z}^{-1}(S)}\colon\sigma_{Z}^{-1}(S)\to S

is an embedded resolution of singularities that is isomorphic to the universal resolution of
Z\cap S
in
S
.

The corresponding statements hold in the equivariant setting.

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{Z}
is an analytic submanifold of
\widetilde{V}
, we have that
\partial\widetilde{Z}=\widetilde{Z}\cap\partial V
. The equality in (
A.1
) then follows from
[
6
, Proposition 3.2]
since the centers of the blow-ups used to define
\widetilde{V}
are analytic submanifolds and thus are neatly embedded
5
5

5

That is,
C\cap\partial V_{j}=\partial C
and
C\pitchfork\partial V_{j}
.
in the respective intermediate blow-up.

The second assertion follows from the functoriality of the resolution of singularities in
[
12
]
with respect to closed embeddings
V^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V
. To be precise,
[
12
]
does not prove this but refers to
[
59
, Theorem 1.0.1]
, see also §5.2 op. cit.. As the argument in
[
59
]
only uses the notion of a marked ideal, it carries over to our setting. Note that, as discussed in
[
42
, §71]
, this relies on us using very simple marked ideals.
∎

Theorem A.22
.

Suppose
(X^{\prime}/B,G)
is an oriented real analytic orbifold and
X\subset X^{\prime}
a
G
-invariant relatively compact open subspace. Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to Y
be a
G
-invariant submersion to a smooth oriented manifold
Y
. Given analytic maps
\varphi\colon[B/G]\to\mathcal{V}
and
\psi\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{V}
, where
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
are compact oriented real analytic orbifolds with corners, we can define a pushforward

(A.3)

(f_{\psi})_{*}\colon\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\to\Omega^{*-\text{rdim}}(Y)

which agrees with the composition

(A.4)

\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{p^{*}}\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{W})\xrightarrow{(fp)_{*}}\Omega^{*}(Y)

if
\varphi\pitchfork\psi
, where
\mathcal{X}:=[X/G]
and
p\colon\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{X}
is the canonical projection.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{W}=[W/\Gamma]
for some analytic compact Lie group
\Gamma
and let
\mathcal{B}
be the global quotient associated to the
(G\times\Gamma\times H)
-action on
X\times\Gamma\times W
. By Corollary
A.12
and Corollary
A.20
, we can find a morphism
\rho\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathcal{B}
of relative analytic orbifolds that defines a universal resolution of singularities for
\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{W}
. Set
\mathcal{Z}:=\rho^{-1}(\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{W})
. The map
\widetilde{f}:=f\circ\text{pr}_{\mathcal{X}}\circ\rho|_{\mathcal{Z}}
is still a co-oriented vertical submersion and we can thus define
(f_{\psi})_{*}
by the composition

(A.5)

\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{\text{pr}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{B})\xrightarrow{\rho^{*}}\Omega_{c}^{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}})\to\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{Z})\xrightarrow{\widetilde{f}_{*}}\Omega^{*-k}(Y)

where
k=\text{rdim}(\widetilde{f})=\dim(Y)-\dim(\widetilde{Z})=\dim(Y)-\dim(Z)
. If the fibre product is transverse, we can take
\rho
to be the identity, whence (
A.5
) agrees with (
A.4
).
∎

Lemma A.23
.

Let
f^{\prime}
be the restriction of
f
to
\partial\mathcal{X}
and
\psi^{\prime}
be the restriction of
\psi
to
\partial\mathcal{W}
. Then the pushforward
(f_{\psi})_{*}
satisfies

(A.6)

d(f_{\psi})_{*}(\alpha)=(f_{\psi})_{*}(d\alpha)+(-1)^{|\alpha|+\dim(\mathcal{Z})}\Bigl((f^{\prime}_{\psi})_{*}(\alpha|_{\partial\mathcal{X}})+(-1)^{\dim(\mathcal{X})+\dim(\mathcal{V})}(f_{\psi^{\prime}})_{*}(\alpha)\Bigr)

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{X})
be arbitrary and set
\widetilde{\alpha}:=\rho^{*}\text{pr}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}\alpha
. By Stokes’ Formula,
[
53
, Proposition 2.2]
, we have

d(f_{\psi})_{*}(\alpha)=(f_{\psi})_{*}(d\alpha)+(-1)^{|\alpha|+\dim(\mathcal{Z})}(\widetilde{f}|_{\partial\mathcal{Z}})_{*}(\widetilde{\alpha}).

By abuse of notation we write
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{W}
for the orbifold
\mathcal{B}
defined in the previous proof. By Proposition
A.21
(
1
), we have

\partial\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Z}\cap\rho^{-1}((\partial\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{W})\sqcup(-1)^{\dim(\mathcal{X})}\mathcal{Z}\cap\rho^{-1}(\mathcal{X}\times\partial\mathcal{W})=:\partial_{1}\mathcal{Z}\sqcup\partial_{2}\mathcal{Z}.

Let
f^{\prime}
be the pullback of
f
to
\partial X=X\times_{B}\partial B
. This is still a submersion and by Proposition
A.21
(
2
) and Lemma
3.22
, the composite

(A.7)

\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{\text{pr}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{X}\times\partial\mathcal{W})\xrightarrow{\rho^{*}}\Omega_{c}^{*}(\rho^{-1}(\mathcal{X}\times\partial\mathcal{W}))\to\Omega_{c}^{*}(\partial_{2}\mathcal{Z})\xrightarrow{\widetilde{f}_{*}}\Omega^{*-k}(Y)

agrees with
(f^{\prime}_{\psi})_{*}
. Similarly, if
\psi^{\prime}\colon\partial\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{V}
is the restriction of
\psi
, then

(A.8)

\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{\text{pr}_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\Omega_{c}^{*}(\mathcal{X}\times\partial\mathcal{W})\xrightarrow{\rho^{*}}\Omega_{c}^{*}(\rho^{-1}(\mathcal{X}\times\partial\mathcal{W}))\to\Omega_{c}^{*}(\partial_{2}\mathcal{Z})\xrightarrow{\widetilde{f}_{*}}\Omega^{*-k}(Y)

agrees with
(f_{\psi^{\prime}})_{*}
. Therefore,

(A.9)

(\widetilde{f}|_{\partial\mathcal{Z}})_{*}(\widetilde{\alpha})=(f^{\prime}_{\psi})_{*}(\alpha|_{\partial\mathcal{X}})+(-1)^{\dim(\mathcal{X})}(f_{\psi^{\prime}})_{*}(\alpha).

by
[
40
, Proposition 7.4(30)]
.
∎

Lemma A.24
.

Suppose
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to Y
and
\varphi,\psi
are as in Theorem
A.22
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a cartesian square of oriented with
q
a smooth submersion between two smooth manifolds. Equipping
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
with the pullback orientation, we have

(A.10)

(f^{\prime}_{\psi})_{*}p^{*}=q^{*}(f_{\psi})_{*}.

Proof.

The first assertion is immediate, while the second follows from the functoriality of the resolution of singularities with respect to smooth morphisms,
[
12
, Theorem 1.1(3)]
and
[
53
, Proposition 2.1(4)]
.
∎
